<a id="t733965"></a>
## 1. 📌 Use of Commercially Hosted LLMs

- **লিংক:** https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/discussion/733965
- **লেখক:** **Po-Hao "Howard" Chen** (@javaduke95, Contributor) · _host_ · 2026-08-09 09:52 UTC · ▲20
- **ভোট:** 20 · **কমেন্ট:** 13 · **শেষ কমেন্ট:** 2026-09-23 20:31 UTC (Danny Daniel)

Use of commercially hosted LLMs and other external inference services is permitted, provided that the service and method of use otherwise comply with the Competition Rules, including requirements that external data, models, software, and associated tools be reasonably accessible to all participants and of minimal cost. 

In other words, for purposes of this competition, submitting Competition Data, including report text, to an external LLM or API for inference or other computational processing (for example, extracting labels from reports) will not, by itself, be considered prohibited **PRIVATE SHARING** of Competition Data outside the Team.

Please refer to the [RSNA Knee Abnormality Detection Competition Rules](https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/rules).  This is in accordance to Section 2.6.b, **EXTERNAL DATA AND TOOLS**.

The **PRIVATE SHARING** restriction is intended to prohibit sharing Competition Data, code, or competition-specific work product with other participants, teams, or third parties for collaboration or competitive use outside the registered Team.

Participants remain responsible for ensuring that any external service complies with all applicable Competition Rules and the service's terms of use, including the requirements for reasonable accessibility and minimal cost.

The Competition Host reserves the right to determine whether a particular service, model, or configuration is reasonably accessible, is prohibitively costly, or otherwise creates an unfair competitive advantage.

### কমেন্ট (13)

**[1]** **Nicolai Karcher** (@pejophylo, Contributor) · 2026-08-09 10:23 UTC · ▲4 · LB rank 784

"Participants remain responsible for ensuring that any external service complies with all applicable Competition Rules and the service's terms of use, including the requirements for reasonable accessibility and minimal cost.

The Competition Host reserves the right to determine whether a particular service, model, or configuration is reasonably accessible, is prohibitively costly, or otherwise creates an unfair competitive advantage."



Could you give us your assessment regarding the use of common public MRI datasets for this challenge? Many of them only allow research (and no commercial) use, and getting a price to me could be classified as commercial use (I'm not a lawyer though so I don't know).

For example, [this dataset](https://stanford.redivis.com/datasets/4a2c-4cpkzrn2c?access&requirement=474) specifically states: "Permission is granted to view and use the Dataset without charge for personal, **non-commercial research purposes only**. Any commercial use, sale, or **other monetization is prohibited**."

I understand these restrictions are describing external datasets, so we should probably get in contact with those databases. But I was wondering what your independent assessment would be. Thanks!

**[1.1]** ↳ **PC Jimmmy** (@pcjimmmy, Master) · 2026-08-18 15:07 UTC · LB rank 635

That's a great suggestion - contact the source and ask if they are ok with you using the data in a kaggle competition.  Not sure if they would reply, but also past experience suggest kaggle reluctant to reply to this type of question during the competition.

**[2]** **Ragheb haddara** (@l4r3nn, Contributor) · 2026-08-10 21:45 UTC · ▲1 · LB rank 4224

The rules about commercially hosted LLMs are important here. Since knee MRI interpretation requires domain expertise, it's tempting to use an LLM to help with reasoning. But the competition rules likely restrict this to ensure the solutions are based on the image data, not external reasoning. Check the rules page carefully before incorporating any LLM-based features.

**[3]** **UGUR OZER** (@aeonairisk, Contributor) · 2026-08-14 21:30 UTC · ▲1 · LB rank 2572

To make the dataset question above concrete: the Osteoarthritis Initiative (OAI) is free of charge and open to any researcher after a click-through data use agreement, but the agreement limits use to non-commercial research. The same access pattern covers MRNet, fastMRI+ and SKM-TEA, so a ruling on the category would settle it for essentially every public knee-MRI resource. Given the prize money, could the host confirm whether external datasets under research-only agreements are permitted, and if so whether the winners' open-source obligation is affected? Teams need this before committing pretraining work ahead of the entry deadline. Thank you.

**[3.1]** ↳ **Po-Hao "Howard" Chen** (@javaduke95, Contributor) · _host_ · 2026-08-27 22:14 UTC · ▲6

From the competition’s perspective, use of these datasets would be considered non-commercial; receipt of competition prize money does not, by itself, make their use a commercial sale or commercial exploitation. These datasets would not be excluded by the competition rules solely because their licenses restrict use to non-commercial purposes.

A separate consideration is whether an external dataset is reasonably accessible to all competitors. Datasets that can be accessed through a straightforward registration or click-through data-use agreement would generally satisfy this requirement. By contrast, datasets requiring institution-specific approvals, negotiated written agreements, IRB approval, lengthy credentialing, or other substantial administrative steps may present a meaningful accessibility barrier, particularly given the remaining competition timeline. Such requirements could therefore affect whether a dataset is considered allowable under the competition’s external-data rules, independent of whether its use is characterized as commercial or non-commercial.

Our job (Host) is tough here because RSNA and Kaggle do not own or control these external datasets and cannot interpret or waive their individual data-use agreements, expedite requests and so on, on behalf of the dataset owners. Teams remain responsible for ensuring that their intended use, including participation in a prize-bearing competition and any required release of code or models, is permitted under the applicable dataset license or agreement.

Therefore, to the extent the question concerns the *competition rules*, readily accessible datasets with non-commercial restrictions are not prohibited on that basis alone. Dataset-specific licensing obligations remain the responsibility of each team, and datasets involving substantial approval or access hurdles may need to be considered separately under the competition’s equal-accessibility requirements.

Sorry for the verbose response. I hope it's at least a little helpful...

## 2. 📌 Knee Abnormality Detection AI Challenge Overview

- **লিংক:** https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/discussion/733343
- **লেখক:** **Po-Hao "Howard" Chen** (@javaduke95, Contributor) · _host_ · 2026-08-06 15:54 UTC · ▲83
- **ভোট:** 83 · **কমেন্ট:** 12 · **শেষ কমেন্ট:** 2026-09-11 16:24 UTC (Ahsan Bin Hasan Pasha)

**Courtesy of Dr. Jacob Kazam**

##### Overview of the Clinical Problem

The 2026 RSNA Knee Abnormality Detection AI Challenge asks participants to build machine learning models that detect a defined set of clinically important abnormalities on knee MRI examinations. It is the first RSNA AI Challenge to pair every imaging study with the text of its original radiology report, and to do so across a large, multilingual, international dataset. Models may learn from both the images and the report text, reflecting how knee MRI is actually interpreted and communicated in clinical practice.

The knee is one of the most frequently injured and most frequently imaged joints in the body. Knee disorders are a major and growing cause of pain and disability worldwide, affecting athletes, manual workers, and older adults alike. The knee is the most common site of osteoarthritis, which affects nearly a quarter of adults aged 40 and older, an estimated 654 million people worldwide. Acute injuries are also common: knee injuries account for an estimated 15 to 40 percent of all sports-related injuries.

Several structures are commonly involved. Anterior cruciate ligament (ACL) tears are among the most frequent sports injuries, often occurring during sudden pivots or changes of direction and leading to immediate instability. Medial collateral ligament (MCL) injuries typically result from outward (valgus) stress on the knee and frequently accompany other structural damage. Meniscal tears, which may be traumatic or degenerative, disrupt the joint’s shock absorption and mechanics, producing pain, locking, and functional limitation. Cartilage loss, joint effusion, synovitis, bone contusions, fractures, and Baker (popliteal) cysts are additional findings that cause symptoms and influence management.

MRI is central to evaluating the knee because it depicts all of these structures, including ligaments, menisci, articular cartilage, and bone, without ionizing radiation, providing a comprehensive whole-joint assessment that other modalities cannot match. However, MRI interpretation varies across readers and practice settings, and access to subspecialty-trained musculoskeletal (MSK) radiologists is limited in many parts of the world. Reliable, consistent automated detection of knee abnormalities could help extend expert-level interpretation, reduce variability, and support more timely care.

The goal of this competition is to develop machine learning models that detect twelve clinically important knee abnormalities, learning from a large international dataset of knee MRI examinations paired with their original, multilingual radiology reports.

##### Anatomical Overview

The knee is a synovial hinge joint formed by three bones: the femur (thigh bone) above, the tibia (shin bone) below, and the patella (kneecap) in front. The ends of these bones are covered by articular cartilage, a smooth layer that allows near-frictionless motion and distributes load. The joint is enclosed by a capsule lined with synovium, which produces lubricating fluid; an abnormal excess of this fluid is termed a joint effusion, and inflammation of the synovium is termed synovitis.

![](https://www.googleapis.com/download/storage/v1/b/kaggle-forum-message-attachments/o/inbox%2F413040%2Fe6095eef091115d6f20f3d5c071dd72e%2Fimage1.jpg?generation=1786032010214772&alt=media)

![](https://www.googleapis.com/download/storage/v1/b/kaggle-forum-message-attachments/o/inbox%2F413040%2Fc6429a733527b6dd080a84a472a6a34c%2Fimage2.jpg?generation=1786032025815728&alt=media)

*Knee anatomy: anterior view (top) and lateral view (bottom), showing the femur, tibia, patella, cruciate ligaments, and meniscus. Illustrations by BruceBlaus, CC BY 3.0, via Wikimedia Commons.*

For the purposes of this challenge, the knee is considered in three compartments. The medial compartment lies between the medial femoral condyle and the medial tibial plateau, on the inner side of the knee. The lateral compartment lies between the lateral femoral condyle and the lateral tibial plateau, on the outer side. The patellofemoral compartment lies between the patella and the femoral trochlea, the groove on the front of the femur. Osteoarthritis, or cartilage loss, is assessed separately in each of these three compartments.

Four ligaments stabilize the knee. The anterior cruciate ligament (ACL) and posterior cruciate ligament (PCL) lie within the joint, in the intercondylar notch, and control front-to-back stability and rotation. The medial collateral ligament (MCL) and lateral collateral ligament (LCL) run along the inner and outer sides of the knee and resist side-to-side stress. This challenge focuses on tears of the ACL and the MCL.

Between the femur and tibia sit two menisci, the medial meniscus and the lateral meniscus, C-shaped wedges of fibrocartilage that cushion the joint, absorb shock, and improve the fit between the rounded femur and the relatively flat tibia. Tears of the medial and lateral meniscus are evaluated separately.

Other relevant structures include the bone marrow within the femur, tibia, and patella, where a bruise from impact is called a bone contusion (or bone marrow edema) and a break in the bone is a fracture; and the soft tissues behind the knee, where a fluid-filled outpouching of the joint lining is called a Baker, or popliteal, cyst.

##### Imaging Overview

Several modalities can image the knee. Radiographs (X-rays) show bones and joint-space narrowing but cannot directly visualize ligaments, menisci, or cartilage. Ultrasound and CT have specific roles but offer limited soft-tissue contrast for the internal structures of the joint. Magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) is the reference standard for evaluating internal derangement of the knee because it provides excellent soft-tissue contrast, depicts ligaments, menisci, cartilage, and bone marrow simultaneously, and does not use ionizing radiation.

A knee MRI examination is not a single image but a set of series, each acquired with a particular pulse sequence and in a particular imaging plane. The three standard planes are axial (cross-sectional, viewed as if looking up through the leg), coronal (a front-facing, side-to-side view), and sagittal (a side-profile, front-to-back view). Different structures are best seen in different planes; for example, the cruciate ligaments and menisci are well evaluated on sagittal and coronal images, and the patellofemoral cartilage on axial images.

Sequences differ in how they weight tissue signal. Fluid-sensitive sequences, such as proton-density or T2-weighted images, often with fat suppression, make edema, effusion, and tears appear bright and are central to detecting most abnormalities; a meniscal tear, for instance, appears as abnormally increased signal that reaches the surface of the meniscus on more than one image. Other sequences emphasize anatomy or cartilage detail. Interpreting a study therefore requires integrating information across multiple series, planes, and slices for the same knee, an inherently three-dimensional task.

![](https://www.googleapis.com/download/storage/v1/b/kaggle-forum-message-attachments/o/inbox%2F413040%2F055d8dc92fe53e98325b81b9eae26dee%2Fimage3.jpg?generation=1786032046408361&alt=media)

*Sagittal proton-density, fat-suppressed (fluid-sensitive) MRI of the knee, the type of sequence on which most abnormalities are detected. Image by Ptrump16, CC BY-SA 4.0, via Wikimedia Commons.*

For the purpose of the 2026 Knee Abnormality Challenge, a “fluid sensitive” sequence refers to one in which edema, hemorrhage, and other types of fluid appear bright and fat is suppressed in some way.

Because findings can be subtle and the criteria for what counts as clinically significant are nuanced, interpretation varies between readers. This variability, combined with limited access to subspecialty MSK radiologists, motivates the development of consistent automated tools.

##### Label Description

Models are evaluated on twelve binary labels, each indicating the presence or absence of a specific finding in the imaged knee. The labels, and the criteria used by the annotating radiologists, are summarized below. In each case, ambiguous or borderline findings (“on the fence”) were graded as negative to favor specificity.

ACL tear: A high-grade partial or full-thickness tear of the anterior cruciate ligament, meaning complete discontinuity of the ligament, or more than 50 percent of fibers disrupted, with or without secondary signs such as characteristic pivot-shift bone contusions. Mild signal change, degeneration, or thickening without discontinuity is graded negative.

MCL tear: A high-grade partial or complete acute tear of the medial collateral ligament, with disrupted fibers and edema within and adjacent to the ligament. Low-grade sprains and chronic or remote stress changes are graded negative.

Medial meniscus tear: Abnormal signal that definitely contacts the meniscal surface on at least two images, or a morphologic abnormality such as a truncated, diminutive, or displaced fragment, involving the medial meniscus. Intrasubstance degeneration that does not reach the surface is negative.

Lateral meniscus tear: The same criteria applied to the lateral meniscus.

Medial compartment osteoarthritis: A moderate or large area (roughly 1 cm or greater) of high-grade cartilage loss, defined as greater than 50 percent of cartilage thickness, in the medial compartment, with or without underlying subchondral marrow changes.

Lateral compartment osteoarthritis: The same criteria applied to the lateral compartment.

Patellofemoral compartment osteoarthritis: The same criteria applied to the patellofemoral compartment.

Joint effusion: A moderate or large amount of fluid distending the joint.

Synovitis: Inflammation and thickening of the synovial lining of the joint.

Baker (popliteal) cyst: A moderate or large fluid collection in the characteristic location behind the knee.

Contusion: A bone contusion, seen as bone marrow edema-like signal from impact, without a discrete fracture line.

Acute fracture: An acute cortical break or fracture line.

Each study in the annotated reference set was independently labeled by two subspecialty-trained MSK radiologists, with disagreements adjudicated by a third radiologist to produce a single consensus ground truth. Labels are assigned at the level of the whole examination, for a single knee.

##### Examples

The following annotated examples, drawn from the challenge’s image annotation guidelines, illustrate representative positive and negative cases for several of the labels. In each figure, yellow arrows and arrowheads mark the findings described in the caption.

## 3. 📌 Welcome to Knee Abnormality Detection Challenge!

- **লিংক:** https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/discussion/733375
- **লেখক:** **Po-Hao "Howard" Chen** (@javaduke95, Contributor) · _host_ · 2026-08-06 17:17 UTC · ▲17
- **ভোট:** 17 · **কমেন্ট:** 1 · **শেষ কমেন্ট:** 2026-08-21 20:27 UTC (Maksym Malin)

The knee is the largest joint in the human body, and MRI is central to diagnosing major sports injuries, trauma, and degenerative disease, but interpretation is demanding and access to subspecialty musculoskeletal expertise is uneven. 

This challenge invites the RSNA and Kaggle community to build models that detect 12 clinically important knee abnormalities, spanning ligament and meniscal tears, osteoarthritis, effusion, synovitis, fracture, and more.

This is also RSNA’s first AI Challenge to pair MRI examinations with original multilingual radiology reports. We hope that combination encourages creative, rigorous work that reflects the **real **clinical task: connecting images, language, and meaningful patient care.

And don't forget the efficiency prizes!   "Real world" here also means striking a balance between label-level accuracy and efficient use of resources.

A challenge dataset can make a large collaborative effort look deceptively simple. We are deeply grateful to the contributing institutions, radiologists, data and privacy teams, RSNA colleagues, and Kaggle team who made this possible.  

Above all, we owe it to you... every participant who will bring curiosity, technical skill, and healthy skepticism to the work. Whether this is your first Kaggle competition or your hundredth, we hope you will share reproducible ideas, learn from one another, and help move imaging AI forward thoughtfully. 

Good luck, and welcome.

Learn more about the challenge and [RSNA’s AI Challenge program](https://www.rsna.org/artificial-intelligence/ai-image-challenge).

**Remember**: Kaggle is for everyone. Whether you're teaming up or sharing tips in the competition forum, we expect everyone to follow our Kaggle community guidelines.

###### Competition's Official Discord
In addition to this competition forum, you can continue the discussion in our official Kaggle Discord Server here:
#### [discord.gg/kaggle](http://discord.gg/kaggle)
 
The Discord is a great place to ask getting started questions, chat about the nuances of this competition, and connect with potential team mates. Learn more about Discord at our [announcement here](https://www.kaggle.com/discussions/general/429933). Here are a few things to keep in mind though:

**1. Discord Competition Channels are 'Public' - Don't Share Private Information**

Discord channels for specific competitions are considered 'public' spaces where you are allowed to talk about competition details. Please remember that private sharing of competition code or data outside of your team is, as always, not permitted. Code sharing must always be done publicly through the Kaggle forums/notebooks.

**2. Discord Competition Channels are Not Monitored by Staff - Keep Important Information on the Kaggle Forums**

Kaggle Staff and Hosts running competitions will not monitor Discord or be available to answer questions in Discord. This is intended to be a more casual space to discuss competitions and help each other. Please keep important questions, insights, writeups, and other valuable conversation on the Kaggle forums. 

Happy modeling!

## 5. From AlexNet to DINOv3: A Practical Backbone History for the RSNA Knee MRI 2026 Challenge

- **লিংক:** https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/discussion/740164
- **লেখক:** **Seung Sup Lee** (@seungsuplee, Contributor) · _topic-এর লেখক_ · 2026-09-08 14:35 UTC · ▲1 · LB rank 2633
- **ভোট:** 1 · **কমেন্ট:** 1 · **শেষ কমেন্ট:** 2026-09-26 14:34 UTC (Seung Sup Lee)

- When you open the attached PDF, you can access the embedded links. -

Author: Sean Lee, Graph‑Axis‑AI Grounded.
Prepared: 2026‑09‑08

Introduction

I have been reviewing the model families that are currently relevant to the RSNA Knee Abnormality Detection
2026 competition, not only as a list of backbones, but as an architectural history: who developed each family,
what problem it was designed to solve, what changed from the previous generation, and whether that
change is actually useful for knee MRI.
The competition is a particularly interesting test bed for this question. The official task is to predict 12 knee
abnormalities at the study level from MRI studies consisting of multiple DICOM series. The dataset is interna‑
tional, multi‑site, multi‑scanner and multi‑protocol, and the training data also includes multilingual radiology
reports. RSNA describes the challenge as its first knee MRI AI challenge and its first challenge to combine medical
images with multilingual report text.
• Kaggle competition: Source
• Kaggle dataset description: Source
• RSNA challenge page: Source
• RSNA 2026 announcement: Source
My current conclusion is that the question “Which backbone is best?” is too narrow.
For this competition, the final performance is more likely to come from the interaction of:
DICOM physical normalization
+ series/plane selection
+ slice sampling
+ study‑level aggregation
+ report‑derived supervision
+ target‑specific pooling/heads
+ an efficient backbone
rather than from simply replacing one backbone with a larger one.
My current primary candidate remains DINOv2‑Small, with ResNet34 as a control model, while newer or
alternative families such as DINOv3, ConvNeXt, EfficientNetV2, ResNeXt, Swin Transformer, MAE and
RadImageNet‑pretrained CNNs are worth controlled ablation rather than automatic promotion to the main
model.

2. CNN Lineage: From AlexNet to ConvNeXt
The modern CNN era was accelerated by AlexNet, which demonstrated that a deep convolutional network
trained with GPUs could dramatically improve large‑scale image recognition.
VGG then showed that a very regular architecture built from repeated 3×3 convolutions could scale effectively
with depth.
• VGG: Source
Google’s Inception / GoogLeNet took another direction: instead of using a single filter scale, it processed fea‑
tures through multiple branches and scales in parallel.
• GoogLeNet / Inception: Source
The major next step was ResNet. Its residual formulation,
2
y = F(x) + x
made much deeper networks substantially easier to optimize.
• ResNet: Source
For the current competition, ResNet34 is especially useful as a control model. It is simple, stable, memory‑
efficient and easy to adapt to 2.5D slice stacks. It also gives us a clean way to determine whether an improvement
came from the backbone itself or from preprocessing, labels, sampling or aggregation.
DenseNet introduced dense connectivity, where each layer receives features from all preceding layers. This
improves feature reuse and gradient flow, but the concatenation pattern can increase activation‑memory traffic.
• DenseNet: Source
ResNeXt extended the residual idea by introducing cardinality: multiple parallel transformations with the same
topology, efficiently implemented with grouped convolutions.
• ResNeXt: Source
EfficientNet then focused on coordinated scaling of network depth, width and input resolution instead of scal‑
ing them independently.
• EfficientNet: Source
The later EfficientNetV2 incorporated training speed directly into the design objective and introduced Fused‑
MBConv blocks.
• EfficientNetV2: Source
RegNet is also interesting for Kaggle because it came from studying the design space of networks rather than
searching for one isolated architecture. FAIR reported that some RegNet variants could outperform EfficientNet
under comparable FLOPs while running substantially faster on GPUs.
• RegNet: Source
Finally, ConvNeXt asked a very useful question: if we modernize ResNet using lessons learned during the Trans‑
former era, how far can a pure CNN still go?
• ConvNeXt: Source
For this challenge, I consider ConvNeXt‑Tiny one of the most useful additional CNN controls because it helps
separate the question of Transformer superiority from the effect of modern training and architectural design.
3. Transformer and Self‑Supervised Lineage: ViT ‑> MAE / DINO ‑> DINOv2
‑> DINOv3
The Vision Transformer (ViT) replaced convolutional feature extraction with a sequence of image patches pro‑
cessed by a Transformer.
• ViT: Source
DeiT then showed that ViTs could be trained much more data‑efficiently than the original large‑scale ViT recipe
suggested.
• DeiT: Source
BEiT adapted BERT‑style masked modeling to images.
• BEiT: Source
MAE simplified this idea further: mask a very large fraction of image patches, encode only the visible subset and
reconstruct the missing content.
• MAE: Source
3
This is particularly relevant to medical imaging because unlabeled DICOM data is far easier to obtain than expert
labels. A future medical pipeline could therefore use large‑scale self‑supervised MRI pretraining before task‑
specific fine‑tuning.
DINOv2, introduced by Meta AI, is a self‑supervised visual foundation model trained to produce broadly trans‑
ferable visual representations.
• DINOv2 paper: Source
For the current RSNA Knee competition, DINOv2‑Small remains attractive because it offers strong representa‑
tion quality without the computational cost of much larger variants.
The direct successor, DINOv3, was released by Meta in 2025. Meta scaled self‑supervised learning much further
and introduced changes intended to preserve strong dense visual features during large‑scale training.
• DINOv3 overview: Source
• DINOv3 research page: Source
However, newer does not automatically mean better for this competition.
A fair DINOv2‑S vs DINOv3‑S comparison should keep everything else fixed:
• identical DICOM preprocessing,
• identical physical crop,
• identical slice sampling,
• identical resolution,
• identical cross‑series aggregator,
• identical target heads,
• identical folds and training budget.
Only then can we tell whether the representation itself is better for this task.
4. Swin Transformer and the Medical 3D Branch
Swin Transformer, developed by Microsoft Research, introduced hierarchical representations and shifted‑
window attention.
• Swin Transformer: Source
This matters because medical imaging is not naturally a collection of unrelated 2D photographs. MRI contains
local structure, global anatomical context, multiple planes and ordered slices.
The medical‑imaging community extended Transformer ideas into 3D architectures such as UNETR and Swin
UNETR.
• UNETR: Source
• Swin UNETR: Source
These models are mainly segmentation architectures rather than direct replacements for a study‑level 12‑label
classifier. Still, their history is relevant because they demonstrate how hierarchical Transformer representations
can be adapted to volumetric medical data.
5. U‑Net and nnU‑Net: Why Medical Imaging Has Its Own Architectural
History
The history of medical imaging cannot be described only through ImageNet backbones.
U‑Net became one of the foundational architectures of biomedical image segmentation because it combined a
contracting encoder with an expanding decoder and skip connections.
4
• U‑Net: Source
nnU‑Net took a different step. Its major contribution was not a new convolution block, but a self‑configuring
pipeline that automatically adapts preprocessing, architecture, training and post‑processing to the dataset.
• nnU‑Net: Source
This is conceptually important for Kaggle as well: sometimes the pipeline and configuration strategy matter
more than inventing a new backbone.
6. RadImageNet: Domain‑Specific Pretraining Deserves a Serious Test
One of themost relevant alternatives for this challenge is not a new architecture at all, but a differentpretraining
domain.
RadImageNet contains about 1.35 million annotated radiologic images across CT, MRI and ultrasound and
provides pretrained versions of ResNet50, DenseNet121, InceptionV3 and InceptionResNetV2.
• RadImageNet paper: Source
• Official repository: Source
The original RadImageNet study reported improvements over ImageNet pretraining on several small medical
datasets, including ACL injury MRI and meniscus tear MRI.
That makes RadImageNet unusually relevant to this particular competition.
A useful experiment would be:
ImageNet‑ResNet50 vs RadImageNet‑ResNet50
under exactly the same knee‑MRI preprocessing, 2.5D input and study‑level aggregation.
This would test pretraining‑domain transfer rather than architecture alone.
7. MONAI: Not My Choice for This Competition, but Potentially a Future
Standard Layer for Medical Imaging AI
I also tested MONAI during my RSNA Knee MRI work.
For this specific competition, my current assessment is that MONAI is not the best fit for the main Kaggle
pipeline. The challenge is dominated by a very specific study‑level classification problem under a strict
notebook runtime budget, with custom DICOM preprocessing, slice selection, multi‑plane aggregation and
lightweight backbone experimentation. In that setting, a more direct PyTorch pipeline has been easier for me
to control, profile and optimize.
This is not a criticism of MONAI itself. MONAI is a PyTorch‑based, open‑source framework designed specifically
for healthcare imaging, with medical‑image transforms, caching, domain‑specific networks and losses, multi‑
GPU support, model bundles, labeling tools and deployment components.
• Project MONAI: Source
• MONAI GitHub: Source
• MONAI Tutorials: Source
• MONAI Documentation: Source
Project MONAI was initiated by NVIDIA and King’s College London and has since grown into a broader medical‑
imaging AI ecosystem. Its architecture is much more relevant to long‑term medical AI development than a sin‑
gle Kaggle competition because it is designed around reusable healthcare‑imaging workflows rather than one
competition‑specific classifier.
My current view is therefore:
5
MONAI is not the framework I would prioritize for maximizing this particular RSNA Knee Kaggle
score today, but I believe MONAI ‑ or a MONAI‑like PyTorch‑native medical‑imaging framework
‑ has a strong chance of becoming an important standard layer for future MRI and DICOM AI
training workflows.
The reasons are structural:
• medical‑image‑aware preprocessing instead of generic computer‑vision transforms,
• native handling of 2D, 2.5D and 3D healthcare‑imaging workflows,
• reusable DICOM/NIfTI‑oriented data pipelines,
• medical‑specific losses, metrics and transforms,
• caching and large‑volume data handling,
• integration with labeling through MONAI Label,
• packaging and reproducibility through MONAI Bundles,
• a path from research code toward deployment.
For future MRI systems, I expect the more important question to become:
DICOM / MRI acquisition
v
medical-image-aware preprocessing
v
MONAI or equivalent medical AI framework
v
PyTorch backbone / foundation model
v
multi-GPU training
v
validation / annotation / deployment
rather than treating every medical imaging project as an isolated collection of custom image‑loading scripts.
So my position is deliberately split:
For this Kaggle competition: custom PyTorch pipeline first.
For future MRI/DICOM training infrastructure: MONAI deserves serious long‑term attention.
This is an empirical opinion from my current testing, not a claim that MONAI is already the universal standard
or that it will necessarily outperform custom pipelines on every task.

These last models should not simply be dropped into the classification pipeline as if they were equivalent back‑
bones. Their more natural role may be ROI localization, segmentation, anatomical supervision or 3D repre‑
sentation learning.
9. The More Important Question: Is the Backbone Really the Bottleneck?
This is where I think the competition becomes more interesting.
A knee MRI study is not a single image. It contains multiple series, different planes, different sequence sensitiv‑
ities and a variable number of slices.
So the full prediction problem is closer to:
DICOM study
v
physical orientation / position ordering
v
scanner- and spacing-aware normalization
v
series / plane selection
v
anatomically meaningful slice sampling
v
shared visual encoder
v
per-series / per-plane pooling
v
cross-series aggregation
v
12 target-specific heads
This means that a better backbone can still lose to a weaker backbone if:
• slices are physically misordered,
• PixelSpacing is ignored,
• the crop represents a different physical field of view across scanners,
• important series are omitted,
• irrelevant end slices dominate sampling,
• all 12 abnormalities are forced to use the same pooling strategy,
7
• or weak labels from reports are poorly calibrated.
That is why I currently view this as a study‑construction and supervision problem first, and a backbone‑
ranking problem second.
10. Debate: Questions I Would Like to Discuss With Other Competitors
Debate 1 ‑ DINOv2 vs DINOv3
Does DINOv3 actually improve knee‑MRI transfer under identical preprocessing and compute, or is the differ‑
ence smaller than the gain from better study construction?
Debate 2 ‑ Generic SSL vs radiology‑domain pretraining
Would a smaller RadImageNet‑pretrained CNN beat a much stronger generic visual foundation model when gold
labels are scarce?
Debate 3 ‑ 2.5D hierarchical models vs full 3D models
Is full 3Dmodeling worth thememory and runtime cost, or does carefully designedmulti‑plane 2.5D aggregation
capture most of the useful anatomical context?
Debate 4 ‑ Backbone scaling vs image resolution
If DINOv2‑Small is already representation‑sufficient, is compute better spent on 336/448 resolution and better
slice coverage than on DINOv2‑Base/Large?
Debate 5 ‑ One pooling rule or target‑specific pooling?
ACL tears, meniscus tears and fractures are focal findings, while osteoarthritis and effusion can be spatially dif‑
fuse. Should all 12 labels really share one aggregation rule?
Debate 6 ‑ Are we optimizing the wrong layer?
If report‑derived supervision and DICOM normalization dominate the error budget, then changing the backbone
may produce much smaller gains than improving labels, physical coordinates and series selection.
Debate 7 ‑ Competition framework vs long‑term medical‑imaging framework
MONAI may add abstraction and workflow overhead that is unnecessary for this particular Kaggle classifier, but
could that same structure become an advantage when moving from competition code to reusable MRI/DICOM
training, annotation and deployment pipelines?
I would be interested in controlled ablations from other competitors, especially experiments that change one
factor at a time while keeping folds, preprocessing and training budget fixed.
11. Suggested Controlled Benchmark
A simple benchmark matrix could look like this:
8
Experiment Backbone / pretraining Purpose
A ResNet34 / ImageNet Stable control
B DINOv2‑S Main SSL baseline
C DINOv3‑S New‑generation SSL test
D ConvNeXt‑T Modern pure‑CNN test
E EfficientNetV2‑S Efficiency test
F ResNet50 / RadImageNet Domain‑pretraining test
G Swin‑T Hierarchical Transformer test
Everything else should remain fixed.
Then separately ablate:
1. physical slice ordering,
2. fixed‑mm crop,
3. 224 ‑> 336/448 resolution,
4. slice coverage,
5. cross‑plane aggregation,
6. target‑specific pooling,
7. report‑label calibration.
This should tell us much more than comparing public leaderboard scores from notebooks with completely dif‑
ferent pipelines.

Closing Thought
My working hypothesis is that the strongest solution will not be defined by a single fashionable backbone.
It will be defined by how well the system preserves the physical and clinical structure of the MRI study, con‑
verts weak multimodal supervision into reliable targets, and then uses a sufficiently strong but computationally
efficient encoder.
For that reason, I would rather compare systems under controlled conditions than rank architectures by gen‑
eration or publication date.
Comments, contradictory ablations and alternative interpretations are welcome.

​Since the image model (Student) manages to bypass these textual gaps and detect the true visual features, have you tried using the model's high-confidence predictions to relabel the noisy data and train iteratively? or do you avoid this to prevent a confirmation bias

**[6]** **Aic** (@gunianxue, Expert) · 2026-08-17 09:09 UTC · LB rank 1758

I’d like to ask how long it usually takes to train a model using DINOv2. Also, roughly how much computational resources are required for this competition?

**[6.1]** ↳ **Tim Krige** (@timkrige, Expert) · 2026-08-17 09:44 UTC · ▲2 · LB rank 1201

This is a hard question to answer. Dino has a variety of sizes, small to huge. Within a size there are layers, with larger sized having more layers. In some competitions there have been amazing results from the pretrained dino weights and only finetuned heads. Others require unfreezing a few of the last layers, and still other competitions benefit from a full backbone unfreeze. The more of the backbone you unfreeze the more VRAM and GPU speed  you need. I am finding strong correlations between number of layers unfrozen and score, but I only have limited vram so I am typically training the smallest dino's.

I am on a 3070 and a 5070ti for most of my training. Models take a day or so to train 5 folds. 

A good technique on a tiny model is better than just throwing data at a large model and hoping compute can solve it, typically. You would also learn more like this.

I hope this helps.

<a id="t743534"></a>
## 8. What is difference between study and series?

- **লিংক:** https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/discussion/743534
- **লেখক:** **Yogesh Krishna** (@yogeshkrishna1709, Contributor) · _topic-এর লেখক_ · 2026-09-26 06:50 UTC
- **ভোট:** 0 · **কমেন্ট:** 2 · **শেষ কমেন্ট:** 2026-09-26 09:32 UTC (Yogesh Krishna)

What do these terms in problem statement actually mean? 
In a session, multiple MRIs taken, are these of same individual or different individuals? Because neither taking multiple MRIs of same individuals make sense nor does different individuals MRIs taken in same session/study.
Also does 1 study mean a single MRI only or multiple MRIs? (Ofcourse, represented as several DICOM series/files)

### কমেন্ট (2)

**[1]** **Wisdom Aduah** (@anaphase21, Contributor) · 2026-09-26 08:42 UTC · ▲2 · LB rank 2998

I will offer my layman understanding of this. All the series within a study represents a single person. So why multiple series? Well, each series tries to provide different evidence, or makes it easier to identify different abnormalities.

Within a study, three different views of the knee are taken: side view (sagittal), front view (coronal) and bottom view (axial). Each of these planes provides the reviewer a different angle to look for an abnormality. This is because an abnormality may only be visible when looking at it from one particular view (which you can't tell beforehand when performing the MRI scan). 

Each of these three planes may also have different properties such as fat suppression and fluid sensitivity. A normal MRI will make fat appear bright, which can make it sometimes difficult to differentiate fat from fluids. A fat suppressed series only allows fluids to look bright while suppressing fats from appearing bright. This makes it easier to identify abnormalities related to fluids such as effusion and synovitis. See the hosts's post [here](https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/discussion/733343) for more on this.

The slices in a series may also vary by thickness. Series with many slices will typically have thinner slices. If you consider all these variations (view, fat suppression, fluid sensitivity, and thickness), it's not difficult to see why a study can have up to 14 series. A sagittal series may have fat suppression or not. The same sagittal series may have different slice thickness as well.

In order to make a judgment about a knee abnormality, the reviewer will need to see all these series (different angles, different fat and fluid configurations) in order to have a holistic understanding of the knee's condition.

**[1.1]** ↳ **Yogesh Krishna** (@yogeshkrishna1709, Contributor) · _topic-এর লেখক_ · 2026-09-26 09:32 UTC

<a id="t733932"></a>
## 10. "Not addressed" is a label too — what we learned reading 4,407 knee reports with an LLM

- **লিংক:** https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/discussion/733932
- **লেখক:** **stevenleehans** (@stevenleehans, Expert) · _topic-এর লেখক_ · 2026-08-09 05:54 UTC · ▲55 · LB rank 1897
- **ভোট:** 55 · **কমেন্ট:** 9 · **শেষ কমেন্ট:** 2026-09-26 08:50 UTC (Lavin Wins)

**TL;DR** — Only 58 of 4,407 training studies have real labels, so everyone manufactures
targets from the radiology report. An LLM does this measurably better than a regex
(**0.8780 vs 0.8136** against the 58 gold studies). But the more useful finding is about
the *gaps*: **25.4% of all label cells are "the report does not address this"**, and what
that silence *means* is different for every finding. Getting that wrong costs more than the
labeller's accuracy does.

---

##### Credit

**LLM-read report labels are not our idea, and we were not first.** Several people
published them before us, and anyone arriving at this post should have their work too:

- **Pilkwang Kim** — [rsna-knee-llm-labels](https://www.kaggle.com/datasets/pilkwang/rsna-knee-llm-labels)
  (2026-08-06, the first of these) and
  [rsna-knee-baseline-v1](https://www.kaggle.com/code/pilkwang/rsna-knee-baseline-v1)
- **barun2104** — [Stratified Folds & LLM Soft Labels](https://www.kaggle.com/datasets/barun2104/rsna-knee-stratified-folds-and-llm-soft-labels) (2026-08-07)
- **lixin73** — [LLM Report Labels (GPT-5.6-Sol)](https://www.kaggle.com/datasets/lixin73/rsna-knee-llm-report-labels-sol56) (2026-08-08)

And the pipelines this work was measured against:

- **Karnakbayev Artur** — [rsna-knee-eda-to-2-5d](https://www.kaggle.com/code/karnakbaevarthur/rsna-knee-eda-to-2-5d)
- **Will / wguesdon** — [rsna-knee-dinov2-at-meniscus-resolution](https://www.kaggle.com/code/wguesdon/rsna-knee-dinov2-at-meniscus-resolution)
- **Alexandre Moritz**, **Roman Rozen** — public EDA and baselines

The observation that *labels*, not the encoder, are the binding constraint came from
measuring their pipelines — not from improving on them.

**So what is this post for?** Not the labelling. It is about what the labeller does when it
*refuses* to answer, which is a quarter of the time, and which we have not seen discussed.
If you already use one of the label sets above, section 4 is the part that may change what
you do with it.

---

##### 1. The setup, for anyone arriving late

`train.csv` has a `Report` column. `test.csv` does not. So text can only ever produce
**targets**, never a model input. And only **58 of 4,407** studies carry real annotator
labels for the twelve findings — 1.3%.

That means almost everyone in this competition is training against labels they invented,
and the quality of that invention is a ceiling nothing downstream can pass.

Measured against those 58 gold studies:

| label source | macro AUC vs gold |
|---|---|
| regex / lexicon extraction | 0.8136 |
| LLM reading the same reports | **0.8780** |

We published the labels: **[RSNA Knee LLM Report Labels](https://www.kaggle.com/datasets/stevenleehans/rsna-knee-llm-report-labels)**.

---

##### 2. The part we did not expect

We asked the labeller for a probability per finding, **with an explicit "the report does
not address this" option** that maps to 0.5. That turned out to be the most informative
design decision we made, because it makes the *gaps* visible instead of silently coercing
them to negative.

**25.4% of all cells came back at exactly 0.5.** And they are wildly uneven:

| finding | gold AUC | "not addressed" |
|---|---|---|
| **Synovitis** | **0.678** | **83.7%** |
| Baker's | 0.946 | 48.2% |
| Fracture | 0.793 | 42.9% |
| ACL | 0.993 | 8.3% |
| Medial Meniscus | 0.954 | 5.5% |

**Synovitis is the worst column by a distance — and 84% of it is missing.** Yet 27 of the
58 gold studies *have* synovitis. It is common in the joint and seldom written down.
The labeller is being honest, and the honesty destroys the column.

---

##### 3. One thing we tested, and it worked

Radiologists report **effusion** readily and **synovitis** rarely, and the two co-occur.
So we asked a single pre-registered question: does the *Effusion* field predict *gold
Synovitis* better than the *Synovitis* field does?

```
LLM Synovitis field  vs gold Synovitis : 0.6780
LLM Effusion  field  vs gold Synovitis : 0.7115   <-- yes
gold co-occurrence: P(syn | eff) = 0.63  vs  P(syn | no eff) = 0.22
```

**A field that is not about synovitis predicts synovitis better than the synovitis field
does.** Filling only the *undecided* synovitis cells from effusion — never overriding an
explicit statement — moves that column **0.678 → 0.790** and the whole key
**0.8780 → 0.8873**.

---

##### 4. The part worth your attention: generalising it made things worse

The obvious next move is to do that for all twelve findings. So we did, properly: for each
finding, learn a ridge model predicting it from the other eleven, **fit on the 4,407-study
corpus** (never on the 58 gold — those were used only to score, once), and use it to fill
that finding's undecided cells.

| key | macro vs gold |
|---|---|
| v1 baseline | 0.8780 |
| **v2 — synovitis only** | **0.8873** |
| v3 — learned imputation, all twelve | 0.8805 |

**The blanket version is worse than the targeted one.** Per label it splits cleanly:

```
Synovitis  +0.056     Baker's   -0.029
PF OA      +0.023     Contusion -0.031
Fracture   +0.015     Lateral OA -0.019
```

Here is why, and it is the actual lesson:

| finding | gold+ when report is SILENT | when it SPEAKS |
|---|---|---|
| Synovitis | **0.34** | 0.76 |
| PF OA | 0.21 | 0.41 |
| Baker's | **0.03** | 0.44 |
| Medial OA | **0.00** | 0.36 |

**When a radiologist does not mention a Baker's cyst, there almost certainly is not one —
3% versus 44%. The silence *is* the label.** Overwriting it with a correlation-based guess
throws away real information. But silence about synovitis still leaves a 34% chance it is
present, so there the silence is genuinely uninformative and imputing helps.

Across the nine readable findings, that "silence ratio" correlates **+0.59** with how much
imputation helped. (Nine points — suggestive, not proven.)

> **"Not addressed" is not one thing. For some findings it means *absent*; for others it
> means *nobody looked*. Treating them alike is worse than leaving the gaps in.**

---

##### 5. Honest limits

- **58 studies is a small ruler.** On it, differences below roughly 0.02 macro are not
  measurable. We have had three separate readings from this ruler overturned by the
  leaderboard — treat small gaps as *unknown*, not as zero.
- **A better key is not automatically a better model.** We swapped these labels in and got
  no gain on the first attempt; it only paid off after unrelated pipeline bugs were fixed.
- **We also tried the version that cheats** — selecting *which* findings to impute using the
  gold labels. It scored 0.8845, still below the disciplined 0.8873. Nothing was left on the
  table by not cheating, which is not always how that goes.
- **Gold prevalence is the annotator's sampling, not disease prevalence.** Every gold study
  has at least one positive finding; mean 4.14 per study.
- These are **model-generated labels, not ground truth** — a better approximation of what
  the report says, nothing more.

---

##### 6. If you take one thing

Ask your labeller for **"I don't know"** as a first-class answer, then look at where it
says so. The distribution of its refusals told us more about this dataset than its accuracy
did — and one column, repaired on that basis, was worth more than any change we made to the
images.

Labels and weights are public; questions and corrections welcome.

Thank you to the hosts for organizing this challenge and for taking the time to answer questions throughout the competition. I appreciate that assessing third-party datasets individually is difficult, especially when their access requirements and licenses differ.

Questions about external knee MRI data are spread across several threads. Without a clear answer for commonly used datasets, teams may train under different assumptions about what is permitted, which makes it difficult to ensure a level playing field.

I have read the [original question](https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/discussion/733652), the [registration-gated dataset question](https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/discussion/735497), and the [institutional-agreement follow-up](https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/discussion/737950). There are also separate questions about [pretrained weights](https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/discussion/735121) and [research-use agreements](https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/discussion/738111).

The host has also [explained](https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/discussion/733965) that non-commercial terms alone do not exclude a dataset, while straightforward registration and substantial institutional approval may be treated differently. | [NYU fastMRI](https://fastmri.med.nyu.edu/) and [fastMRI+](https://github.com/microsoft/fastmri-plus) | Data-sharing agreement for images; separately released annotations |
| [Osteoarthritis Initiative (OAI)](https://nda.nih.gov/oai) | Data Use Certification and institutional requirements |
| [KneeMRI](https://zenodo.org/records/14789903) | CC BY-NC-ND 4.0 |
| [KneeXNet-2.5D](https://github.com/pitthexai/Knee_MRI_Segmentation_2.5D) | Public OAI-derived annotated images and pretrained weights; no explicit repository license that I could identify |

Could the hosts give a **yes or no for each resource** under this competition's external-data rules? It would also help to know whether using each resource is compatible with the winner's requirement to release the final model. I understand that teams remain responsible for complying with the original providers' terms.

## 12. Rule 2.5.a.4 vs 2.8.a.1 — do weights trained on no-redistribution external data have to be published? (yes/no)

- **লিংক:** https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/discussion/743420
- **লেখক:** **Richard\_61** (@yiliu15, Contributor) · _topic-এর লেখক_ · 2026-09-26 03:15 UTC · ▲1 · LB rank 26
- **ভোট:** 1 · **কমেন্ট:** 0

@javaduke95 A narrow follow-up on the winners' obligations. The same point was raised in [#737950](https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/discussion/737950) and [#735121](https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/discussion/735121) but has no host answer yet.

Rule 2.8.a.1 requires that "the model weights should be provided as a public kaggle dataset so it is both publicly accessible and linked to the inference/submission code." Rule 2.5.a.4 says: "In the event that input data or pretrained models with an incompatible license are used to generate your winning solution, you do not need to grant an open source license in the preceding Section for that data and/or model(s)."

Several free, click-through knee-MRI datasets forbid sharing material derived from them: KneeMRI is CC BY-NC-ND 4.0, the MRNet Research Use Agreement (§6) prohibits derivative works, and the fastMRI Data Sharing Agreement prohibits distributing data files derived from the dataset. Weights trained on them may therefore not be publishable as a public Kaggle dataset.

We understand you cannot interpret the dataset stewards' own terms (as in [#741819](https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/discussion/741819)). This question is only about the competition's Rules:

1. When a winner's weights were trained on such data, does Rule 2.5.a.4 also exempt those weights from the Rule 2.8.a.1 requirement to publish them publicly — for example, by delivering them privately to the Sponsor for verification while all code is public?
2. If not, is such a solution ineligible for Leaderboard and Efficiency prizes, so that teams should remove that data before the Final Submission Deadline?

A yes/no on each would settle this for everyone before the deadline. Thank you.

**[2]** **Sanjib Biswas** (@aidiagnosis, Contributor) · 2026-09-25 07:01 UTC · ▲-1 · LB rank 2223

I went down a version of this road and it didn't pay, so hopefully this saves you the GPU time.

The short answer: the text round-trip can't add information, only lose it. At inference you have pixels and nothing else. Image -> generated report -> extracted labels is image -> labels with a lossy text bottleneck inserted in the middle. Whatever the VLM can see, a classifier trained directly on the same pixels can also see, without the generation and re-extraction steps each contributing their own errors.

The one version that isn't circular is using image -> text as pretraining - train the encoder to match report embeddings, then fine-tune a label head on top. I tested the premise of that and it failed: a linear probe on BGE-M3 embeddings of the reports (PCA to 128 dims) scored 0.8423 on 58 gold, against 0.8843 for LLM+regex extraction on the same studies. The embedding carried less retrievable label signal than twelve extracted numbers - much worse on MCL (0.78 vs 0.95) and lateral meniscus (0.78 vs 0.89), marginally better on contusion and fracture, all within the ±0.02 that 58 studies carry. I dropped the idea on that result.

Two things I measured that might be more useful to you:

Swapping the LLM is worth nothing. On all 58 gold: qwen2.5:14b rev4 0.8619, qwen3:14b 0.8572, qwen3:30b-a3b 0.8677. A 0.010 spread on 58 studies is noise. Watch out for reasoning models - qwen3's <think> block collides with JSON-constrained decoding and silently eats answers (12/58 and 52/58 unparsed until we turned thinking off).

Extraction is closer to its ceiling than it looks. I audited every gold positive, report score missed and asked whether the report contains any term for that finding, negation ignored. Extraction caught 207 of 240 positives, and 22 of the 33 misses were reports that never name the finding at all - synovitis, fracture and effusion miss only on silent reports. No reader recovers text that was never written, and neither will a VLM.

Given your labels already read 0.9083 on gold, I'd guess extraction isn't where your remaining headroom is either.

Reciprocating with a question, since your 0.935 on a single fold is well ahead of where I am: how many slices per series are you caching, and how many 2.5D windows do you feed per series? I measured mine corpus at median 30 slices per series (p90 39, p99 160), and going from 24 to 32 cached slices - 69% to 84% of all slices retained - was worth more to me than anything I did to the labels.

## 15. \[Baseline Notebook\] 2.5D ResNet-34 PyTorch Pipeline (Fold 0) \| Preprocessed 3D Volumes

- **লিংক:** https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/discussion/733934
- **লেখক:** **Barun Kumar** (@barun2104, Expert) · _topic-এর লেখক_ · 2026-08-09 06:32 UTC · LB rank 3687
- **ভোট:** 0 · **কমেন্ট:** 1 · **শেষ কমেন্ট:** 2026-09-26 00:29 UTC (Chris Deotte)

Hey Everyone! 👋
To help jumpstart model exploration in the RSNA Knee Abnormality Detection Challenge, I've published a complete, end-to-end [PyTorch baseline notebook](https://www.kaggle.com/code/barun2104/baseline-rsna-knee-mri-2-5d-resnet34) using a **2.5D ResNet-34 architecture** trained on Fold 0.

Handling 3D DICOM volumes directly during training can cause severe I/O bottlenecks. To make training fast, clean, and reproducible, this baseline runs off preprocessed, memory-efficient `(24, 160, 160)` volume arrays.

###### 💡 Key Notebook Highlights
- **2.5D ResNet-34 Backbone:** Instead of computationally heavy 3D convolutions, we map the 24 depth slices across input channels, adapting ImageNet pre-trained weights to leverage transfer learning.

- **12 Multi-Label Pathology Targets:** Trains directly on all primary targets (`ACL`, `MCL`, `Meniscus tears`, `OA`, `Effusion`, etc.) using `BCEWithLogitsLoss`.

- **Fast Batch Training (AMP FP16):** Uses Automatic Mixed Precision and batch size of 32 for rapid epoch iteration times on Kaggle GPU (T4 / P100).

- **Cross-Validation Metric:** Tracks Mean Macro ROC-AUC across Fold 0.

###### 🔗 Related Resources & Datasets
- **📓 Training Notebook:** [[Baseline] RSNA Knee MRI — 2.5D ResNet34](https://www.kaggle.com/code/barun2104/baseline-rsna-knee-mri-2-5d-resnet34)

- **📊 Metadata CSV Dataset:** [RSNA Knee - Stratified Folds & LLM Soft Labels](https://www.kaggle.com/datasets/barun2104/rsna-knee-stratified-folds-and-llm-soft-labels)

- **🖼️ Processed 3D Volumes Dataset:** [RSNA Knee MRI - Processed 3D Volumes](https://www.kaggle.com/datasets/barun2104/rsna-knee-mri-processed-3d-volumes)

###### 🚀 What's Next on the Roadmap?
- **Semi-Supervised Pre-Training:** Leveraging the `pseudo_*` targets in the metadata file to pre-train on unannotated studies before fine-tuning on ground truth folds.

- **Backbone Benchmarking:** Testing EfficientNet and ConvNeXt 2.5D variants.

- **Data Augmentation:** Adding 3D spatial transformations (random flipping, slight rotations, intensity scaling).

Check out the notebook, feel free to fork/copy it for your own experiments, and let me know if you have any feedback or ideas to improve the CV score!

**What model architectures or slice depths are you planning to experiment with first?**

Hi Jiwei Liu,
Our automated content review system recently found that your content is not compliant with one or more of our policies. See below for more information about your content status and how to correct the issue.

Result: We have unpublished the content and issued a ban on your account, unless we determine otherwise after an appeal.

The notebook referenced is a simple python script:
```
"""Build a reusable T4 TorchInductor cache for the M1-C9 MaxSpan graph."""

from __future__ import annotations

import hashlib
import importlib.metadata
import importlib.util
import json
import os
from pathlib import Path
import platform
import shutil
import sys
import tarfile
import time


REFERENCE_PARENT_MANIFEST_SHA256 = (
    "e71afd1717aa9c2751a7eafc36960c480a7f10f1da841d26d53cd331b47e51fd"
)


def sha256_file(path: Path) -> str:
    digest = hashlib.sha256()
    with path.open("rb") as handle:
        for block in iter(lambda: handle.read(8 << 20), b""):
            digest.update(block)
    return digest.hexdigest()


def package() -> Path:
    paths = sorted(
        path.resolve()
        for path in Path("/kaggle/input").glob("**/m0_c3_manifest.json")
        if path.is_file()
    )
    if len(paths) != 1:
        raise FileNotFoundError(
            f"expected one m0_c3_manifest.json, observed {len(paths)}"
        )
    return paths[0].parent


def import_file(name: str, path: Path):
    spec = importlib.util.spec_from_file_location(name, path)
    if spec is None or spec.loader is None:
        raise RuntimeError(f"cannot import runtime: {path}")
    module = importlib.util.module_from_spec(spec)
    sys.modules[name] = module
    spec.loader.exec_module(module)
    return module


def main() -> None:
    work = Path("/kaggle/working")
    cache = Path("/tmp/torchinductor_cache")
    cache.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=False)
    os.environ["TORCHINDUCTOR_CACHE_DIR"] = str(cache)
    os.environ["TORCHINDUCTOR_FX_GRAPH_CACHE"] = "1"
    os.environ["TORCHINDUCTOR_AUTOGRAD_CACHE"] = "1"

    import numpy as np
    import torch

    if torch.cuda.device_count() != 2:
        raise RuntimeError(
            f"cache builder requires two visible GPUs, observed "
            f"{torch.cuda.device_count()}"
        )
    gpu_names = [torch.cuda.get_device_name(index) for index in range(2)]
    if any("T4" not in name for name in gpu_names):
        raise RuntimeError(f"cache builder requires T4 GPUs, observed {gpu_names}")

    parent = package()
    manifest_path = parent / "m0_c3_manifest.json"
    manifest_hash = sha256_file(manifest_path)
    if manifest_hash != REFERENCE_PARENT_MANIFEST_SHA256:
        raise RuntimeError(
            f"parent manifest drift: expected={REFERENCE_PARENT_MANIFEST_SHA256} "
            f"observed={manifest_hash}"
        )
    manifest = json.loads(manifest_path.read_text())
    checkpoint = parent / "maxspan_member0.pth"
    checkpoint_hash = sha256_file(checkpoint)
    expected_checkpoint_hash = manifest["files"].get(checkpoint.name)
    if checkpoint_hash != expected_checkpoint_hash:
        raise RuntimeError(
            f"checkpoint drift: expected={expected_checkpoint_hash} "
            f"observed={checkpoint_hash}"
        )

    runtime_path = parent / "maxspan_runtime.py"
    runtime_hash = sha256_file(runtime_path)
    expected_runtime_hash = manifest["files"].get(runtime_path.name)
    if runtime_hash != expected_runtime_hash:
        raise RuntimeError(
            f"runtime drift: expected={expected_runtime_hash} "
            f"observed={runtime_hash}"
        )
    runtime = import_file("m1_c9_fast_maxspan_runtime", runtime_path)

    device_audits = []
    for index in range(2):
        device = torch.device(f"cuda:{index}")
        torch.cuda.set_device(device)
        torch.cuda.reset_peak_memory_stats(index)
        model = runtime.load_model(checkpoint, device)
        image = torch.zeros(
            (
                1,
                runtime.EVAL_WINDOWS,
                3,
                runtime.MODEL_SIZE,
                runtime.MODEL_SIZE,
            ),
            dtype=torch.float32,
            device=device,
        )

        def invoke(candidate):
            with torch.inference_mode(), torch.autocast(
                "cuda", dtype=torch.float16
            ):
                return candidate(image).float()

        torch.cuda.synchronize(device)
        eager_started = time.monotonic()
        eager = invoke(model)
        torch.cuda.synchronize(device)
        eager_seconds = time.monotonic() - eager_started

        compiled = torch.compile(
            model,
            backend="inductor",
            mode="default",
            fullgraph=False,
            dynamic=False,
        )
        torch.cuda.synchronize(device)
        compile_started = time.monotonic()
        compiled_first = invoke(compiled)
        torch.cuda.synchronize(device)
        compile_first_seconds = time.monotonic() - compile_started

        torch.cuda.synchronize(device)
        steady_started = time.monotonic()
        compiled_steady = invoke(compiled)
        torch.cuda.synchronize(device)
        steady_seconds = time.monotonic() - steady_started
        eager_values = eager.detach().cpu().numpy()
        first_values = compiled_first.detach().cpu().numpy()
        steady_values = compiled_steady.detach().cpu().numpy()
        device_audits.append(
            {
                "device": index,
                "name": gpu_names[index],
                "eager_seconds": eager_seconds,
                "compile_first_seconds": compile_first_seconds,
                "compiled_steady_seconds": steady_seconds,
                "max_abs_compiled_vs_eager": float(
                    np.max(np.abs(first_values - eager_values))
                ),
                "max_abs_compiled_repeat": float(
                    np.max(np.abs(steady_values - first_values))
                ),
                "peak_reserved_bytes": torch.cuda.max_memory_reserved(index),
            }
        )
        del compiled_steady, compiled_first, eager, image, compiled, model
        torch.cuda.empty_cache()

    archive = work / "m1_c9_maxspan_inductor_cache_t4.tar.gz"
    with tarfile.open(archive, "w:gz", compresslevel=6) as handle:
        handle.add(cache, arcname=cache.name)
    cache_files = sorted(path for path in cache.rglob("*") if path.is_file())
    cache_bytes = sum(path.stat().st_size for path in cache_files)
    archive_hash = sha256_file(archive)
    builder_source_hash = sha256_file(Path(__file__).resolve())
    shutil.rmtree(cache)
    if cache.exists():
        raise RuntimeError(f"failed to remove raw cache directory: {cache}")
    receipt = {
        "status": "VALID_M1_C9_MAXSPAN_INDUCTOR_CACHE_T4",
        "artifact_version": 3,
        "created_utc": time.strftime("%Y-%m-%dT%H:%M:%SZ", time.gmtime()),
        "builder_source_sha256": builder_source_hash,
        "python": sys.version,
        "platform": platform.platform(),
        "torch": torch.__version__,
        "torch_cuda": torch.version.cuda,
        "cudnn": torch.backends.cudnn.version(),
        "triton": importlib.metadata.version("triton"),
        "gpu_names": gpu_names,
        "gpu_capabilities": [
            list(torch.cuda.get_device_capability(index)) for index in range(2)
        ],
        "compile": {
            "backend": "inductor",
            "mode": "default",
            "fullgraph": False,
            "dynamic": False,
            "input_shape": [
                1,
                runtime.EVAL_WINDOWS,
                3,
                runtime.MODEL_SIZE,
                runtime.MODEL_SIZE,
            ],
            "input_dtype": "torch.float32",
            "autocast_dtype": "torch.float16",
            "inference_mode": True,
        },
        "cache_runtime_path": str(cache),
        "parent_manifest_sha256": manifest_hash,
        "maxspan_runtime_sha256": runtime_hash,
        "checkpoint_name": checkpoint.name,
        "checkpoint_sha256": checkpoint_hash,
        "device_audits": device_audits,
        "cache_files": len(cache_files),
        "cache_bytes": cache_bytes,
        "archive_name": archive.name,
        "archive_bytes": archive.stat().st_size,
        "archive_sha256": archive_hash,
        "raw_cache_removed": True,
    }
    receipt_path = work / "m1_c9_maxspan_inductor_cache_t4_receipt.json"
    receipt_path.write_text(json.dumps(receipt, indent=2) + "\n")
    print(json.dumps(receipt, sort_keys=True), flush=True)


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

<a id="t740610"></a>
## 18. Plateau at 0.903: sharing my pipeline and ablations, and looking for advice

- **লিংক:** https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/discussion/740610
- **লেখক:** **Will** (@pareru806, Contributor) · _topic-এর লেখক_ · 2026-09-10 12:48 UTC · ▲15 · LB rank 1498
- **ভোট:** 15 · **কমেন্ট:** 14 · **শেষ কমেন্ট:** 2026-09-26 00:21 UTC (Chris Deotte)

Hi Kagglers,

My current best public score is **0.903**. I have tried several backbones and approaches to report-derived supervision, but improvements of even 0.001 are becoming difficult. Reading the [discussion about compact models reaching 0.93–0.94+ at 224/288 resolution](https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/discussion/735304) has made me reconsider where to spend my next experiments.

I would appreciate advice on what to audit or ablate next. I am sharing my setup and some unsuccessful experiments so others can see what I have tried.

##### Current setup

- **Labels:** report-derived soft targets for the 4,407 training studies, with masks/weights for missing, uncertain or ambiguous supervision.
- **Images:** physical slice ordering using image position projected onto the slice normal; 130-mm crops cached at 336×336; six sequence slots; 12 selected slices per available slot, mainly within the central 20–80% of the stack. Selected slices are grouped into three-channel inputs.
- **Model:** mainly DINOv2-S/14, hierarchical aggregation within and across series, and twelve learned label queries.
- **Training:** 50-epoch schedules for the DINO members, using BCE-only or BCE plus a pairwise ranking loss.
- **Validation:** five-fold CV against fixed report-derived validation targets and masks. Promising pilots are extended to the remaining folds. The 58 expert-labeled studies have already informed development, so I treat them as a development panel rather than an independent test set.

The current composition has **four model recipes, each trained on five folds: 20 checkpoints total**. Fold predictions are rank-averaged within each recipe, and the resulting family outputs are rank-normalized for composition. Each label receives three equal-weight family contributions; I have not tuned those weights.

| Role | Backbone | Model input | Epochs | Loss | Training labels |
| --- | --- | ---: | ---: | --- | --- |
| Generalist | DINOv2-S/14 | 336 | 50 | BCE | All 12 |
| Specialist | DINOv2-S/14 | 336 | 50 | BCE | ACL, MCL, medial/lateral meniscus |
| Second DINO | DINOv2-S/14 | 336 | 50 | BCE + pairwise ranking | All 12 |
| CoAtNet | coatnet_rmlp_2_rw_384 | 384 | 25 | BCE | All 12 |

The CoAtNet input is resized from the same 336-pixel cache. The specialist replaces the generalist on its four columns. After rank normalization:

```text
Four specialist-owned columns: (specialist + CoAtNet + second DINO) / 3
Other eight columns:            (generalist + CoAtNet + second DINO) / 3
```

##### Diagnostics on the expert-labeled development panel

These are OOF predictions on the 58 expert-labeled studies: each study uses its held-out checkpoint from each recipe before composition. They are not predictions from all five checkpoints on every gold study. There are only 9–35 positives per finding, so the intervals are wide.

“Report target AUC” means the report-derived target scored against the expert labels on this panel. The difference from image-model AUC is descriptive; I do not think its sign alone identifies whether supervision or representation is the bottleneck.

| Finding | Positives | Image-model AUC (95% CI) | Report target AUC |
| --- | ---: | --- | ---: |
| MCL | 9 | 0.980 [0.932, 1.000] | 0.921 |
| Baker's | 12 | 0.958 [0.892, 1.000] | 0.902 |
| ACL | 24 | 0.958 [0.893, 1.000] | 0.944 |
| Medial OA | 15 | 0.950 [0.889, 0.992] | 0.891 |
| Medial Meniscus | 26 | 0.947 [0.888, 0.990] | 0.927 |
| Contusion | 19 | 0.915 [0.826, 0.981] | 0.821 |
| Effusion | 35 | 0.901 [0.804, 0.971] | 0.696 |
| Fracture | 18 | 0.875 [0.756, 0.969] | 0.814 |
| Lateral Meniscus | 23 | 0.858 [0.746, 0.949] | 0.894 |
| PF OA | 21 | 0.824 [0.696, 0.924] | 0.819 |
| Lateral OA | 11 | 0.782 [0.575, 0.954] | 0.829 |
| Synovitis | 27 | 0.693 [0.544, 0.824] | 0.676 |

Two observations are motivating my next experiments:

- **Synovitis supervision is sparse.** My extractor classifies 3,610 of 4,407 reports (about 82%) as `not_mentioned`. Overall, 3,745 Synovitis targets (about 85%) are masked, including uncertain cases. Separately, my report audit found that 12 of 27 expert-positive studies had no explicit Synovitis statement. This limits what direct extraction can recover from those reports; it does not establish the image model's ceiling.
- **Lateral findings remain difficult.** The image-model AUC is below the report-target AUC for Lateral OA and Lateral Meniscus. For Lateral OA, 10 of 11 expert-positive studies mention the finding in their reports. This motivates looking at image construction and training, but does not rule out problems with target quality or disease definitions.

##### Experiments that did not provide a useful further gain

Longer schedules and the specialist update have helped; by increasing the training epochs from 25 to 50, the AUC score raised from 0.899 to 0.903 on the public LB. The experiments below did not provide a convincing further gain in the recipes I tested.

- **Alternative public labels:** I tried [Steven's public LLM labels](https://www.kaggle.com/datasets/stevenleehans/rsna-knee-llm-report-labels). Better target AUC on gold58 did not translate into a convincing image-model improvement in my tested recipe. I do not regard target-quality differences as predicted model gains.
- **Explicit 2×2 spatial region tokens:** I appended pooled regional features to the label-query attention. At 50 epochs, the paired composition gain over two folds was **+0.000003**, with 95% CI **[−0.00086,+0.00084]**. This recipe did not help; I have not tested anatomical segmentation supervision.
- **Earlier DINOv3-B trials:** These were unsuccessful under an older pipeline. However, the configurations differed in preprocessing, supervision and training settings, so I do not regard them as a controlled conclusion about DINOv3 or about freezing versus fine-tuning.

##### Questions I would appreciate help with

1. **Study construction:** For strong single-model results, what mattered most: series selection, physical crop, slice coverage or sampling? What would you check first in the images actually reaching the model? In 2.5D inputs, have you compared consecutive source slices with neighbors in a sparsely sampled stack?

2. **Weak supervision:** How do you handle report-silent and uncertain findings? Have masking, weighting or objective changes helped more than further extraction work?

3. **Compact CNNs:** For competitors doing well with ResNet, EfficientNet or ConvNeXt, which broad training choices mattered most? I am running a matched ConvNeXt-Tiny experiment before spending compute on larger inputs.

4. **Validation:** How do you decide whether a small gain against noisy report-derived labels is worth pursuing while limiting dependence on the public leaderboard?

Thanks to everyone sharing experiments. The recent threads on [fold count](https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/discussion/740375) and compact models prompted this post. Even high-level observations about what to audit or test next would be helpful.

If any of this is useful to you, I'd genuinely enjoy comparing notes — especially with anyone working on MRI preprocessing, weak supervision, or a modelling approach different from mine. 

**I'm also looking for teammates!** If anyone would like to join my team — or would have me join yours — please let me know. I'd bring this pipeline and the full ablation history behind it, including the experiments that didn't work. Everything here was trained on my own GPU.

### কমেন্ট (14)

**[1]** **Tucker Arrants** (@tuckerarrants, Master) · 2026-09-10 17:19 UTC · ▲16 · LB rank 19

Some thoughts: take the best scoring single model public notebook (ignore the blends) and reproduce it locally. Use public LLM labels if you don't have your own, especially those that have documented extraction processes. Ideally you use your own so you know exactly where they succeed and where they fail. 

Start with 224px / 288px and a simple CNN encoder, like a ResNet34 or EffNetB0. No attention mechanisms, just use simple pooling operations. You can score in the 0.94+ range with this alone and it allows for efficient experiments at the lower resolutions.

Validate against the report extracted labels, not the provided 58 labels and not against the LB - neither are large enough sample sizes to resolve 0.002-0.003 differences. You want to stack these small improvements. Set a CV threshold for what is a genuine improvement and what is noise. Mine is around 0.003. You can get this tighter running multiple seeds if you need. When you get a real improvement, submit to LB to establish a CV - LB correlation.

This is your baseline. Then ablate through the standard image augmentation stacks. Many of these are generic and many are MRI specific, which you can get from previous competitions. Change one thing at a time so you can isolate what helped and what did not.

Then, you can try different backbones and resolutions, to see if the additional training time is really worth it.

After all of that, you can start trying more "experimental" mechanisms like attention and/or newer image encoders. I have yet to try any DINO inits and based on some preliminary experiments, I have a low prior for it matching standard ImageNet init backbones, but I could be wrong.

Kaggle public code section has been sullied with AI slop as people medal farm. Be cautious of this. There are only a few useful notebooks out there ([like this one](https://www.kaggle.com/code/dreaddevelopment/knee-mri-training-the-twelve-finding-model)). I generally assume a contrarian position about public notebooks - they are likely overly complex and not properly "built up" from simple baselines, using a clean CV protocol. Use them as their own baselines, and prune them to see what is actually useful and what is uneeded. "Prime" your own LLM saying something like "this code is probably over engineered, how can I simplify it?"

Last point - spend a lot of time just thinking about the problem. Have brainstorm sessions with your LLM of choice, without building anything. Stream of conciousness type chats. These "vibe chatting" sessions are where I get most of my ideas - LLMs may not be particularly creative in the context of Kaggle competitions, but they are great for organizing thoughts and finding ideas in that structure, that you might have otherwise not noticed yourself.
