**[1.2.1]** ↳ ↳ **Tucker Arrants** (@tuckerarrants, Master) · 2026-09-12 13:44 UTC · ▲1 · LB rank 19

DINO is not a model, it's a way of pretraining that basically teaches a model to match crops of itself. It works on any architecture. DINO v1 even released a ResNet50. I am guessing you are using DINOv2 which is ViT-only. So comparing DINOv2 to a ResNet changes the backbone, the objective, and the pretraining data all at once.

Seems like you also compared a LB result with a CV result. 0.86 CV could score 0.95 on the public leaderboard - everyone has different labels so I can't tell you what a CV of 0.86 means. Use the same fold split and find the CV between your models to compare them.

"DINO crop matching pretraining objective creates a useful prior for MRI classication" is a very different claim from "ViTs are better at MRI classifciation than standard CNNs" and using DINOv2 mixes both at the same time.

Hi Tucker, thanks for the detailed reply — this is exactly the kind of clarification I needed.

        DINO ≠ DINOv2: I was sloppy. I meant DINOv2 (ViT-S/14) specifically, and I now see the comparison against ResNet changes three things at once: backbone (ViT vs CNN), pretraining objective (DINOv2 self-supervised vs ImageNet supervised), and pretraining data (LVD-142M vs ImageNet-1k). Any of those alone could explain the gap.

        LB vs CV: You're completely right, and this is the more embarrassing mistake. The 0.932 is my public LB score, while the 0.86 is a local CV on my own fold — I shouldn't have compared them at all. 0.86 CV could easily correspond to a very different LB number, exactly as you said. I'll only compare models on the same fold split from now on.

        "DINO prior for MRI" vs "ViT > CNN": Yeah, I was conflating them. Since I've only tried DINOv2-ViT against supervised-ResNet, I can't attribute the gap to either factor.

    My plan to disentangle this:

        Run ResNet50 with DINOv1 self-supervised pretraining (Meta released that checkpoint) on the same fold split, same preprocessing, same labels, evaluated purely on CV.

        Also run a supervised ViT (e.g. vit_small_patch16_224.augreg_in1k from timm) under the identical setup.

## 21. Title: Building an open analytics companion for this competition — what would you want in it?

- **লিংক:** https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/discussion/742770
- **লেখক:** **RamónLópez** (@lopeznomar, Contributor) · _topic-এর লেখক_ · 2026-09-23 10:04 UTC · ▲-3
- **ভোট:** -3 · **কমেন্ট:** 1 · **শেষ কমেন্ট:** 2026-09-25 23:57 UTC (Chris Deotte)

Hi everyone,

Quick intro to what I'm working on, and a genuine ask for your input.

The problem this competition puts in front of us A si![]ngle knee MRI can carry a dozen different findings at once — ACL and MCL injuries, meniscus tears, osteoarthritis, effusion, synovitis, Baker's cyst, contusion, fracture. We're scoring on macro-averaged AUC across all of them, plus a separate efficiency track. That combination is unusual: a model can look strong on the aggregate number and still be quietly weak on two or three findings that happen to be rare. And the efficiency track means the fastest path to a better score isn't always a bigger model.

The leaderboard tells you where you are. It never tells you why.

What I'm building In the last Kaggle competition I entered, I built a free open-source analytics tool for replays. It ended up being used by people from 51 countries in the final weeks, and the biggest lesson was simple: competitors don't want another score, they want to understand their own model.

So I'm building the same idea for this one — a free, no-sign-up, open-source companion where you upload your predictions and immediately see:

Per-finding performance, so you know which of the 12 targets is dragging your macro AUC down
Your hardest cases: where the model is confidently wrong
Threshold and calibration behaviour per target
Efficiency trade-offs: accuracy gained versus runtime spent
Run-to-run comparison, so you can tell a real improvement from noise
Everything runs in your browser. Nothing gets uploaded or stored.

The ask It isn't finished, and that's on purpose. I'd rather build what you actually need than guess.

What would genuinely help you in the next few weeks? A view, a metric, a plot, a sanity check you keep doing by hand? Drop it in the comments and I'll work through the list publicly — I shipped one new feature per day in the last competition and plan to do the same here.

Where to reach me

Right here in the comments — I'll work through every suggestion publicly.
On X: @lopeznomar — I post daily build updates there.
By email: lopeznomar@gmail.com
The app itself is in the works — as soon as it's live, this thread gets the link, and the best ideas from this conversation become the first features.                                                                                                             
Good luck to everyone. 🦵

Independent open-source project. Not affiliated with, endorsed by, or sponsored by Kaggle or RSNA.

(https://www.googleapis.com/download/storage/v1/b/kaggle-forum-message-attachments/o/inbox%2F812296%2F1d1a6e3cd2b4892a9bb835c0ec7e326d%2Frsna-knee-ops-lab-intro-clean.png?generation=1790157292685080&alt=media)

The reason for (2) is your own fullspan result: widening the span at a fixed 44 slices cost 0.009, with the conclusion that widening only pays if slices are added to keep the sampling dense. If Fine Spacing is a 96-slice corpus, then scoring it on a 64-slot volume at 62 windows is that same density mismatch in the same direction - so the 0.932 wouldn't be available at 62 windows, and the checkpoint swap and the window count would be two different levers rather than one.

Putting 64 slices over the wide span brought it to 0.928, and 80 slices to 0.932. Density is the lever rather than span, and 1.20 percent of the stack per slice is the finest I've measured and also the best. Scoring an 80 slice volume at 62 windows leaves about a fifth of it unread, and at that point the checkpoint swap and the coverage change stop being separable, which is exactly what you said.

**[3.1.1]** ↳ ↳ **Ziad Ahmed** (@ziadahmeddd, Contributor) · 2026-09-19 08:11 UTC · ▲-1 · LB rank 1256

Reporting back as promised. Your density curve doesn't transfer to our pipeline, and I think the reason is instructive.
We tested it at fixed everything-else on one fold: 8 slices/slot → 10 slices/slot, which on our 0.12–0.88 band moves us from 1.58% to 1.27% of the stack per slice into the range you measured as best. It came back −0.0045 (plateau −0.0046) against a 0.003 noise threshold, so a real, small negative. We also tried tightening the crop 140 → 110 mm at the denser sampling: +0.0005, inside noise, unresolved.
The difference is probably structural rather than a contradiction. You sample 80 slices across whole series and score every window position, so extra slices buy genuinely new coverage. We sample 6 per-series slots into a masked attention head that pools 48 tokens, so extra slices mostly dilute the softmax rather than adding field of view the head already sees the whole span, just coarsely. Density is a coverage lever for you and a pooling-composition change for us.
Two other one-variable results from the same week, in case they're useful: swapping RadImageNet for ImageNet ResNet-50 at identical architecture and correct per-corpus normalisation cost us −0.063, and replacing our attention head with masked mean pooling cost −0.014. So for this pipeline the medical pretraining and the learned pooling are both doing real work.
Thanks again for publishing the geometry it saved us from spending a submission finding out that 0.932 wasn't available at 62 windows.

**[3.1.2]** ↳ ↳ **Dread Development** (@dreaddevelopment, Expert) · _topic-এর লেখক_ · 2026-09-23 01:20 UTC · LB rank 144

Thats a clean result & I think your read on it is right.

Worth knowing from this side, the lever has a ceiling for us too.
Going from 48-72 eval windows , a 50% increase moved the board 0.944 & cost 8 minutes of runtime. So it isnt that density pays for me & not for you, its that it stops paying & in your setup it stop paying earlier then goes slightly negative.

Coverage lever versus pooling compositon  change is exactly the distinction. If the head already sees the whole span, extra slices arent new information, they're more tokens competing in the same softmax & past some point thats a cost rather than a gain.

One that does go the other way for you is RadImageNet, that was a negative in my pipeline.

And your masked mean result matches something I saw from the other direction, changing the pooling at inference on weights that were trained with attention cost about 0.02. The heads weighting is not a formality.

**[3.1.3]** ↳ ↳ **Ziad Ahmed** (@ziadahmeddd, Contributor) · 2026-09-23 10:53 UTC · ▲-1 · LB rank 1256

That framing helps a lot "more tokens competing in the same softmax" predicts the shape we got, not just the sign. Our coverage curve over 4,407 studies, weak-label macro, shipped heads, nothing retrained:

k=16  0.8867     k=40  0.8934  (+0.0002 vs k=31)
k=24  0.8909     k=48  0.8939  (+0.0007)
k=31  0.8932     k=56  0.8940  (+0.0008)
k=62  0.8948  (+0.0016)

So it climbs to ~31 and then flattens for the next 30 windows. Noise floor on this ruler is ~0.002, so everything from 40 to 62 is one flat line with a slight upward drift at the end. That matches your 48→72 reading the density lever isn't differently-signed between us, it's just that we're further along the same saturating curve. Caveat on my side: those checkpoints were trained on these studies, so the absolute level is inflated; the shape is what I'd trust, not the number.

On RadImageNet going the other way I think I can offer a mechanism, because it was negative for us too until one change. Fold-0, out-of-fold, n=1102:

ImageNet ResNet-34,   3 adjacent slices as channels   0.8152
RadImageNet R50,      3 adjacent slices as channels   0.8339
RadImageNet R50,      centre slice repeated x3        0.8542

The trunk swap alone was worth +0.019. The channel contract was worth another +0.020 on its own. RadImageNet is pretrained on single-channel medical images replicated across RGB, so a 2.5D stack of adjacent slices is out-of-distribution for its first conv in a way ImageNet weights don't care about ImageNet was trained on real colour. If your pipeline fed it a slice triplet (which any Raptor-geometry setup naturally does), that's likely most of your negative. Costs one line to test.

Same family as your pooling point, now that I look at it: both are cases where the weights carry an input contract that isn't in the config, and violating it reads as "that component doesn't work here."

One disagreement worth flagging, since it's your shipped config: the reversed arm measures negative on our ruler. v5 forward 0.8943 vs reversed 0.8873, and adding both reversals takes the blend from 0.8948 to 0.8918. Your stack weights maxspan-v5-reverse at 0.10, so either our ruler disagrees with the board, or it earns its place through decorrelation inside a bigger ensemble than the two arms I'm testing it in. I'd believe the second just recording that I couldn't reproduce it as a standalone gain.
One more, unrelated but cheap: coatnet_rmlp_2 accepts img_size=256 with the 384 checkpoint and zero missing tensors, strict=True passes, 2.9× faster. It also loses 0.029 AUC. The rmlp bias generalises architecturally and not functionally  the tensors fit, the features don't survive.

## 23. Optimizations for blazingly fast submissions (\<10 minutes!) + Looking for teammates :)

- **লিংক:** https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/discussion/743374
- **লেখক:** **Prateek Grover** (@prateekgroverin, Contributor) · _topic-এর লেখক_ · 2026-09-25 19:18 UTC · ▲2 · LB rank 131
- **ভোট:** 2 · **কমেন্ট:** 1 · **শেষ কমেন্ট:** 2026-09-25 23:51 UTC (Chris Deotte)

On inference, I went from ~40 min to under 10 with a CoAtNet. A few things that helped, which might transfer:

- One model copy per GPU, each with its own shard of studies - replacing DataParallel, which re-broadcasts every parameter on each forward call. 
- Read each DICOM once, concurrently. One threaded pass per series serves both slice ordering and pixels. A single reader gets ~59 MB/s from /kaggle/input; 16 threads get ~372.
- Parse DICOM headers directly instead of through pydicom: 0.307 → 0.058 ms/file.
- Read pixels straight from the PixelData offset (np.frombuffer) - bit-identical to pydicom, 1.22 → 0.33 ms/slice.
- Take the 1st/99th percentiles on a strided subsample. np.percentile sorts, and that sort was ~60% of decode time.
- fp16 weights with half inputs, no autocast - 2.08× over autocast on a T4.
- uint8 to the GPU, normalise on device, pinned memory, non-blocking copies, persistent workers.

***Looking for teammates strong on labels/targets or bringing a resilient single model submission > 0.940.***

<a id="t742267"></a>
## 26. Multimodal Fusion Bottlenecks: Aligning 3D MRI Volume Embeddings with Multilingual Report Transformers

- **লিংক:** https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/discussion/742267
- **লেখক:** **Lavin Wins** (@lavinwins, Contributor) · _topic-এর লেখক_ · 2026-09-21 03:44 UTC · ▲-10 · LB rank 279
- **ভোট:** -10 · **কমেন্ট:** 8 · **শেষ কমেন্ট:** 2026-09-24 13:28 UTC (Lavin Wins)

##### Introduction:

Hi all,
As the leaderboard accelerates past 0.95, I’ve been experimenting with the multimodal aspect of the RSNA Knee Abnormality Detection dataset. While visual-only backbones give a solid baseline, properly fusing the 3D MRIs with the multi-language text reports presents a unique architecture bottleneck.

I wanted to share a specific roadblock I hit regarding cross-attention alignment, along with a lightweight fusion snippet, to see how others are tackling this.


##### The Roadblock / Insight

When feeding the 3D MRI embeddings (extracted via a 3D-EfficientNet) and text embeddings (via a multilingual BERT variant) into a late-fusion Cross-Attention layer, I noticed a severe gradient imbalance. The model aggressively overfits to the text reports within the first 3 epochs, completely ignoring subtle visual cues for rarer abnormalities like MCL tears.
To mitigate this, I implemented a custom Layer-wise Learning Rate Decay (LLRD) specifically for the text encoder to prevent it from dominating early training.

##### Quick preview of the Multimodal Cross-Attention Block I am testing

`

import torch
import torch.nn as nn

class RSNAKneeFusion(nn.Module):
    def __init__(self, visual_dim=1024, text_dim=768, embed_dim=512):
        super().__init__()
        self.vis_proj = nn.Linear(visual_dim, embed_dim)
        self.txt_proj = nn.Linear(text_dim, embed_dim)
        self.cross_attn = nn.MultiheadAttention(embed_dim, num_heads=8, batch_first=True)
        
    def forward(self, image_feats, text_feats):
        # image_feats shape: [B, S, 1024] (3D context tokens)
        # text_feats shape:  [B, T, 768]  (Radiology report tokens)
        v = self.vis_proj(image_feats)
        t = self.txt_proj(text_feats)
        
        # Query: Image, Key/Value: Text
        attn_out, _ = self.cross_attn(v, t, t)
        return attn_out.mean(dim=1) # Global fused representation
`

###The Peer-Level Questions :

Q1 ) Text Bias: Is anyone else finding that multimodal models overfit drastically faster than vision-only models on this dataset? Are you utilizing a warm-up strategy for the image backbone before introducing text?

Q2) Tokenization: With the reports being in a dozen different languages, did you find standard translation APIs helpful, or are native multilingual models (like XLM-RoBERTa) retaining the clinical nuances better?

Q3) CV-to-LB Correlation: My local 5-fold CV tracks perfectly with the text-dominant model, but I suspect a private LB shakeup if the hidden test set contains missing or brief text reports. How are you handling potential missing text modalities at inference?

- **লিংক:** https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/discussion/742327

- **ভোট:** 3 · **কমেন্ট:** 4 · **শেষ কমেন্ট:** 2026-09-22 04:15 UTC (Raymond Yuen)

Hello there... I'm pretty new to Kaggle. The only comps I've done with a private LB are MAP and S6E8, and in both the private scores barely moved (relatively), so I've never actually seen a serious shake-up. I'm quite curious about how this one will end.

So we're only given 58 gold studies, plus reports that don't exactly line up with the dicoms. My best training label (picked by LB, not by its AUC against the 58) sits at around 0.89 macro AUC against the gold, while my model's local eval against gold is around 0.925 and LB 0.947. So the model already beats the labels it was trained on, which makes local validation pretty hard to trust. My current approach is to just train on everything (eval set just soley on gold) and trust +0.002 level LB changes.

What I see is everyone's clustered up. The silver line and bronze line literally share the same three digits, and near my rank teams squish within a 0.005 range. Up there 0.001 looks like it's worth roughly 7–9 places, and there's a big pile of teams sitting on the exact same score. 

So how much does it usually move? And could this competition be more severe?

Any advice would be great. May the force be with you.

<a id="t742050"></a>
## 33. Why ~200 public forks sit at exactly 0.941 

- **লিংক:** https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/discussion/742050
- **লেখক:** **starkhushi** (@starkhushi, Expert) · _topic-এর লেখক_ · 2026-09-19 07:49 UTC · ▲2 · LB rank 607
- **ভোট:** 2 · **কমেন্ট:** 6 · **শেষ কমেন্ট:** 2026-09-20 14:31 UTC (starkhushi)

Around 200 teams share a public score of exactly 0.941, and most run a fork of @mattiaangeli's *Bend the Knee to the Dinosaurs* with the 0.941 recipe from @maverickss26 / @renta0426. I diffed the forks against the base and found three things worth knowing if you're in that block.

**1. The base notebook documents its own experiments.** Its `.ipynb` metadata holds an `rsna_optimization` entry per version, naming the single predictive change each version made. Pull the notebook with `kaggle kernels pull mattiaangeli/bend-the-knee-to-the-dinosaurs -m` and read the metadata of the JSON file. It's the closest thing to an ablation log this competition has.

**2. The 0.941 recipe is three changes, not four.** Against v27 (0.940) it changes the per-label outer CoAt weights, re-ranks the inner CoAt/Raptor hybrid, sets the Raptor arms to 0.55/0.15, and re-ranks `_ke_cr`. That last one does nothing: `_raptor.csv` is already a percentile rank at that point.

**3. Mattia's own stack keeps the Raptor arms at 0.60/0.10.** His ledger shows the per-label map and the inner re-rank adopted, but not the 0.55/0.15 arms. Every 0.55/0.15 build I could find (maverickss26, renta0426, mine, a public copy of mine) sits at 0.941, and @jiweiliu's speed fork independently reverted the same two weights.

Every number here is a real submission:

| configuration | public |
|---|---|
| v27, `model_sources`/`kernel_sources` missing (all 20 DINOv2 members silently dropped) | 0.937 |
| v27, complete sources | 0.940 |
| + per-label map, inner re-rank, arms 0.55/0.15 | 0.941 |
| + arms back to 0.60/0.10 | [SCORE] |

A runnable version with the full source checklist is here: https://www.kaggle.com/code/starkhushi/rsna-knee-why-public-forks-stop-at-0-941

One caveat: the per-label map was tuned on the public split, and `Lateral Meniscus = 1.00` discards three of the four stages for that column. Keep a flat-0.60 version as your second final pick.

### কমেন্ট (6)

**[1]** **Mattia Angeli** (@mattiaangeli, Master) · 2026-09-19 09:37 UTC · ▲4 · LB rank 101

The bigger point is that basically **no one besides [@DreadDevelopment](https://www.kaggle.com/dreaddevelopment) and me has released genuinely new models recently**.

Building a new model, prototyping it, training it and running proper ablations takes hours or days. Changing `0.55/0.15` to `0.60/0.10`, re-ranking, or tweaking per-label blend weights takes minutes.

Repeat those minute-scale changes across dozens or hundreds of submissions and you are simply overfitting the public LB. A `.940 → .941` move from another blend tweak is not strong evidence of a better model.

That is also why I would be very cautious about reading too much into whether 0.55/0.15 or 0.60/0.10 is “better.” Without new models or an independent validation signal, repeatedly searching those blend weights is basically another hyperparameter search on the public split.

Some of this is probably encouraged by the incentive to reach the top of the public leaderboard and make popular notebooks, but that is different from demonstrating better generalization on the hidden test.

**[1.1]** ↳ **?** · 2026-09-19 09:48 UTC · ▲-2

_(মুছে ফেলা বা খালি পোস্ট)_

<a id="t737312"></a>
## 40. Fluid\_Sensitive and Fat\_Suppression appear identical across all series

- **লিংক:** https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/discussion/737312
- **লেখক:** **RoshiBear** (@roshibear, Contributor) · _topic-এর লেখক_ · 2026-08-24 19:13 UTC · ▲2
- **ভোট:** 2 · **কমেন্ট:** 3 · **শেষ কমেন্ট:** 2026-09-17 15:13 UTC (PC Jimmmy)

In train_series.csv, Fluid_Sensitive and Fat_Suppression are exactly the same for all 24,371 series:

10,361 series have both = 0
14,010 series have both = 1
0 series have different values

However, the dataset description says that these two features are not necessarily the same thing. That also makes sense because, in MRI, a fat-suppressed scan isn't automatically fluid-sensitive.

So i was wondering:

Are these two columns actually supposed to be the same, or is one being derived from the other in this dataset?

This matters because if they're always identical, we might treat them as one feature instead of two.

Thanks!

### কমেন্ট (3)

**[1]** **Po-Hao "Howard" Chen** (@javaduke95, Contributor) · _host_ · 2026-08-28 17:56 UTC · ▲1

This question is best addressed at a general level rather than as a statement about how participants should interpret this specific dataset. Part of the challenge is understanding the characteristics of the training data and how the diagnostic space represented here relates to the broader complexity of musculoskeletal MRI. Any curated dataset is necessarily a subset of the real world, and even carefully defined features may not capture every combination or edge case encountered in clinical practice.

Fat suppression, whether achieved by inversion recovery, frequency-selective fat saturation, or another technique, is not inherently the same as fluid sensitivity.

In musculoskeletal MRI, however, the two can and often do occur together. Fluid-sensitive sequences commonly highlight edema, hemorrhage/contusion, and other abnormalities in soft tissues and bone marrow. Because subcutaneous tissues and bone marrow contain substantial amounts of fat, suppressing the fat signal generally makes these fluid-related abnormalities easier to visualize. 

As a result, most clinically useful fluid-sensitive MSK sequences are also fat-suppressed.

That relationship is not absolute. A fluid-sensitive sequence does not necessarily have to be fat-suppressed, and a fat-suppressed sequence is not necessarily fluid-sensitive. For example, when evaluating bone tumors or musculoskeletal infection, fat-suppressed non-fluid-sensitive sequences may be used before and after contrast administration so enhancement can be assessed directly.

Accordingly, while the two columns appear identical across the current training dataset, they represent conceptually distinct imaging characteristics. Their equality in the training data should not be interpreted as meaning the terms are generally interchangeable in MRI, or that they must be equal in another dataset.

<a id="t741310"></a>
## 43. Six data traps in RSNA Knee: slice order, missing laterality, silent reports (EDA)

- **লিংক:** https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/discussion/741310
- **লেখক:** **gchauhan** (@gchauhan, Contributor) · _topic-এর লেখক_ · 2026-09-14 15:31 UTC · ▲1 · LB rank 1696
- **ভোট:** 1 · **কমেন্ট:** 0

I published an EDA that tries to answer one question: what does this data force a model to do? 
https://www.kaggle.com/code/gchauhan/eda-rsna-knee-what-the-data-tells-us

**What stood out**

- Training targets come from reports; the score comes from radiologists reading images. On the 58 expert-labelled studies, reports are silent on about half of the expert-positive Synovitis cases. Fracture is in 7% of reports but 31% of expert labels. Treating "not mentioned" as "absent" teaches the model the reporting habit, not the finding.
- InstanceNumber runs against slice position in over a third of series. If you stack slices by instance number, neighbouring-slice context is scrambled for those series. Sort by position along the slice normal.
- Laterality is missing in about half of studies. Work out the side from geometry before mirroring right knees to left. If you flip for augmentation, medial/lateral labels (menisci, OA) have to swap too.
- The CSV hides the contrast. "non-FS" mixes T1, PD and T2, and most repeated series types are a second contrast rather than a retake. Keying slots only on plane × FS merges different images.
- A study is a variable set of 3–14 series. The most common combination covers only 40% of studies, so a missing-series mask isn't optional.
- Slices are ~10× farther apart than pixels (~0.3 mm in-plane vs ~3.5 mm between slices). That favours 2.5D, meaning a 2D encoder plus a depth model, over 3D from scratch, which is also what past RSNA spine/PE winners found.

The last section maps each finding to a modelling consequence and to what winners of past RSNA imaging competitions did.

Report labels are from @pilkwang's LLM-read report labels (https://www.kaggle.com/datasets/pilkwang/rsna-knee-llm-labels). Thanks!

Question for others: how are you handling "not mentioned" in the report labels: as negative, as a prior, or masked? And has anyone measured the effect of fixing slice order on local CV?

_কোনো কমেন্ট নেই।_

<a id="t741212"></a>
## 44. Winner's obligations when the submitted weights are a public checkpoint someone else trained

- **লিংক:** https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/discussion/741212
- **লেখক:** **Ziad Ahmed** (@ziadahmeddd, Contributor) · _topic-এর লেখক_ · 2026-09-13 22:13 UTC · LB rank 1258
- **ভোট:** 0 · **কমেন্ট:** 0

Section 2.8.a requires a winner to deliver "training code, inference code, model weights" and to publish the weights as a public Kaggle dataset. Section 2.6 separately permits freely and publicly available pretrained models, and the Code Requirements page states this explicitly.
Those two are clear individually but not in combination. Consider a submission whose predictions come entirely from a publicly available checkpoint that another participant trained and released, used without modification. The participant's own contribution is the inference pipeline preprocessing, windowing, aggregation, runtime engineering not the weights.
1. Can such a submission be eligible for a Leaderboard or Efficiency Prize?
2. If so, what satisfies the training-code obligation pointing to the original author's published training code, or must the winner have trained the weights themselves?
Section 2.8.a.a carves out commercially available software that the Sponsor can procure without undue expense, but says nothing about model weights, which is where the ambiguity sits.
This matters most for the Efficiency Track, where a single strong public checkpoint plus a fast inference pipeline is a natural approach, and asking now avoids discovering the answer at the Nov 5 deadline.

_কোনো কমেন্ট নেই।_

<a id="t740439"></a>
## 46. The leaderboard shows 3 decimals and I spent 20 submissions inside one of them

- **লিংক:** https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/discussion/740439
- **লেখক:** **Lê Quang Cảnh** (@zhincez, Contributor) · _topic-এর লেখক_ · 2026-09-09 17:06 UTC · LB rank 1454
- **ভোট:** 0 · **কমেন্ট:** 2 · **শেষ কমেন্ট:** 2026-09-12 08:48 UTC (Navneet)

I blended a public notebook with my own CoAtNet ensemble, then spent two weeks tuning the blend. Here is the whole weight sweep with the leaderboard score each one produced. W is the weight on the public source, rank-mean blending.

```
W_pub   LB
0.00   0.934   (pure my own ensemble)
0.15   0.935
0.30   0.936
0.45   0.936
0.50   0.937
0.60   0.937
0.70   0.937
0.75   0.937
0.90   0.936
1.00   0.936   (pure public notebook)
```

Ten points on the axis, four distinct numbers, and a flat plateau from 0.50 to 0.75.

Then I changed the blend operator instead of the weight, all at W = 0.60:

```
rank-mean                 0.937
probability space         0.937
logit space               0.937
geometric mean on rank    0.937
power mean, order 2       0.936
rank clipped to [.05,.95] 0.937
```

Then adaptive weights: per study, per label, twelve separate coefficients, the same thing with the sign flipped, a version with double the span. Every one of them: 0.937.

Counting up, 13 of my submissions read exactly 0.937 and I cannot order any of them.

The part worth sitting with: pure public is 0.936 and pure mine is 0.934, so everything my own ensemble adds on top of a public notebook is worth at most one displayed digit. Two weeks of blend tuning bought me 0.001, and I have no idea whether that 0.001 is real or rounding.

I do not have a clean answer, but two things I would do differently:

Check the display resolution against the effect size before starting an axis. Three decimals means anything under about 0.0005 is invisible, and blend weight moves the score by less than that across most of its range. That axis was never measurable here, no matter how many submissions I threw at it.

Build the local ruler first. Mine ran the wrong way: my 58-study gold set ranked variants in roughly the opposite order to the leaderboard, so I had no way to break the ties either. I kept submitting because the leaderboard was the only number I had, which is exactly backwards.

If anyone has a local validation setup that actually correlates with the board here, I would like to hear how you built it. Especially how you split, given how few studies carry the rarer labels.

### কমেন্ট (2)

**[1]** **ParsaAshrafi** (@parsaashrafii, Contributor) · 2026-09-12 08:26 UTC · LB rank 1035

Your observation is very reasonable. With a leaderboard displayed to three decimals, changes below roughly 0.0005 can be impossible to distinguish, so a large sweep may mostly be measuring rounding noise.

I would build the local ruler first:

- use study-level, multilabel-stratified splits;
- generate strict OOF predictions for every candidate blend;
- report bootstrap confidence intervals, not only one mean AUC;
- compare per-label deltas, especially for rare findings;
- only spend a submission when the expected gain is larger than the display resolution and remains stable across folds.

For blend tuning, tracking rank correlation between OOF variants and leaderboard results is also more informative than chasing equal displayed scores. If several submissions show the same rounded score, keep the simplest and most reproducible one.

**[2]** **Navneet** (@navneetbende, Contributor) · 2026-09-12 08:48 UTC · ▲-1

Thank you for the leaderboard showing 3 decimals @zhincez

<a id="t740094"></a>
## 47. Metric edge cases: single-class label columns, and corrupted DICOMs in the test set

- **লিংক:** https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/discussion/740094
- **লেখক:** **Piterson465** (@piterson465, Contributor) · _topic-এর লেখক_ · 2026-09-08 08:13 UTC · LB rank 2115
- **ভোট:** 0 · **কমেন্ট:** 1 · **শেষ কমেন্ট:** 2026-09-12 08:25 UTC (ParsaAshrafi)

Two narrow questions about how the evaluation behaves in edge cases. Both change
how a submission is built, so I would rather ask than guess.

1. Single-class label columns.
   The metric is the unweighted mean of 12 per-label ROC AUCs. If one of the 12
   label columns happens to contain only one class in the private test set, what
   does the scorer do with it: skip that column and average over the remaining
   ones, substitute 0.5, or does the submission error out? With 12 labels and the
   rarer findings this seems worth pinning down, because "skip" and "substitute
   0.5" give materially different macro scores for the same predictions.

2. Corrupted DICOMs in the test set.
   Two threads report training studies whose PixelData is truncated relative to
   the Group 0028 header, so pydicom raises ValueError on .pixel_array
   (discussion/738708 and discussion/737163). Are the test studies known to be
   free of this, or should submissions be written to survive it? A single
   unhandled file would turn a working submission into a zero, so if the test set
   is not guaranteed clean it would help everyone to say so explicitly.

Thanks!

### কমেন্ট (1)

**[1]** **ParsaAshrafi** (@parsaashrafii, Contributor) · 2026-09-12 08:25 UTC · LB rank 1035

Good questions. I would handle both cases defensively in the submission:

1. For a single-class label, do not assume that the scorer skips the column or substitutes 0.5. In local validation, `roc_auc_score` is undefined for a single class, so the exact competition behavior should be confirmed by the host.

2. For corrupted DICOMs, wrap `pixel_array` in a guarded loader, log the failure, and use a deterministic fallback such as a neighboring valid slice or a zero-filled image. This prevents one malformed study from crashing the entire submission.

Until the host confirms the scorer behavior and test-data guarantees, treating both cases defensively is the safest approach.

<a id="t740718"></a>
## 48. Clarification on Manual Annotation of Unlabeled Training MRIs

- **লিংক:** https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/discussion/740718
- **লেখক:** **oshiko0450** (@oshiko0450, Contributor) · _topic-এর লেখক_ · 2026-09-10 21:40 UTC · LB rank 1397
- **ভোট:** 0 · **কমেন্ট:** 2 · **শেষ কমেন্ট:** 2026-09-12 08:23 UTC (ParsaAshrafi)

Hi,

I would like to clarify one point about manual annotation of the training data.

I understand from previous discussions that:

* the 58 provided labels were assigned from the MRI images independently of the clinical reports;
* when the report and image-based label disagree, the image-based label should be considered authoritative;
* the remaining training reports may be used to derive weak labels.

The Competition Rules also state that submissions may not use information from hand labeling or human prediction of the validation dataset or test data records.

Would it be permitted for a participant to manually review some of the unlabeled MRI studies in the provided training set and create additional image-based labels, using the published competition label definitions, and then use those labels for model training?

If this is permitted, would participant-created labels for the provided training images be considered External Data under the competition rules, or would they be considered derived from the provided competition data?

Thank you for the clarification.

### কমেন্ট (2)

**[1]** **PC Jimmmy** (@pcjimmmy, Master) · 2026-09-11 03:17 UTC · LB rank 639

I believe the rules state that you cannot hand label test images - that's not applicable to this competition as we cannot see the test images.  It was applicable in competitions in the past when the test images were available for download/review.

If you want to manually examine and label the train images - go for it.  Modification of train data has NEVER been considered as "external data".

**[2]** **ParsaAshrafi** (@parsaashrafii, Contributor) · 2026-09-12 08:23 UTC · LB rank 1035

Based on Section 3.4(b), the restriction applies to hand-labeling or human prediction of validation/test records. It does not prohibit manually reviewing the provided training MRIs. Since those images are part of the Competition Data, participant-created labels for the training set would generally be derived labels rather than External Data.

I would still recommend documenting the labeling protocol and asking the host for explicit confirmation before relying on it, especially if the labels are used in a final submission.

<a id="t738339"></a>
## 53. Stuck around 0.74 OOF - what separates the 0.87 tier from 0.93+?

- **লিংক:** https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/discussion/738339
- **লেখক:** **Abdullah Wasee** (@abdullahwasee, Contributor) · _topic-এর লেখক_ · 2026-08-31 10:57 UTC · ▲1 · LB rank 2417
- **ভোট:** 1 · **কমেন্ট:** 3 · **শেষ কমেন্ট:** 2026-09-09 08:44 UTC (Nathan Jacob)

Hi - first Kaggle competition, and I've hit a wall I can't reason my way past. Sharing what I've measured in case the gap is obvious to someone who's been here longer.

Where I am. 0.739 macro AUC on the 58 annotated studies, 5-fold. Report extraction at 0.725 against the same 58.

Pipeline. Rule-based multilingual extraction (clause-scoped negation in both directions, severity grading), then a 2.5D ResNet-34 with gated attention pooling over 4 sequence slots × 3 adjacent-slice triplets at 256px, 140 mm physical crop.

Things that measurably helped me, in case they're useful to other beginners:

Sorting slices by ImagePositionPatient projected onto the slice normal rather than filename. Filenames are SOP Instance UIDs and are effectively random - this was my single biggest bug.
Grading mild findings lower. I found reports saying "geringer Gelenkerguss" (slight effusion) annotated as negative, so scoring every mention at 0.85 was systematically wrong. Effusion went 0.628 → 0.726, ACL 0.775 → 0.855.
Sharpening the soft labels: BCE against targets clustered near 0.5 barely trains. GOLD went 0.653 → 0.725.

Where I'm stuck. Medial Meniscus 0.59 and MCL 0.60 - the thin structures. Everything large and diffuse is fine (Medial OA 0.89, Effusion 0.88).

What I'd love guidance on:

Reading public notebooks, the consensus seems to be 336px with 3 slices rather than more slices at lower resolution. Is that mostly resolution, or mostly that adjacent triplets beat spread-out slices? I've seen both claimed.
How much of the ResNet → DINOv2 gap is real versus fold noise? One post measured DINOv2-S → B as a null (+0.0011), which makes me unsure whether the backbone matters at all past a point.
Public solutions seem to plateau near 0.87 while the top is 0.952. Is that gap ensembling and scale, or is there a class of technique the published notebooks aren't using?

Happy to share my full ablation log if useful. Thanks for reading - the discussions here have already saved me several wasted weeks.

### কমেন্ট (3)

**[1]** **Rasoul Mojtahedzadeh** (@rasoulmojtahedzadeh, Expert) · 2026-08-31 11:17 UTC · ▲2 · LB rank 791

Use Steven's knee report dataset to see if it improves your LB score:

https://www.kaggle.com/datasets/stevenleehans/rsna-knee-llm-report-labels

**[1.1]** ↳ **Abdullah Wasee** (@abdullahwasee, Contributor) · _topic-এর লেখক_ · 2026-08-31 17:47 UTC · ▲1 · LB rank 2417

Thanks, that made a bigger difference than I expected.

**[2]** **Nathan Jacob** (@nathanjacob, Expert) · 2026-09-09 08:44 UTC · LB rank 464

Your pipeline is actually solid - ResNet-34, gated attention pooling, 4 sequence slots, clause-scoped negation in extraction. The
fact that you caught the DICOM sorting bug (SOP Instance UID vs ImagePositionPatient) tells me you're debugging at the right level.
                                                                                                                                   
I think the gap from 0.74 → 0.87+ is mostly a label quality ceiling, not an architecture problem. Here's why:                    
  
**The label extraction bottleneck**                                                                                              
                                                                                                                                   
With only 58 gold-labeled studies out of 4,407, you're almost entirely dependent on your rule-based extraction for training signal. The thread on best single-model scores (735304) has a revealing pattern: every jump from 0.87 → 0.93+ came from better label extraction (mostly LLM-based), not from architecture changes. One competitor got 0.949 single-model just by switching to Gemma 4 for local label extraction.                                                                                              

The consensus emerging is that a "single prompt LLM extraction has not been beaten by more sophisticated extraction procedures" -and the image model ("student") consistently outperforms the label extractor ("teacher"), which means better labels → the model learns what the labels can't explicitly capture.                                                                                 
                                                                                                                                   
**Resolution might also be a factor**
                                      
Your 256px at 140mm crop gives you 0.547 mm/px effective resolution. Some of the 0.93+ models are running at 336-384px (0.383-0.416 mm/px at the same physical crop). For subtle findings like early OA or small meniscal tears, that resolution difference could matter — especially in the coronal plane where compartmental OA grading needs to resolve joint space narrowing. 
                                                                                                                                   
**What I'd try in your position:**                                                                                               
                                      
1. Before touching architecture: swap your rule-based extraction for Steven's public labels dataset or run a local LLM extraction pass. This is probably the single biggest lever.                                                                                
2. If you want to stay rule-based: make sure your extraction handles severity grading for effusion/synovitis (these are the hardest labels - reports often say "mild effusion" without a clear positive/negative boundary) and multilingual reports (9-12 languages in this dataset).
3. Try bumping to 336px at the same 140mm physical crop — relatively cheap experiment that might add a point or two.             
                                                                                                                                   
The good news: your pipeline fundamentals (attention pooling, physical crop, proper slice ordering) are in the right ballpark.   
The bad news: below 0.87, it's almost certainly a data problem, not a model problem.

<a id="t740162"></a>
## 54. Things that helped (and didn't) when approaching RSNA Knee from scratch   

- **লিংক:** https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/discussion/740162
- **লেখক:** **Nathan Jacob** (@nathanjacob, Expert) · _topic-এর লেখক_ · 2026-09-08 14:15 UTC · ▲1 · LB rank 460
- **ভোট:** 1 · **কমেন্ট:** 1 · **শেষ কমেন্ট:** 2026-09-08 19:02 UTC (Rasoul Mojtahedzadeh)

I've been working through this competition over the past few days and wanted to share what I've      
learned, since a lot of it wasn't obvious coming in.                                               
                                                                                                           
**The labeling situation is tricky.** Only 58 out of 4,407 studies have gold labels. The rest need to be 
extracted from radiology reports — and those reports are in 9+ languages. I wrote up the data structure  
and DICOM quirks in [Know Your Knee](https://www.kaggle.com/code/nathanjacob/know-your-knee-eda-dicom-deep-dive-and-baseline) if you're just getting started.                                                                                    
                                                                                                           
**The 4-arm ensemble architecture** that the top solutions use is really clever once you break it down - separate heads for different MRI planes fused with attention. I tried to explain the intuition behind it in [4-Arm Ensemble Explained](https://www.kaggle.com/code/nathanjacob/4-arm-ensemble-explained-rsna-knee-0-937).
                                                                                                           
**The multilingual reports** are an underexplored angle. Running DINOv2 with cross-lingual label
extraction covers a lot more of the dataset than English-only approaches. Wrote about that [here](https://www.kaggle.com/code/nathanjacob/dinov2-baseline-knees-in-9-languages).                    
                                                                                       
Would love to hear what's working for others. Anyone had luck with the efficiency track?

### কমেন্ট (1)

**[1]** **Rasoul Mojtahedzadeh** (@rasoulmojtahedzadeh, Expert) · 2026-09-08 19:02 UTC · LB rank 789

Nice summary!
What didn't work for me so far:
1. DinoV3 doesn't perform as good as DinoV2 for me, which is strange.
2. I tried hard to use reports as an auxiliary loss, unfortunately it hurts the performance no matter how much I played with its weight in the total loss. It seems that reports are quite noisy, unfortunately.

**[1]** **PC Jimmmy** (@pcjimmmy, Master) · 2026-09-02 12:56 UTC · ▲1 · LB rank 641

**[2]** **Seung Sup Lee** (@seungsuplee, Contributor) · 2026-09-02 14:48 UTC · ▲1 · LB rank 2633

Yes, you can train on your own GPU.

You can set up Ubuntu locally with Python, PyTorch, CUDA, and the libraries you need, then do most of your training there.

If you are starting now, I recommend targeting Kaggle’s **Tesla T4 x2** environment rather than the P100, since the P100 is being retired.

One important thing: each T4 has **16 GB of VRAM**. T4 x2 means two separate 16 GB GPUs, not one 32 GB GPU.

For a beginner, I strongly recommend starting with **one GPU first**. Make sure the whole training or inference pipeline finishes correctly within 16 GB VRAM. Once that works reliably, then modify the code to use both T4 GPUs in parallel.

Multi-GPU code adds extra complexity, so debugging is much easier if the single-GPU version already works.

Also, if your local GPU has much more VRAM, your code may work locally but fail on Kaggle. You may need to reduce the batch size, input size, model size, or memory usage before uploading it.

So the safest approach is:

**Local Ubuntu → one GPU → fit within 16 GB VRAM → complete the full pipeline → then optimize for T4 x2.**

**[3.1.1]** ↳ ↳ **Dread Development** (@dreaddevelopment, Expert) · 2026-09-03 03:15 UTC · ▲1 · LB rank 145

Hey! So I treat vast as disposable compute, not storage so small files & code go directly over SSH with scp on Linux
rsync -avP is better because interrupted transfers resume.
For large datasets, upload them once to a persistent storage like a vast volume, S3 etc then let every new instance pull from there instead of uploading repeatedly.
I do use the spot instances with little to no issue ever, you just have to make sure you're monitoring & saving checkpoints.
I have also found that chunking & sending parallel transfers is much more effective for large files.
I have used the spot instances for a number of comps as well as my own distillation, pre training & fine tuning with less issues than I have finger so, hope this helped!
If you have any more questions feel free!

Is there something I'm missing here, or are the reports completely useless for the competition?

### কমেন্ট (4)

**[1]** **PC Jimmmy** (@pcjimmmy, Master) · 2026-09-02 01:21 UTC · ▲2 · LB rank 641

You identified the problem.  You need to build a model that uses the 12 categories as labels based on examination of only the dicom images.

To get the training data you have to convert the reports to labels between 0.0 and 1.0 for each of the 12 classes.  Not an easy task.  The best set of labels probably wins the competition.

You want to learn how to make labels - dig in.   

You only want to learn the prediction model than a number of folks have shared a set of pretty decent labels.   

Learning to make labels is a skill set you need in the real world.   Not one that LLM's seem to have mastered yet.

**[1.1.1]** ↳ ↳ **PC Jimmmy** (@pcjimmmy, Master) · 2026-09-02 01:33 UTC · ▲1 · LB rank 641

I been here for almost 9 years - have not participated in every competition but I think this is the first were we were not handed the labels. **[1.2]** ↳ **Komil Parmar** (@komilparmar, Expert) · 2026-09-02 05:22 UTC · ▲1 · LB rank 59

@pcjimmmy nicely shared. I started to read works on Pseudo Labelling which is probably the single best investment anyone can make in this competition. And I was stunned by how many papers are there just regarding pseudoabelling. I also suggested the paperswithcode site to add that as a separate category and **they added it**. 🤯

The problem though which one will realize while applying those methods is that all of those papers are regarding pseudo labelling on unlabeled data. That isn't the situation here. We already have near perfect labels for those samples achieved using llms. And if you replace them or even update them via pseudoabelling, the model will perform worse then simply training on LLM labels.

So we have to learn how to update and when to update those pseudo labels. That's the key. 🔑

Hi Everyone,

While building and decoding the volume cache for the training set using pydicom, I encountered pipeline crashes caused by corrupted pixel buffer sizes in 2 specific DICOM studies.

 Problem Description
The pydicom decoder fails when calling dcm.pixel_array due to a byte mismatch between the metadata header (Group 0028) and the actual pixel payload. The actual byte count in the buffer is exactly half of what is expected.

Affected Study UIDs & Errors:
Study UID: 1.2.826.0.1.3680043.8.498.34685905030370793639196564723935583035

ValueError: The number of bytes of pixel data is less than expected (409600 vs 819200 bytes)

Study UID: 1.2.826.0.1.3680043.8.498.37833587429731221455928642963031995680

ValueError: The number of bytes of pixel data is less than expected (173056 vs 346112 bytes)

Hopefully, the host team can inspect or patch these two DICOM volumes if necessary.

## 64. Has anyone tried a GRU/LSTM head over slices here?

- **লিংক:** https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/discussion/738495
- **লেখক:** **Abdullah Wasee** (@abdullahwasee, Contributor) · _topic-এর লেখক_ · 2026-08-31 17:46 UTC · LB rank 2417
- **ভোট:** 0 · **কমেন্ট:** 0

Every RSNA winner I can find uses a recurrent head to aggregate slices rather than pooling - 2019 hemorrhage (1st place was literally titled "Sequential model wins"), 2022 cervical spine (CNN+LSTM), 2023 abdominal trauma (CNN+GRU, max-pooled over slice logits), 2024 lumbar spine. The 2023 writeup says GRU beat self-attention in their ablations.

I haven't seen it mentioned in this competition's discussions, where the consensus seems to be attention pooling over sequence slots.

My guess at why it might not transfer: those are CT volumes with 96 slices, and most setups here use 3–9 slices per plane. A GRU over 3 steps has little sequence to model.

Has anyone tried it? And if so, did more slices per plane make it worth it, or does the resolution trade-off kill it?

Currently at 0.807 public / 0.751 on the annotated 58, ResNet-34 2.5D with attention pooling, so I'm asking from behind - happy to share my ablation log if useful.

_কোনো কমেন্ট নেই।_

## 68. External data rule — datasets that are free to access but carry research-use agreements

- **লিংক:** https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/discussion/738111
- **লেখক:** **pizzaboy** (@pizzaboythe3rd, Contributor) · _topic-এর লেখক_ · 2026-08-29 16:33 UTC · ▲3 · LB rank 74
- **ভোট:** 3 · **কমেন্ট:** 0

The rules allow external data if it is "freely & publicly available to all — pretrained models included."

Some widely used academic medical-imaging datasets (for example the Stanford AIMI releases) are free for anyone to access, but access is through a standard research-use agreement whose terms include non-commercial-use and no-derivative-works clauses.

Two questions, so we can plan within the rules:

1. Does a dataset behind such a free-but-signed research-use agreement count as "freely & publicly available to all" for this competition?
2. If a winning solution used a model pretrained on such a dataset, would that conflict with the winners' open-sourcing obligation as the hosts read it?

A written answer either way would be appreciated — happy to simply not use such data if the answer is no.

<a id="t738096"></a>
## 69. Dinov2 Base vs Small at 224 resolution

- **লিংক:** https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/discussion/738096
- **লেখক:** **Komil Parmar** (@komilparmar, Expert) · _topic-এর লেখক_ · 2026-08-29 14:20 UTC · ▲5 · LB rank 59
- **ভোট:** 5 · **কমেন্ট:** 2 · **শেষ কমেন্ট:** 2026-08-29 15:18 UTC (Komil Parmar)

Just wanted to share a small ablation, so you don't have to conduct the same. 

So far I had used only the small variant of DINOv2 for all my experiments, so I tried whether base gives any further improvement. Everything else about the two runs is identical, like same data, same schedule, same augmentation, same seed. Only the backbone was swapped.

Findings (gold macro AUC by epoch chart attached):

![Dinov2 Small vs base variant Gold 58 score](https://www.googleapis.com/download/storage/v1/b/kaggle-forum-message-attachments/o/inbox%2F11351811%2Fe70c9accde9a4dbd06307dff24d40073%2Fdinov2_small_vs_base_gold.png?generation=1788012410517623&alt=media)  

Base converges clearly faster: it holds a lead of up to ~+0.015 through the early and middle epochs.
However, given a long enough schedule, both curves converge to the same final score, the endpoint difference is within noise (see the green/red delta bars fading to zero).

Practical takeaway: if for some reason you are fighting against overfitting (YOU SHOULD NOT BTW) or you're compute-bound or just want training short schedules for any other reason, base reaches a given score in noticeably fewer epochs. If you train to convergence, small gets you the same result at roughly 4× less compute and it's the **obviously better choice for the efficiency prize**.

This is consistent with what was discussed in the earlier encoder-size thread, capacity doesn't seem to raise the ceiling at 224. Whatever is limiting scores at this resolution, it doesn't appear to be model size. 

Caveats: single fold, single seed, 224 px only, I haven't tested whether the same holds at higher resolutions BUT I do believe the trend will favor larger variants at larger resolution.

I know the configuration and other training details are missing, but that's intentional. :`)

### কমেন্ট (2)

**[1]** **PC Jimmmy** (@pcjimmmy, Master) · 2026-08-29 15:12 UTC · ▲1 · LB rank 641

<a id="t735826"></a>
## 70. What is the real bottleneck now: labels, image pipeline, or model capacity?

- **লিংক:** https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/discussion/735826
- **লেখক:** **Oscar Yáñez Feijóo** (@oscaryezfeijo, Contributor) · _topic-এর লেখক_ · 2026-08-18 09:38 UTC · ▲13 · LB rank 2335
- **ভোট:** 13 · **কমেন্ট:** 11 · **শেষ কমেন্ট:** 2026-08-29 12:15 UTC (?)

I’ve been following the recent discussions and there seems to be an interesting pattern:

- Single DINO-based models can already reach around **0.91–0.92+**.
- Increasing encoder size does not necessarily improve CV.
- Several people report meaningful gains from **crop geometry, resolution and slice selection**.
- At the same time, most of the training labels are derived from reports, and label quality may itself impose a ceiling.

This makes me wonder: **what do you currently think is the main bottleneck in this competition?**

A. Report-derived label quality

B. MRI preprocessing / physical crop / slice selection

C. Model architecture or backbone capacity

D. Validation strategy

E. Something else entirely

Without revealing solution-specific details, I’d be interested to hear which category has produced the largest improvements in your own experiments.

For me, the emerging lesson is that improving what the model *sees* and what we *teach it* may be more important than simply using a larger backbone.

### কমেন্ট (11)

**[1]** **Seung Sup Lee** (@seungsuplee, Contributor) · 2026-08-18 10:26 UTC · ▲1 · LB rank 2631

Interesting question. I’m new to this competition, but I’ve spent the last few days approaching it mainly as a systems/optimization problem rather than assuming that a larger model will automatically give a better result.

So far, my experiments seem to support B, with A potentially being just as important.

I first froze my CPU preprocessing pipeline as a control. It preprocesses all 4,407 studies into a fixed (4407, 6, 9, 224, 224) uint8 cache. I then kept that exact cache and label pipeline unchanged while testing the GPU side.

With a pretrained ResNet34 pipeline, my current control OOF BCE is about 0.664. I then replaced the representation/model side with pretrained DINOv2-Small, including diagnosis-specific slot attention, while keeping the same CPU data and labels.

Surprisingly, simply moving to DINOv2 did not improve the result. My best 5-fold DINO candidate produced approximately 0.674 OOF BCE (rank correlation ~0.391), actually worse than the ResNet34 control on BCE. Increasing or changing model-side complexity therefore did not solve the problem by itself.

That result changed where I’m spending my compute.

My next controlled experiment keeps the successful ResNet34 GPU pipeline fixed and changes only the image preprocessing: physical slice ordering using DICOM orientation/position, removal of stack-end regions, anatomically better slice grouping, and fixed-mm physical cropping before resize. This should let me measure the contribution of the image pipeline independently from the backbone.

After that, I want to isolate label quality in the same way. Most of my current training targets are report-derived soft labels, so I suspect there is also a point where better image representation cannot compensate for noisy supervision.

One practical lesson from these experiments has been that compute cost and model size are poor proxies for useful information. A more expensive model can simply process the same imperfect input more expensively.

At this stage my working hypothesis is:

image geometry / slice selection + label quality > backbone size.

I don’t consider that conclusion proven yet, but the controlled experiments so far are pushing me strongly in that direction.

**[2]** **Tom Aindow** (@taindow, Master) · 2026-08-18 13:20 UTC · ▲3 · LB rank 81

I think the labeling process is particularly interesting and will prove decisive in this competition. It's one thing to extract labels from the report, but another to faithfully reproduce the labels derived from the image. If you look over the gold set it's not hard to find instances where the image label clearly flouts the rules of diagnosis laid out in the introduction to the competition (e.g. the report says "small effusion" and the effusion label is set to 1, even though only a "moderate or large" is the heuristic. It's not surprising, as there is an element of subjectivity in such a judgement, and no doubt it's similar for many of the heuristics used: close to the decision boundary, there will often be disagreement between the report label and the image label; radiologists will just disagree - it's the nature of the beast.

One immediate thing this suggests: asking an LLM for it's confidence in a diagnosis might be misleading if what you're getting is an estimate of it's confidence in applying heuristics to reports. It may be very confident that effusion is not present after reading "small effusion", since the rule is easily applied and the words are clear; whereas in reality the process that generated the word "small" has a lot of noise and may just have easily produced the word "moderate". I will be very interested to read how everyone generated their labels at the end of this competition.

Check to see how your label extractor performs relative to the 58 provided labels. Then check how your image model performs on them. If your image model is not beating the label extractor "teacher", there is likely modeling headroom. If your image model is beating the label extractor by a large margin, the quality of the labels might be holding the image model back. There's a lot of cool things to try with the label extraction, but even the simple regex extraction shown in the public notebooks can take you pretty far.

**[5]** **k256.dev** (@k256net, Master) · 2026-08-19 09:44 UTC · ▲1 · LB rank 1946

I think the biggest bottleneck is A, although I also believe there is something in E.

We have confirmed that some of the labeled data contains cases where the label contradicts the content of the report. This suggests that even the labeled data itself may not be 100% reliable.

It is extremely difficult to know whether a given label represents the “true diagnosis” or a “false diagnosis.” The same issue may also exist in both the Public and Private Leaderboards.

In other words, some cases that are not actually correct may be treated as ground truth.

For example:
1. Filter to reports whose effusion sentence contains a small-size qualifier
   ("small", "minimal", "trace", or the equivalent in the other languages).
   You will find this group contains **both** `Effusion = 0` and `Effusion = 1`.
2. In my sample, two of these reports used the **same English template and the
   same sentence**, and got opposite labels.
3. Then look for a report stating a **moderate** effusion. At least one of those
   is labeled `Effusion = 0`, so it is not a simple
   "small = negative, moderate = positive" threshold either.

## 74. Data/Reporting Inconsistencies

- **লিংক:** https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/discussion/733491
- **লেখক:** **avg-HU** (@patrickmatulich, Contributor) · _topic-এর লেখক_ · 2026-08-07 05:03 UTC · ▲8 · LB rank 717
- **ভোট:** 8 · **কমেন্ট:** 3 · **শেষ কমেন্ট:** 2026-08-27 11:39 UTC (telingc)

The decision criteria for the competition is binary for the 12 areas. However, for scenarios like the meniscus in which it is common to have degenerative interstitial signal this is counted as 0 and 1 in the sample set. Can you clarify the grading of these findings? Is it supposed to be 0 = no tear and 1 = tear? If so, then the example dataset has errors. Secondly, I noticed in the reports there are some inconsistencies such as "Minimal reactive bone marrow edema present…related to adjacent meniscal tear…No acute fracture or bone bruise" which is somewhat contradictory. Especially since in that same dictation it says that the medial compartment cartilage is intact. 

I don't know of a scenario in which a patient has "bone marrow edema" in the setting a meniscal tear and normal cartilage in which you could factually say "no contusion"

Another report describes chondral lesion within the lateral facet of the patella which should represent chondrosis of the patellofemoral compartment, but the impression lists "lateral compartment chondromalacia" which is also factually wrong

"Lateral meniscus and medial meniscus are normal. Loss of integrity of the medial meniscus in the trunk section and changes in signal intensity consistent with horizontal tear in the posterior horn are observed. "

### কমেন্ট (3)

**[1]** **?** · 2026-08-07 05:34 UTC

_(মুছে ফেলা বা খালি পোস্ট)_

**[2]** **Po-Hao "Howard" Chen** (@javaduke95, Contributor) · _host_ · 2026-08-07 11:51 UTC · ▲4

Hello! This is an important question and useful observation.

The radiology reports in the training set are deidentified versions of the original contributor reports. They are provided as clinical text and may contain ambiguous wording, internal inconsistencies, or findings that do not map one-to-one to the challenge’s binary targets. This design reflects a common real-world setting in medical imaging where large-scale model development often relies on routinely generated clinical reports.

The sample labels were assigned from image review using the challenge annotation rubric by two independent readers with a third adjudicating disagreements. 

For meniscal tears, the target is a definite tear; intrasubstance degenerative signals that do not reach the articular surface are negative.  The same process was used for the testing set.

Similarly, marrow edema, cartilage findings, and terminology in the narrative or impression do not by themselves determine the contusion, osteoarthritis, or other challenge labels. Those labels reflect the standardized image-review criteria.  (See the [Overview discussion thread](https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/discussion/733343) near the bottom where these are defined)

A report may therefore use language that appears inconsistent with the corresponding image-based label because the original report and the image-based labels were generated through different processes.

## 76. Unmentioned finding = negative, or unknown? (weak labels from report text)

- **লিংক:** https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/discussion/737650
- **লেখক:** **Alejandro Zorrilla Bejarano** (@alejandrozobe, Contributor) · _topic-এর লেখক_ · 2026-08-26 15:41 UTC · ▲1
- **ভোট:** 1 · **কমেন্ট:** 1 · **শেষ কমেন্ট:** 2026-08-27 01:58 UTC (Amil Gentili)

Hi all,

Working on generating weak labels from the report text for the 4,349 unlabelled studies (keyword + negation approach, no LLM). One design question I haven't fully settled:

When a finding (e.g. Fracture) is never mentioned in the report at all, should the weak label be:
(a) 0 (absence) - reasoning: radiologists systematically report what they see, so silence implies absence. Under this closed-world assumption, if a report doesn't mention a finding at all, it's effectively a negative statement by omission - the same way a report doesn't need to say "no headache, shoulder not broken" for every possible unrelated finding; it only mentions what's relevant/present, or
(b) an explicit "unknown/not mentioned" state - reasoning: reports vary a lot in style/completeness across institutions, so silence might just mean the radiologist didn't comment on it.

I saw the report by another user comparing against a published LLM label set (topic 733932/734055) that used an explicit refusal code rather than forcing binary - curious if others have thoughts on which assumption holds better here, especially given how varied report length/style is across the 10+ languages in this dataset.

Any data points (even anecdotal, from manually reading a few reports) would help. Thanks!

### কমেন্ট (1)

**[1]** **Amil Gentili** (@agentili, Expert) · 2026-08-27 01:58 UTC · ▲3 · LB rank 488

As a radiologist, if I do not mention a fracture on a knee MRI, it means that I did not see a fracture. I think that most radiologist would do the same for meniscal or fligamentous tear.  For osteoarthtitis it may not be so clear cut, as there are a lot of different way to describe osteoarthritis without mentioning it directly, but by describing it (cartilage loss, chondromalasia, cartilage fissuring, cartilage thinning, osteophytes, etc)

## 77. Sharing my knee MRI

- **লিংক:** https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/discussion/736170
- **লেখক:** **tennogh** (@tennogh, Master) · _topic-এর লেখক_ · 2026-08-19 18:21 UTC · ▲20 · LB rank 8
- **ভোট:** 20 · **কমেন্ট:** 6 · **শেষ কমেন্ট:** 2026-08-26 22:14 UTC (Harsh\_2035)

After seeing [this discussion](https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/discussion/733497) by @harsh2035, I figured it would be fun to share [my knee MRI](https://www.kaggle.com/datasets/tennogh/my-knee-mri).

For context, this was taken 6 weeks after an ice climbing accident and confirmed a full ACL rupture. There are other abnormalities such as partial tears and contusion. Because of this accident I was forced to stop mountaineering for 6 months and started competing on Kaggle.

The dataset license is CC0, however the report text is written by a radiologist and I don't claim any rights to it. I have purged the data of any sensitive information and put it into the competition format.

<a id="t737597"></a>
## 79. What we've ruled out at 0.79 OOF — and what's left

- **লিংক:** https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/discussion/737597
- **লেখক:** **Berat Kirbiyik** (@beratkirbiyik, Contributor) · _topic-এর লেখক_ · 2026-08-26 08:57 UTC · ▲4 · LB rank 1241
- **ভোট:** 4 · **কমেন্ট:** 0

We're stuck at 0.7815 OOF (0.870 public) and have run a series of controlled ablations, each single-variable, fold-0 unless stated. Sharing the negatives because they seem more useful than the positives.

Setup: EfficientNet-B0, 4 sequence slots (Axial_FS, Sagittal_FS, Coronal_FS, Sagittal_T1), 9 adjacent slices at 288px from a 140mm physical crop, task-specific masked attention over slots, weighted BCE, 5-fold scanner-aware split.

What moved the needle:

192px, no crop, 24 equal-spaced slices     0.7695 OOF
288px, 140mm crop, 12 adjacent             0.7777 OOF
288px, 140mm crop, center-9 of 24          0.7815 OOF

Slice geometry mattered more than resolution: center-9 adjacent beat 9 equal-spaced across 24 by +0.018 on fold 0.

What didn't move it (fold 0, baseline 0.7914):

EMA, decay 0.999          0.7940   +0.003
Mixup, alpha 0.2          0.7937   +0.002
60 epochs, cosine         0.7922   +0.001
Asymmetric loss           0.6526   -0.139

Three different regularization approaches all land within noise of each other. That reads to me like the bottleneck isn't the training regime.

Laterality — tested and ruled out. Right and left knees are mirror images along the medial-lateral axis, so I expected the side-dependent targets to suffer without normalization. They don't:

mean |right - left| AUC gap, side-dependent targets   0.014
same for all other targets                            0.031

If anything the side-dependent targets are less affected. Also worth noting: the Laterality tag is present on 2185/4407 studies, but deriving side from the image centre in patient coordinates resolves 4274/4407 and agrees with the tag 98.4% of the time.

Where the error concentrates:

Baker's           0.855      Lateral Meniscus  0.740
ACL               0.853      PF OA             0.748
Effusion          0.843      Lateral OA        0.751
Fracture          0.832      MCL               0.758

The pattern I see: large, discrete, positionally distinctive findings work. Thin, small, graded ones don't. A Baker's cyst is a fluid sac in a fixed location. A meniscal tear is 1-3mm of signal that has to touch an articular surface on two consecutive slices.

The question: for those who are past 0.90 — is the gap in how the model attends spatially? We pool globally over the encoder output. A 3mm tear is roughly 0.01% of a 288px frame. I'm considering either an ROI crop around the joint line or spatial attention pooling instead of GAP, but I'd rather not spend a week on the wrong one.

Happy to answer questions on any of the above.

_কোনো কমেন্ট নেই।_

[↑ সূচিতে ফিরুন](#toc)

<a id="t737566"></a>
## 80. Why everyone's Synovitis AUC is stuck around 0.6-0.7 (it's not your model)

- **লিংক:** https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/discussion/737566
- **লেখক:** **starkhushi** (@starkhushi, Expert) · _topic-এর লেখক_ · 2026-08-26 04:42 UTC · ▲6 · LB rank 601
- **ভোট:** 6 · **কমেন্ট:** 0

Looking at public notebooks, Synovitis is consistently the worst of the 12 labels — mine included (0.62-0.72 across four different architectures). I dug into why, and I'm now fairly convinced it's the supervision, not the models:

1. Only ~16% of the 4,407 reports mention any synovitis term at all — I searched synovit/sinovit/υμεν/синови/sinovyal across the nine report languages. For the other ~84% of studies, whatever label table you train on has inferred the target rather than read it.

2. The label tables themselves agree with the radiologist-annotated studies at only ~0.79 AUC on Synovitis, versus ~0.99 for ACL. Your model is learning from a teacher that's barely better than a coin flip with a lean.

3. The one thing that moved it for me: an encoder pretrained on medical images (RadImageNet) hit 0.78 on Synovitis where all my ImageNet-pretrained models sat at 0.62-0.72 — suggesting the image signal exists, but natural-image features can't see it.

Practical takeaways: don't burn architecture-tuning time on Synovitis expecting parity with other labels; a medically-pretrained member in an ensemble specifically helps here; and if anyone has a labeling approach that reads Synovitis better from the reports (e.g., inferring it from effusion co-mentions), that might be worth more than any model change.

Effusion↔Synovitis correlate at ~0.5 in the annotated studies, so there's signal to borrow. Curious if anyone has tried it.

_কোনো কমেন্ট নেই।_

[↑ সূচিতে ফিরুন](#toc)

## 83.  Benchmarked the public report-label tables against the 58 annotated studies

- **লিংক:** https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/discussion/737454
- **লেখক:** **starkhushi** (@starkhushi, Expert) · _topic-এর লেখক_ · 2026-08-25 15:52 UTC · LB rank 609
- **ভোট:** 0 · **কমেন্ট:** 0

Since only 58/4,407 studies carry radiologist labels, everyone is training on report-derived tables — but I couldn't find a comparison of them, so I measured each against those 58 studies.

Macro AUC vs radiologists: stevenleehans v4 blend 0.893, stevenleehans v2 0.887, pilkwang report_labels_v2 0.870. Averaging two independent tables beats either alone on several labels.

Per-label the spread is huge — ACL ~0.99, Synovitis ~0.79 (only ~16% of reports mention synovitis in any of the nine languages, so it's largely inferred). Worth knowing before you blame your architecture for a weak Synovitis score.

Notebook with the code and per-label table: [https://www.kaggle.com/code/starkhushi/rsna-knee-which-report-labels-should-you-train-on]

Thanks to both dataset authors — all credit for the tables is theirs.

**[2.1.1]** ↳ ↳ **PC Jimmmy** (@pcjimmmy, Master) · 2026-08-20 18:40 UTC · ▲1 · LB rank 643

For sure an issue I have seen in every Medical competition over the 9 years I been on kaggle is what I think of as measurement error.  Most of them I would have always said, the winner is predicting the kaggle truth and not necessarily the real world truth.

The business I spent 45 years in used mostly humans to make classification judgments.  I spent more hours of my life than I care to think about as we attempted to improve those judgments.  It was mission impossible - the only real fix was to replace or augment those decisions with machines/vision systems, etc.

To expect that 4700 different radiology folks from all across the world to be in agreement with the limited folks who classified our 58 train and the full test would never cross my mind for sure.  My assumption and hopefully yours is that both random and systemic variation to the reports exist.

The power of what we are doing - helping make those judgments consistent is really a great thing.  Its one of the reasons that you will find me every day hollering at the folks on Facebook who want to not allow data centers to exist in their town.

**[3]** **Berat Kirbiyik** (@beratkirbiyik, Contributor) · 2026-08-24 13:24 UTC · LB rank 1241

Thanks for opening this up — the Efficiency LB really is the more informative board.

I profiled our inference pipeline and hit a wall I can't get past. Breakdown for ~1300 studies:

header pass          0.5 min
slice ordering      12.0 min   <- the wall
decode + resize      4.1 min
container + import   5.0 min
GPU (5-fold)        10.0 min

The ordering pass reads the geometry tags of every slice in every selected series (~186 files per study) to sort by the projection onto the slice normal, then keeps only 9. Things I tried that didn't work:

Threads: saturates at 64. Going to 128/256/512 changes nothing, so it's not latency-bound.
Reading only every Nth slice and interpolating: slice agreement with the full ordering drops to ~39%, no better than chance for the ones not sampled.
InstanceNumber instead of the projection: 94.5% agreement but zero speedup — the cost is opening the file, not parsing the tag.

You mentioned ~20 min total with preprocessing plus a single 5-fold model. Are you doing something fundamentally different at the ordering step, or skipping it? Genuinely curious whether the geometric sort is worth its cost at all

Could the organizers clarify what criterion the annotation team used for the Synovitis label on these non-contrast studies? Specifically, was synovitis scored through effusion-related surrogates (e.g., effusion-synovitis / capsular distension, or Hoffa-synovitis, as in MOAKS-style scoring), or only when explicit synovial thickening/hypertrophy was described or visible? Relatedly, is Effusion graded independently of Synovitis, and was there a size threshold (e.g., physiologic vs. trace vs. small)? Many radiology reports mention effusion without commenting on the synovium at all, so the intended distinction between these two labels would help everyone deriving training labels from the report text. Thanks!

- **ভোট:** 1 · **কমেন্ট:** 1 · **শেষ কমেন্ট:** 2026-08-22 08:58 UTC (Komil Parmar)

Hi, I’ve been thinking a lot about how to make the best use of the available reports.

I guess there are two main ways we could leverage them:

1. Use the reports only to extract pseudo-labels.
2. Use the reports directly for model training, e.g., by aligning image and text features.

How are you planning to make use of the reports in your approach?

### কমেন্ট (1)

**[1]** **Komil Parmar** (@komilparmar, Expert) · 2026-08-22 08:58 UTC · LB rank 59

The straight forward way to utilize the reports is definitely by simply using them as pseudo labels, when you do that, there are mainly two things to keep in mind:
1. Weight the gold labels much higher
2. If you are using multiple models, you can use their disagreement as a metric of how accurate those labels are, roughly at least, and use that again to weight those pseudo labels better. 

I don't believe we should be aligning image with the text features for various reasons, and reports are not available at test time any way so its better to stick with image only to keep the local eval clean.
