<a id="t736720"></a>
## 92. Healthcare professionals: what knee MRI resources do you use in your country or language?

- **লিংক:** https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/discussion/736720
- **লেখক:** **hida1211** (@hida1211, Contributor) · _topic-এর লেখক_ · 2026-08-22 08:16 UTC · ▲7 · LB rank 1836
- **ভোট:** 7 · **কমেন্ট:** 0

Hi everyone,

I've recently seen a few posts on X from non-medical participants who bought Knee MRI (4th ed., Japanese) for this competition.

Knee MRI, 4th edition — 
https://www.igaku-shoin.co.jp/book/detail/115127

Note: most of the books below are Japanese-only. I've included the original titles so you can search for them.

Earlier editions have been translated into English, Chinese and Korean, and the Japanese 4th edition came out this year.

I'm a radiologist and I own the 3rd edition — I read it during residency, and it's an excellent, very detailed reference. Honestly, I was impressed by how much effort people here are putting in.

The book I actually reach for in daily reporting is 下肢MRI読影の一手  3120画像からみた定跡(Lower Extremity MRI: 3,120 Images and the Standard Moves). 
https://www.nanzando.com/products/detail/22231

It has plenty of images, but what makes it useful is the format: patient information → images → "the key finding" → "the key to the diagnosis" → "comment from the referring physician." It walks you compactly through the path from image to written report. Since this competition is multimodal, that findings-to-text flow might be relevant here too.

In Japan, the journal 画像診断 (Diagnostic Imaging) is widely trusted among radiologists. If you just want an overview of knee MRI, you can buy a single issue as an e-book on isho.jp. Two I'd recommend:

Vol. 44, No. 2 (Jan 2024) — Special issue: Musculoskeletal imaging for beginners: a Q&A approach
Vol. 36, No. 7 (May 2016) — Special issue: Knee MRI you should know

For a more accessible option, there's 病気がみえる (Byouki ga Mieru), affectionately known as "Byomie." It's aimed at medical and nursing students — light on actual MRI images, but extremely rich in illustrations and quite cheap. It's a great way to cover knee anatomy and pathology comprehensively. (There was no orthopedics volume when I was a student, which made studying harder, so I bought it as soon as it was released.)

From a quick search, Magnetic resonance imaging of the knee(PMC7571514) is freely available and seems to cover most of the labels used in this competition. I'm planning to read through it myself as a refresher.

If there are other radiologists, orthopedic surgeons, radiologic technologists, or other healthcare professionals in this competition — please share the resources you use for learning knee MRI in this thread. Resources in languages other than English are especially hard for participants in other countries to find, and given the multilingual-report setting of this competition, they might turn out to be valuable (even though Japanese reports don't appear to be included in the dataset, unfortunately).

Knee anatomy and knee MRI interpretation both go surprisingly deep — I'm still learning myself. Let's dig into it together and have fun with it. Good luck, everyone!

_কোনো কমেন্ট নেই।_

<a id="t733652"></a>
## 98. Rules clarification: external knee-MRI datasets, and using an LLM API to derive labels from the reports

- **লিংক:** https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/discussion/733652
- **লেখক:** **Fernando Faria** (@fernandoadefaria, Contributor) · _topic-এর লেখক_ · 2026-08-07 18:25 UTC · ▲21 · LB rank 1307
- **ভোট:** 21 · **কমেন্ট:** 7 · **শেষ কমেন্ট:** 2026-08-20 09:27 UTC (Mazzutti)

1. Do external knee-MRI datasets with free research-use agreements count as "publicly available and equally accessible at no cost"?

The rules permit external data that is freely and publicly available to all participants. The main public knee-MRI resources — MRNet (Stanford), fastMRI+ (NYU), the Osteoarthritis Initiative, and SKM-TEA — are all free of charge, but each requires registering and accepting a research-use agreement before download. None of them charge, and none restrict access to a particular institution, but the click-through step means they aren't anonymously downloadable.

Does that satisfy the "equally accessible at no cost" condition? A yes/no on the category would be more useful than a ruling on any single dataset, since the same access pattern covers essentially every public MSK-MRI resource.

Related: does the answer change for pretrained weights released under a research-use agreement rather than a permissive licence, given the winners' obligation to open-source code and weights under CC-BY-NC 4.0?

2. Is it permitted to send the training reports to a commercial LLM API in order to derive labels from them?

The competition explicitly suggests deriving labels for the unlabeled studies from the report text, and the reports are in nine languages, so a multilingual LLM is the natural tool. But the data-security rule says competition data must not be provided to anyone who has not accepted the rules, and it isn't obvious to me whether sending report text to a third-party API for inference counts as "providing data" or simply as using a tool.

Concretely, is either of these permitted?

(a) Sending report text to a hosted LLM API (OpenAI, Anthropic, Google, etc.) during development, with the derived labels used purely as a training artifact — no report text and no API access at submission time.
(b) Same, but with an open-weights model run locally or in a Kaggle notebook, so the text never leaves the participant's own environment.
I assume (b) is fine and am mainly asking about (a). If (a) is not permitted, that's very much worth everyone knowing before they build on it — and if it is, saying so publicly puts teams without local GPU capacity on the same footing as those with it.

Thanks. Also thanks for the label-criteria post, which was unusually detailed and useful.

### কমেন্ট (7)

**[1]** **Oleksii Zhukov** (@zhukovoleksiy, Master) · 2026-08-07 20:22 UTC · ▲1 · LB rank 2850

@fernandoadefaria Good questions, and I'd like hosts answers to both.  

1. Same question here, I don't think a participant can settle it. The winners' obligation angle you raise makes it two questions: whether click-through datasets are usable at all, and whether using them complicates the CC-BY-NC grant. The rules do carve out incompatible licences on input data and pretrained models, which may cover it, but that's the host's call.

2. My reading is that (a) is not permitted. In competition rules, Section 4.b "Data Security" says participants agree not to "transmit, duplicate, publish, redistribute or otherwise provide or make available the Competition Data to any party not participating in the Competition." Obviously commercial API provider is such a party. That reading doesn't depend on what happens at submission time, since the transmission of reports data itself is the issue and violation of competition rules.

I agree (b) looks fine and that was my initial plan to generate labels: with open weights run locally or in a Kaggle notebook the text never leaves your own environment, and internet-off only applies to the submission notebook, so offline label generation during development isn't restricted. Small multilingual models are great for this task.

But this is my reading of the clause, not a ruling, and I think it's a large enough decision that a host answer would be worth having in writing. I agree it matters for fairness either way. If (a) is allowed it advantages teams who can pay for API calls; if it isn't, teams should know before they build on it to not get DQ.

**[5]** **Mazzutti** (@profmazzutti, Contributor) · 2026-08-20 09:27 UTC · LB rank 18

I pulled the actual rule text, and I think this thread (and [#735497](https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/discussion/735497), [#734109](https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/discussion/734109), [#735121](https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/discussion/735121)) may be asking about the wrong branch of 2.6(a).

2.6(a) is a disjunction: External Data must be "either publicly available and equally accessible ... at no cost to the other Participants, **or** satisfies the Reasonableness criteria as outlined in Section 2.6.b."

And 2.6.b states that "the use of external data and models is acceptable **unless specifically prohibited by the Host**," with the Reasonableness Standard aimed at excessive cost and geo restrictions.

MRNet, fastMRI/fastMRI+, OAI, SKM-TEA and KneeCoT are free, not geo-restricted, and open to any researcher after a click-through agreement. On a plain reading they clear 2.6.b even if one thinks the click-through defeats the first branch
of 2.6(a).

So the question I'd ask the Host is narrower:

**1. Has the Host specifically prohibited any public knee-MRI corpus under 2.6.b?** If not, pretraining on that category is permitted and these four threads are settled.

**2. Section 2.8(a)(1) requires winners to publish model weights as a public Kaggle dataset, and 2.8(a)(2) requires representing an "unrestricted right to grant" the winners licence.** Can weights whose training data carried a non-commercial or no-derivatives licence (CC BY-NC, CC BY-NC-ND) satisfy that representation? 2.6(a) explicitly says permission to use External Data "does not limit your other obligations ... including Section 2.8."

Question 2 is the one that actually gates decisions: permission to train and eligibility to win may differ, and teams are committing pretraining compute now against an October 22 deadline.

<a id="t735855"></a>
## 100. EDA: Only 58/4407 training studies have ground-truth labels what that means for validation

- **লিংক:** https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/discussion/735855
- **লেখক:** **Istiyak Amin Santo** (@istiyakaminsanto, Contributor) · _topic-এর লেখক_ · 2026-08-18 13:15 UTC · ▲1 · LB rank 3209
- **ভোট:** 1 · **কমেন্ট:** 1 · **শেষ কমেন্ট:** 2026-08-19 20:52 UTC (JeremyAM)

Quick EDA that's worth knowing before you build a validation split.

**Label coverage:** `train.csv` has 4,407 rows, but only **58 (1.3%)** have all 12 label columns filled in. The other 4,349 have `Report` text but empty labels. If you carve a validation set from `train.csv` naively, you'll likely end up with very few (or zero) labeled examples in it — worth explicitly filtering to the 58 labeled rows for any clean eval.

**Label prevalence among the 58 labeled studies:**
| Label | Positive rate |
|---|---|
| Effusion | 60% |
| Synovitis | 47% |
| Medial Meniscus | 45% |
| ACL | 41% |
| Lateral Meniscus | 40% |
| PF OA | 36% |
| Contusion | 33% |
| Fracture | 31% |
| Medial OA | 26% |
| Baker's | 21% |
| Lateral OA | 19% |
| MCL | 16% |

**Reports are genuinely multilingual** confirmed at least English, Spanish, Greek, Turkish, Dutch, Bulgarian, Croatian, and German in a sample. If you're doing any NLP on `Report`, don't assume English.

**Series structure** (`train_series.csv`): median 5 series/study (range 3–14), split across Sagittal/Coronal/Axial planes. `Fluid_Sensitive` and `Fat_Suppression` are perfectly correlated in this dataset  only 6 distinct (plane, sequence-type) combos actually occur, despite the data description hedging that they're not always equivalent.

**DICOM notes:** image dimensions vary a lot even within one study (I've seen 640×640 up to 960×960, plus at least one non-square 640×1280), and transfer syntax is mixed (some uncompressed, some presumably JPEG-family per the data page)  resize and don't assume a fixed input shape.

Curious what others are finding on the label side anyone had luck deriving usable labels from the report text for the unlabeled majority?

<a id="t736098"></a>
## 101. How I Transferred the RSNA Dataset from Kaggle to Google Drive

- **লিংক:** https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/discussion/736098
- **লেখক:** **Y.** (@xiongdaxiongmao, Contributor) · _topic-এর লেখক_ · 2026-08-19 14:12 UTC · ▲3
- **ভোট:** 3 · **কমেন্ট:** 0

The RSNA dataset was too large to download through Colab or my local PC due to disk space limitations.

Instead, I used a Kaggle Notebook + rclone workflow. I connected rclone to Google Drive using OAuth, stored the authentication information securely in Kaggle Secrets, and then transferred the dataset directly from Kaggle’s /kaggle/input storage to Google Drive.

The overall workflow was:

Kaggle Dataset → Kaggle Notebook → rclone → Google Drive

I first tested the transfer with a small file and a single study, then started the full dataset transfer. This approach avoids storing the entire dataset on Colab or a local machine and also allows the transfer to be resumed if the Kaggle session stops.

_কোনো কমেন্ট নেই।_

<a id="t735854"></a>
## 102. \[PSA\] 3 code-submission gotchas: GPU incompatibility, missing DICOM codecs, input path quirks

- **লিংক:** https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/discussion/735854
- **লেখক:** **Istiyak Amin Santo** (@istiyakaminsanto, Contributor) · _topic-এর লেখক_ · 2026-08-18 13:04 UTC · ▲1 · LB rank 3209
- **ভোট:** 1 · **কমেন্ট:** 1 · **শেষ কমেন্ট:** 2026-08-18 14:24 UTC (PC Jimmmy)

Saving others some debugging time, three environment issues that silently break a submission:

**1. Default GPU (P100) is incompatible with pre-installed PyTorch**
PyTorch 2.10.0+cu128 needs compute capability sm_70+; P100 is sm_60. `cuda.is_available()` still returns `True`, so it fails silently. Fix in `kernel-metadata.json`: 

```"machine_shape": "NvidiaTeslaT4"```

**2. DICOM codec plugins (pylibjpeg/gdcm) aren't installed**, and scored runs have no internet, so you can't pip install them then. This dataset has JPEG Lossless/JPEG2000 series that need them.
Fix — download the matching wheels locally, upload as a Dataset, install offline in-kernel:

```
pip download --only-binary=:all: --platform manylinux2014_x86_64 --python-version 312 \
  --no-deps -d ./wheels pylibjpeg pylibjpeg-libjpeg pylibjpeg-openjpeg python-gdcm

subprocess.run([sys.executable, "-m", "pip", "install", "--no-index",
                 "--find-links", wheel_dir, "pylibjpeg", "pylibjpeg-libjpeg",
                 "pylibjpeg-openjpeg", "python-gdcm"], check=True)
```

 **3.`/kaggle/input/` paths aren't what you'd expect competition data** is under `/kaggle/input/competitions/<slug>/`, and attached Datasets can land at `/kaggle/input/datasets/<owner>/<dataset>/` rather than the flat path. Don't hardcode discover at runtime with a depth-bounded os.walk (unbounded recursion will crawl all of train_series/ looking for a shallow file).

**Happy to answer questions if others hit the same walls.**
```

### কমেন্ট (1)

**[1]** **PC Jimmmy** (@pcjimmmy, Master) · 2026-08-18 14:24 UTC · LB rank 642

<a id="t735154"></a>
## 104. Scaling the encoder bought us nothing (+0.0011)

- **লিংক:** https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/discussion/735154
- **লেখক:** **stevenleehans** (@stevenleehans, Expert) · _topic-এর লেখক_ · 2026-08-14 08:05 UTC · ▲14 · LB rank 1902
- **ভোট:** 14 · **কমেন্ট:** 3 · **শেষ কমেন্ট:** 2026-08-17 14:45 UTC (Dung Duong (Tony) Huynh)

##### TL;DR

- Swapping **DINOv2-Small → DINOv2-Base** (22M → 87M params, 3.9× the compute per step)
  moved our CV by **+0.0011** against a measured noise floor of **0.0020**. A null.
- Only **5 of 12** labels moved in Base's favour, and the whole macro gain rests on our
  weakest label. Capacity is not what's binding here.
- Persisting the decoded pixels turns the ~500 GB of DICOM into an **11.12 GiB** array,
  which fits on a laptop. A fold now runs in **67 min on an M4 Pro at zero GPU quota**,
  versus 76 min on a Kaggle T4.
- The one genuinely transferable lesson: **a checkpoint carries its own preprocessing
  contract.** Swapping encoder weights without swapping the normalisation constants is a
  silent handicap that produces a plausible, wrong result.

You can see the process of turning the data here. feel free to fork :) 
https://www.kaggle.com/code/stevenleehans/rsna-knee-500gb-to-11gib-cpu-pixel-cache

---

##### 1. Why the 500 GB isn't really 500 GB

The dataset is large, but the model never sees most of it. After slice selection,
windowing, the physical-scale crop, and the resize to 224 px, the entire visual input to
our model is:

```
4,407 studies × 6 slots × 9 slices × 224 × 224, uint8  =  11.12 GiB
```

That's it. Everything else is decode work that gets thrown away when the kernel ends —
and then paid again on the next run.

We measured that decode at **55 minutes per run**, which was **72% of a single fold**.
Writing the array to disk once (sharded `.npy`, memory-mapped on load) removes it
permanently.

**This has a side effect worth more than the speed:** 11 GiB fits on a laptop.

| | Kaggle T4 (fp16) | Apple M4 Pro (fp32) |
|---|---|---|
| per training step | 0.227 s | 0.436 s |
| cache build | 55 min, every run | 0 |
| **one fold, wall clock** | **76 min** | **67 min** |
| GPU quota consumed | 21 min | **0** |

The laptop is 1.9× slower per step and still finishes sooner, because it never rebuilds
the cache. Our weekly GPU quota had been exhausted for five days when we ran these.

**⚠ Caveat we can't resolve:** the same config scored **0.7931** locally vs **0.7957** on
Kaggle — Δ = −0.0026, just outside our floor. We can't separate fp32-vs-fp16 numerics from
a small real deficit. So **every arm below is compared to a local control**, never
cross-machine. If you try this, do the same.

---

##### 2. The experiment: does a bigger encoder help?

Every experiment we'd run to that point changed how the **pictures** were prepared —
crop geometry, slice position, sampling window. None had changed the encoder itself.

So: identical data, identical seed, identical folds, identical batch size. Only ViT-S → ViT-B.

| Encoder | Params | Trainable | s/step | Derived OOF (2,652 studies) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| dinov2-small *(control)* | 22M | 10.7M | 0.436 | **0.7931** |
| dinov2-base | 87M | 42.5M | 1.710 | **0.7942** |

**Δ = +0.0011.** Our paired noise floor at 3 folds is **0.0020**. So this is inside the
noise — 4× the parameters for nothing measurable.

###### The per-label view is worse than the average

| Target | n pos | small | base | Δ |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| MCL | 342 | 0.7529 | 0.7729 | **+0.0200** |
| Lateral OA | 401 | 0.7603 | 0.7708 | +0.0105 |
| Lateral Meniscus | 485 | 0.7385 | 0.7439 | +0.0054 |
| PF OA | 796 | 0.7423 | 0.7462 | +0.0039 |
| Medial Meniscus | 1073 | 0.7726 | 0.7761 | +0.0035 |
| Effusion | 1457 | 0.8106 | 0.8105 | −0.0001 |
| Medial OA | 525 | 0.8121 | 0.8109 | −0.0012 |
| Synovitis | 881 | 0.8206 | 0.8191 | −0.0015 |
| ACL | 573 | 0.7968 | 0.7942 | −0.0026 |
| Baker's cyst | 637 | 0.8614 | 0.8582 | −0.0032 |
| Contusion | 613 | 0.8010 | 0.7935 | −0.0075 |
| Fracture | 177 | 0.8484 | 0.8341 | −0.0143 |

**Only 5 of 12 labels move in Base's favour.** For comparison, when a crop-geometry fix
earlier in our log paid +0.0059, it moved **10 of 12** — that's what a real effect looks
like. Here the entire macro gain rests on MCL, which has only **9 expert positives** in the
58 labelled studies, i.e. our least reliable label.

No label clears our per-label gate of 0.03. This is the shape of noise, not of capacity.

---

##### 3. The bug: a checkpoint carries its own preprocessing contract

The next arm was **RAD-DINO** (`microsoft/rad-dino`) — a ViT-B/14 finetuned from
`dinov2-base` on 882,775 chest radiographs. Since capacity is now priced at zero, and
RAD-DINO is architecturally identical to `dinov2-base` (same 87M params, same 12 blocks,
same patch 14), a gain could only be attributed to **what it was pretrained on**. Clean
single-lever test.

It started losing immediately and the gap widened every epoch:

| fold 0 epoch | small | base | rad-dino |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 0.6997 | 0.6984 | 0.6820 |
| 3 | 0.7399 | 0.7292 | 0.7226 |
| 5 | 0.7666 | 0.7695 | **0.7352** |

Training loss was also *higher* (0.3531 vs 0.3461), so it was fitting worse, not overfitting.

The obvious reading is "medical pretraining doesn't transfer to MRI". **The actual reason
was in a config file:**

| checkpoint | `image_mean` | `image_std` | native px |
|---|---|---|---|
| dinov2-small / base | 0.485, 0.456, 0.406 | 0.229, 0.224, 0.225 | 224 |
| **rad-dino** | **0.5307** (greyscale) | **0.2583** | **518** |

Our model hardcoded the ImageNet constants as buffers:

```python
self.register_buffer("mean", torch.tensor([0.485, 0.456, 0.406]).view(1, 3, 1, 1))
self.register_buffer("std",  torch.tensor([0.229, 0.224, 0.225]).view(1, 3, 1, 1))
```

Correct for DINOv2. **Wrong for RAD-DINO**, which was pretrained on greyscale radiographs —
so it was receiving inputs shifted ~0.2 std from its pretraining distribution with a 12%
scale error on top.

The fix is to read it from the checkpoint instead of assuming it:

```python
cfg = json.loads((ckpt_dir / "preprocessor_config.json").read_text())
mean, std = cfg.get("image_mean"), cfg.get("image_std")
```

We verified both DINOv2 checkpoints resolve to byte-identical ImageNet constants
afterwards, so the results in §2 were unaffected and didn't need re-running.

**Why this is the most useful thing in this post:** left to finish, that run would have
produced a real number, from a real 11-hour experiment, supporting a false conclusion about
the exact hypothesis we most wanted to test. It fails *silently* — no error, no warning,
just a plausible curve pointing the wrong way. If you're swapping encoders in this
competition (and plenty of us are), check `preprocessor_config.json` before you trust the
comparison.

---

##### 4. Four other ways our measurements lied

Offered because they're all cheap to hit:

1. **The silent backbone fallback.** Off Kaggle there's no `/kaggle/input`, so our weights
   lookup returned `None` and the builder fell back to **ResNet-18** — which trains fine and
   logs a plausible score. Any run that doesn't *print* which backbone it loaded is not
   evidence about that backbone.

2. **The narrow probe.** Timing training steps predicted 0.64 h per fold; the fold took
   **1.11 h**, because the probe ignored validation, memory-mapped reads and augmentation.
   That was our third consecutive underestimate from a narrow probe. A probe that times one
   part of a loop does not time the loop — treat it as go/no-go, never as a schedule.

3. **Non-deterministic hardware.** On Apple's MPS backend, identical config *and* identical
   seed agreed to four decimals for two epochs and then diverged. A noise floor measured on
   one backend does not transfer to another. Measure it where you run.

4. **A tool that answered the wrong question.** Our comparison script prints its absolute
   difference under the heading `NOISE FLOOR`, because it was written to compare two seeds
   of one config. Fed a control and a treatment, it labels the **effect** as the floor —
   it reported "floor = 0.0011" for a result that sits *under* the real floor of 0.0020.

---

##### 5. Where this leaves us

The encoder's **size** is not the binding constraint on this problem. That's consistent
with everything else we've measured: our gains came from crop geometry and slice position —
from how the images are prepared, not from how much model is pointed at them.

The RAD-DINO arm is re-running with correct normalisation as of writing; we'll post the
number either way, including if it's a null.

**One limitation we'll state up front so we don't overclaim later:** RAD-DINO's native
input is 518 px and we feed it 224, so it sees 16×16 patches instead of 37×37 with
interpolated position embeddings. That confound isn't cheap to fix (518 px is ~5× the
compute, and 336 px already scored nothing on our pipeline). So whatever we get, the honest
claim is about RAD-DINO **at 224 px**, not RAD-DINO in general.

---

###### Notes on validation

All figures are out-of-fold on report-derived targets, 3 folds, 2,652 studies, paired
(same studies, same seed, same folds). Noise floor of 0.0020 macro was measured, not
assumed — same config, different training seed. Only 58 of 4,407 studies carry expert
labels, so that subset is a sanity check and cannot rank two models; a per-label standard
error there is 0.04–0.09.

Nothing above is a leaderboard claim. Happy to answer questions or be told we've got
something wrong — particularly if anyone has run a ViT-B encoder here and seen it pay.

### কমেন্ট (3)

**[1]** **Dung Duong (Tony) Huynh** (@dungduongtonyhuynh, Contributor) · 2026-08-16 07:58 UTC

Hi OG, thanks for sharing this! May I ask how you reduced each series to 9 slices? Did you randomly sample the slices, or use interpolation? Thank you!

**[1.1]** ↳ **stevenleehans** (@stevenleehans, Expert) · _topic-এর লেখক_ · 2026-08-16 08:59 UTC · ▲1 · LB rank 1902

Hi Tony — neither, actually. No random sampling and no interpolation; it's a deterministic pick of 9 real slices, in three steps.

1. Sort the stack physically first. This one matters more than the sampling. The filenames in this dataset are SOP Instance UIDs, i.e. random — sorted filename order matches true anatomical order only about 5% of the time on this corpus. So before anything else I re-sort each series by projecting ImagePositionPatient onto the normal of ImageOrientationPatient, with fallbacks to SliceLocation → InstanceNumber → filename. That resolves by geometry on 100% of series here.

2. Place 3 anchors across a window of the sorted stack, evenly spaced with linspace (not random). I clip the ends rather than use the full stack, since the outermost slices are mostly off-joint.

3. Take the 3 physically adjacent slices around each anchor — so 3 × 3 = 9.

The reason for the 3+3+3 layout rather than 9 evenly spread slices: each group of 3 becomes the R/G/B channels of one encoder input. Adjacent slices make that triplet a genuine ~10 mm depth neighbourhood at this dataset's slice gaps. Nine evenly spread slices would cover more of the joint but hand the encoder three views ~20 mm apart, which is a slab, not a 2.5D triplet.

Two caveats worth knowing:

Interpolation isn't used because MRI slice gaps here vary a lot; I'd rather feed real slices and fix scale in-plane instead (I crop to a fixed physical extent in mm before resizing, since field of view ranges over 71 distinct values with a median of 160 mm — so a fixed pixel size alone does not fix physical scale).
On my CV, 3 slices actually beat 9 by a margin ~4× my noise floor. But that result confounds two things — with one anchor the code takes the window centre, with three it spreads them to the window edges — so I can't yet tell you whether it's the slice count or the slice position doing the work. Don't take 9 as tuned.

**[1.1.1]** ↳ ↳ **Dung Duong (Tony) Huynh** (@dungduongtonyhuynh, Contributor) · 2026-08-17 14:45 UTC

<a id="t734055"></a>
## 106. train.csv has 4,407 studies and 58 labels — the other 4,349 have reports

- **লিংক:** https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/discussion/734055
- **লেখক:** **maximo lorenzo y losada** (@maximolorenzoylosada, Contributor) · _topic-এর লেখক_ · 2026-08-09 17:09 UTC · ▲3 · LB rank 2604
- **ভোট:** 3 · **কমেন্ট:** 3 · **শেষ কমেন্ট:** 2026-08-15 08:36 UTC (maximo lorenzo y losada)

Counting what is actually in train.csv turned up something I had not expected, so flagging it early.

Of 4,407 studies, 58 carry labels. The other 4,349 have every label column empty.

It is all-or-nothing per study: the 58 labelled studies have all twelve findings filled, and the rest have none. There is no partial labelling.

Every one of the 4,407 studies has a Report, including all 4,349 unlabelled ones. So the diagnostic information is present for the whole training set, just as prose rather than in the label columns.

Two parsing traps in the same file that cost me a wrong answer first time round:

1. The file is 58,556 lines but only 4,407 rows. Reports contain newlines, so wc -l overstates the dataset by about 13x. Use a real CSV reader.
2. Empty is not zero. The unlabelled cells are empty strings, not 0. Filling them with 0 invents roughly 4,349 unasserted negatives per class — my first pass did exactly that and produced a nonsense prevalence of 0.5% per finding.

Prevalence within the 58 labelled studies, which is emphatically not population prevalence — it looks like a deliberately enriched annotation set:

Effusion 60.3%, Synovitis 46.6%, Medial Meniscus 44.8%, ACL 41.4%, Lateral Meniscus 39.7%, PF OA 36.2%, Contusion 32.8%, Fracture 31.0%, Medial OA 25.9%, Baker's 20.7%, Lateral OA 19.0%, MCL 15.5%

Mean 4.1 findings per labelled study, max 9 — heavily multi-label and co-occurring.

One more thing worth knowing before you write a text pipeline: the reports are not all in one language. A crude keyword heuristic over all 4,407 gives roughly 51% English-leaning, 16% Spanish-leaning, 34% ambiguous — and the very first report in the file is Spanish ("Técnica: RMN de la rodilla. Resultados: Rotura de menisco interno..."). That is a keyword count and not language ID, so treat the split as indicative, but assuming English throughout is clearly unsafe.

With 58 labelled studies across 12 correlated findings there is not much room to train or validate a twelve-class imaging model directly — a held-out split of 58 gives single-digit positives for the rarer findings.

Notebook with all of it computed in-cell:
https://www.kaggle.com/code/maximolorenzoylosada/4407-studies-and-58-labels

Deliberately just a count, no method. If I have misparsed something I would like to be told — particularly if the empty cells are meant to be read as negatives rather than unknowns, since that changes the problem completely.

### কমেন্ট (3)

**[1]** **maximo lorenzo y losada** (@maximolorenzoylosada, Contributor) · _topic-এর লেখক_ · 2026-08-09 18:11 UTC · ▲-1 · LB rank 2604

Correction to my own post above, and credit where it is due.

I said the reports were roughly 51% English-leaning, 16% Spanish-leaning and 34% ambiguous. That was a keyword heuristic testing only English and Spanish, so it was structurally incapable of identifying anything else, and the "ambiguous" third was hiding real languages rather than being genuinely undecidable.

What tipped me off was the notebook "RSNA Knee: read the report, then the knee" by prvsiyan, which mentions Greek reports and a codepoint problem with mu. I had not considered Greek at all.

So I redid it by Unicode script rather than keywords. Over all 4,407 reports, and a report can contain more than one script:

Latin 4,301 (97.6%)
Greek 321 (7.3%)
Cyrillic 220 (5.0%)

The Greek is genuine prose, not stray symbols: 321 of 321 contain three or more consecutive Greek letters. One opening, for illustration:

MRI ΑΡΙΣΤΕΡΟΥ ΓΟΝΑΤΟΣ ΤΕΧΝΙΚΗ Η εξέταση έγινε με ακολουθίες παλμών

So this is at least a multi-script corpus: Latin covering English and Spanish at minimum, plus Greek and Cyrillic. Roughly 12% of reports contain non-Latin script.

Why this matters more than a tidier statistic: a keyword or regex pipeline built on English or Spanish terms does not fail loudly on those reports. It extracts nothing and returns a confident negative for every finding. Same shape as the empty-versus-zero trap in my original post, a silent wrong answer rather than an error.

On the mu point in that notebook, worth checking whether your text uses U+03BC GREEK SMALL LETTER MU or U+00B5 MICRO SIGN. They render identically and do not match each other.

One caveat on my own numbers: this is a script check, not language identification. Latin covers English, Spanish and anything else in that alphabet, so treat 97.6% as an upper bound on what I have actually accounted for rather than a solved question.

**[1.1]** ↳ **OpPrime** (@farshidamira, Contributor) · 2026-08-14 14:37 UTC · LB rank 151

Hi, I spent a bit of time on this topic also till I found this post: [link to LLM work to label studies](https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/discussion/733932)

**[1.1.1]** ↳ ↳ **maximo lorenzo y losada** (@maximolorenzoylosada, Contributor) · _topic-এর লেখক_ · 2026-08-15 08:36 UTC · LB rank 2604

Thanks for the pointer — that post is the most useful thing in this forum, and it let me test something my post above implied and never checked.

I warned that a text pipeline built on Latin-script terms fails silently on the Greek and Cyrillic reports: it extracts nothing and returns a confident negative rather than an error. stevenleehans' labeller has an explicit "the report does not address this" answer, published as 0.5, which makes that worry measurable for the first time. If it were quietly failing on the non-Latin reports, its refusals should pile up there.

They don't. It goes the other way, and I think that is worth having on the record.

Setup: joined llm_labels_full.csv to train.csv, 4,407 of 4,407 studies matched. Both source numbers reproduce first — 13,438 of 52,884 cells sit at exactly 0.5, which is the 25.4% reported, and the script counts come back at Greek 321 and Cyrillic 220, for 541 studies containing non-Latin script (12.3%).

Refusal rate on Latin-only reports: 25.9% of cells. On reports containing Greek or Cyrillic: 21.9%. Every study carries all twelve finding cells, so the finding mix is identical across the two groups by construction and the rates compare directly.

That is a ratio of 0.845 and a gap of 4.0 points. Shuffling the script label across the 4,407 studies 2,000 times gives a null sd of 0.96 points, and no shuffle reached the observed gap, so p < 0.0005.

Split out: Cyrillic 17.5%, Greek 24.9%.

It is not a length artefact, and length runs against the result rather than for it. The non-Latin reports are slightly shorter — median 932 characters against 988 — and a shorter report gives a labeller less to work with, not more.

Here is the part I would not want taken the wrong way. A lower refusal rate is not evidence of a correct read. A labeller that reads Greek fluently and one that hallucinates fluently produce the identical signature here: fewer refusals. Refusal rate measures willingness to answer, not accuracy.

And the accuracy question is not currently answerable. Of the 58 gold studies, 6 contain non-Latin script. That is the entire ruler available for it.

So the claim I would actually make: the specific failure mode I flagged — a pipeline going quiet on non-Latin reports — does not appear in this label set, and to that extent my post above was warning about a hazard this approach avoids. Whether the answers it gives on those 541 studies are right is a separate question, and 6 gold studies cannot settle it.

One thing in the per-finding split may matter more than the headline. Ten of the twelve findings are refused less often on non-Latin reports, but two move the other way: Synovitis +8.5 points (82.7% to 91.1%) and Baker's +7.8 (47.3% to 55.1%). Those are already the two most-refused columns, and section 4 of that post turns on how differently they behave when the report is silent. If the silence rate itself shifts by script, the silence ratio that section leans on is being measured across a mixed population — worth a look, since that section is the part of the post I would least want resting on an artefact.

To reproduce: classify a report as containing a script if it has three or more consecutive letters of it (the same rule as my correction above), then take the fraction of the twelve label cells equal to exactly 0.5 within each group. It is about ten lines.

A correction to my own numbers while I am here: my Latin count comes out 4,266 here against the 4,301 I posted on 08-09, a 35-study difference down to the exact codepoint range used for "Latin". Greek and Cyrillic are unchanged, and nothing above depends on the Latin count.

Adding a concrete reason this matters beyond curiosity.

The answer changes how folds should be built. If entire sites are held out for the private set, then site-grouped cross-validation is measuring the right thing and the OOF score it costs is worth paying. If both splits are stratified across all 16 sites, that cost buys nothing, and a random-fold OOF is the better guide.

Those two designs pull in opposite directions, so without an answer everyone is choosing one and hoping. A yes/no on the category, as originally asked, would settle it without revealing anything about which studies are where.

<a id="t734106"></a>
## 112. 58 labelled studies out of 4,407 — the rest of the supervision is in the reports

- **লিংক:** https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/discussion/734106
- **লেখক:** **Luka Duvanov** (@nekkon, Contributor) · _topic-এর লেখক_ · 2026-08-09 22:26 UTC · ▲7 · LB rank 4183
- **ভোট:** 7 · **কমেন্ট:** 3 · **শেষ কমেন্ট:** 2026-08-12 18:03 UTC (Luka Duvanov)

Ground facts I wanted before starting, in case they save someone else the time:

Exactly 58 studies are labelled, and all twelve columns are filled for the same 58. There is no partial labelling to exploit.
The reports are in seven languages — English, Turkish, Spanish, German, Greek (178 of them, in Greek script), Dutch, French — plus 428 a stopword detector cannot place. Turkish negates after the term (efüzyon izlenmedi), so a left-only negation window silently inverts the second-largest language in the corpus.
Fluid_Sensitive and Fat_Suppression are the same column, identical on all 24,371 series. Encode one, drop the other.
Every study has all three planes and both contrast types — no fallback path needed for a fixed-shape input.
A plain multilingual keyword matcher, vocabulary not tuned on the 58, gets 0.86 on Baker's cyst and 0.66 on effusion. That is the floor to beat.
The 58 are not a random sample: ACL prevalence is 41% there against ~20% in the corpus. Do not set priors from them.
https://www.kaggle.com/code/nekkon/knee-mri-58-labels-for-4-407-studies

### কমেন্ট (3)

**[1]** **Navneet** (@navneetbende, Contributor) · 2026-08-10 00:57 UTC

Thank you for the supervision @nekkon

**[2]** **Malav D Modi** (@malavdmodi, Contributor) · 2026-08-12 04:07 UTC · ▲1

I need to ask that my approach i thought was to convert the report to identify labels for rest of report but it cannot happen due to large computation hours of report conversion into english and then into labels. What else approach i can use to convert my report into labels given in the csv file ?

**[2.1]** ↳ **Luka Duvanov** (@nekkon, Contributor) · _topic-এর লেখক_ · 2026-08-12 18:03 UTC · ▲2 · LB rank 4183

You don't need to translate the reports. Translate the vocabulary.

The cost you're hitting is O(corpus): 4,407 reports through a seq2seq model.
What you need is O(vocabulary): roughly 40-60 terms per finding times 7
languages. That's a few hundred strings, translated once, and after that it's
plain string matching over the corpus — seconds on CPU, no GPU hours.

Radiology reports are formulaic, which is what makes this work. "Effusion"
shows up as effusion / efüzyon / derrame / Erguss / συλλογή / effusie /
épanchement plus a few morphological variants each. Build that table per
finding, then match.

Two things that will cost you more than the model choice if you skip them:

1. Negation direction is language-specific. Turkish negates after the term —
   "efüzyon izlenmedi" means NO effusion. A left-only negation window (the
   default in every English NegEx port) silently inverts the second-largest
   language in this corpus. Right-side window for Turkish, left for the
   Germanic and Romance ones.

2. Detect the language first, then apply that language's lexicon and negation
   rule. Stopword-frequency ID is enough and costs nothing. About 428 reports
   won't resolve — those are worth looking at rather than guessing at.

For calibration: a matcher built this way, with the vocabulary written without
looking at the 58, gets 0.86 on Baker's cyst and 0.66 on effusion. That's the
floor, and it runs in minutes.

If you do want a model, skip translation and go multilingual directly —
sentence embeddings from LaBSE or multilingual-e5 are one forward pass per
sentence and stay comparable across all seven languages. But don't fit
thresholds on the 58: ACL prevalence there is 41% against ~20% in the corpus,
so anything calibrated on them will be biased

<a id="t734117"></a>
## 121. Weak labels for all 12 findings + how recoverable each one actually is

- **লিংক:** https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/discussion/734117
- **লেখক:** **Luka Duvanov** (@nekkon, Contributor) · _topic-এর লেখক_ · 2026-08-10 01:10 UTC · ▲1 · LB rank 4183
- **ভোট:** 1 · **কমেন্ট:** 0

Follow-up to the earlier notebook. This one extracts all twelve findings from all 4,407 reports and writes weak_labels.csv as notebook output, so you can attach it directly.

The more useful half is the per-finding measurement on the 58 gold studies. Balanced accuracy ranges from 0.82 (Baker's cyst) to 0.56 (medial meniscus), and the ordering is not random — it tracks what kind of thing the finding is:

Named objects (Baker's cyst, ACL) work: present or absent, one word per language.
Graded severities (effusion) fail: "minimal joint fluid", "trace effusion" — a binary keyword has nowhere to put the adjective. Getting effusion right means learning the annotators' severity threshold, which is a modelling problem, not a vocabulary one.
Unstated inferences (fracture) fail hardest: 0.93 specificity but 0.44 sensitivity, because more than half are described by appearance without the word ever appearing.
Also worth knowing: the extractor finds 2.6 findings per study where the annotators recorded 4.1, and returns nothing at all for 23% of studies. Treat those as unlabelled rather than as all-negative.

Vocabulary corrections are the most useful thing anyone can leave in the comments, especially for Turkish and Greek.

https://www.kaggle.com/code/nekkon/weak-labels-for-all-12-knee-mri-findings

_কোনো কমেন্ট নেই।_

<a id="t734105"></a>
## 122. Strong-pipeline replication: SOFT wins 3/3 seeds (+0.014 AUC), but the 58-study CI crosses zero

- **লিংক:** https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/discussion/734105
- **লেখক:** **FHZ982** (@fhz982, Contributor) · _topic-এর লেখক_ · 2026-08-09 22:09 UTC · ▲2
- **ভোট:** 2 · **কমেন্ট:** 0

**Update after a stronger, pre-specified replication.** The original version of this post reported a large advantage for graded report-derived targets in a modest ResNet-18 pipeline. We have now repeated the core HARD-vs-SOFT comparison with a DINOv2 backbone, three paired seeds, ten epochs, checkpoint selection separated from evaluation, and the 58 official studies kept out of training and model selection.

The result is more interesting, and less tidy, than the original headline:

- SOFT beats HARD on the 58 official studies in all three paired seeds.
- The rank-mean ensemble improves by **+0.0143 macro AUC**.
- Its paired 95% bootstrap interval is **[-0.0041, +0.0330]**: it crosses zero and does not fit inside our pre-specified equivalence margin of +/-0.01.
- On an independent 430-study evaluation set scored against the *binarized lexicon target*, HARD wins by **0.0132** with a 95% interval of **[0.0059, 0.0207]** in its favour.
- Our proposed mechanism does not survive its pre-specified check: Spearman correlation between the signal destroyed by binarization and the per-finding SOFT improvement is **-0.161**.

So the updated conclusion is deliberately narrower: **SOFT transfers better to the 58 official labels in this run, consistently across seeds, but 58 studies do not establish superiority at 95%. HARD is better at reproducing the binarized lexicon target. We cannot claim that retained target nuance explains the official-label pattern.**

That is the result. The rest of the post documents exactly how we got there, what changed from the first experiment, and what the leaderboard comparison still needs to resolve.

##### 1. The comparison

Only 58 of the 4,407 training studies carry per-condition labels, so most pipelines derive targets for the other 4,349 studies from the `Report` column. The choice tested here is simple:

| arm | affirmed mention | hedged mention | explicitly negated | not mentioned |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| HARD | 1 | 1 | 0 | 0 |
| SOFT | 1 | 0.3-0.8 | 0.04-0.16 | 0.28 |

Both arms use the same deterministic multilingual lexicon, studies, image preprocessing, architecture, loss weights, optimizer, schedule, augmentations and seeds. Only the target tensor changes.

The stronger replication used:

- DINOv2, 12 blocks, last six trainable (10.7M trainable parameters), feature dimension 768;
- ten epochs for every run;
- paired seeds 11, 22 and 33;
- interleaved order: SOFT-11, HARD-11, SOFT-22, HARD-22, SOFT-33, HARD-33;
- 3,479 studies for training;
- `va_sel`: 440 studies used only to choose the epoch;
- `va_ev`: 430 different studies opened only after weights were frozen;
- 58 official studies held outside training and checkpoint selection.

Duplicate report texts remain grouped. The second holdout split uses an independent salted hash, so `va_sel` and `va_ev` are disjoint without breaking report groups.

##### 2. Official 58-study result

The selected checkpoints produced:

| seed | SOFT `va_sel` | HARD `va_sel` | SOFT AUC-58 | HARD AUC-58 | SOFT-HARD |
|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| 11 | 0.7853 | 0.7921 | 0.7943 | 0.7840 | +0.0104 |
| 22 | 0.7781 | 0.7912 | 0.7965 | 0.7881 | +0.0083 |
| 33 | 0.7745 | 0.7897 | 0.7959 | 0.7880 | +0.0079 |

Across seeds:

- SOFT: **0.7956 +/- 0.0009**
- HARD: **0.7867 +/- 0.0019**
- paired difference: **+0.0089 +/- 0.0013**
- signs: **3/3 for SOFT**

Three seeds are a stability check, not a sample size. They share training data and evaluation patients; treating them as independent observations would be pseudoreplication. Even the most favourable exact paired sign test is only p=0.125 one-sided.

For the pre-specified primary comparison we rank-averaged the three members of each arm, because AUC reads order rather than calibration. A 4,000-resample paired bootstrap over the same 58 patients gave:

| ensemble | macro AUC | 95% bootstrap interval |
|---|---:|---:|
| HARD rank-mean | 0.7930 | [0.7474, 0.8368] |
| SOFT rank-mean | 0.8071 | [0.7627, 0.8503] |
| SOFT-HARD | **+0.0143** | **[-0.0041, +0.0330]** |

SOFT wins in 93.6% of bootstrap resamples. That is consistent directional evidence, but it is **not** a 95% confirmation. The interval crosses zero. It also does not fit entirely inside the pre-specified practical-equivalence margin of +/-0.01, so it is not evidence of equivalence either. The correct reading is **inconclusive**.

This distinction matters. "The interval crosses zero" means that the experiment has not resolved the effect; it does not mean that the two target schemes are interchangeable.

##### 3. Independent evaluation against the lexicon surrogate

The 430-study `va_ev` set was never used to choose an epoch. Both frozen ensembles were scored against the same binarized lexicon labels:

| ensemble | macro AUC on `va_ev` |
|---|---:|
| HARD rank-mean | 0.8059 |
| SOFT rank-mean | 0.7927 |
| SOFT-HARD | **-0.0132** |

Paired 95% interval: **[-0.0207, -0.0059]**. The interval excludes zero, so HARD has a demonstrated advantage on this endpoint.

The scope is crucial. This endpoint is not a radiologist reading images; it is the binarized output of the report lexicon. It is also exactly the target HARD was trained to reproduce. The result therefore says that HARD is better at reproducing the HARD surrogate. It does **not** overturn the official-label result, and it must not be presented as if it did.

The divergence is still useful. On the surrogate, HARD wins cleanly. On the official 58, SOFT wins all three seeds and the ensemble point estimate. The leaderboard is the next external test because it contains far more official patients, even though Kaggle will return only aggregate macro AUC and no patient-level interval.

##### 4. The mechanism check failed

The original experiment suggested a plausible story: SOFT should help most for findings where binarization destroys the most graded target signal. We fixed that hypothesis before inspecting the strong-pipeline result.

For each finding we measured, on the training set, the weighted target signal destroyed by binarization:

`sum(W * abs(Y_soft - I[Y_soft > 0.5])) / sum(W)`

We then correlated it with the per-finding SOFT-HARD improvement on the official 58. Spearman rho was **-0.161** across 12 findings.

That sign is null or opposite to the proposed mechanism. The causal explanation must therefore be withdrawn. The per-finding pattern can be described, but this experiment does not explain it.

| finding | positives | HARD | SOFT | difference |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| ACL | 24 | 0.784 | 0.866 | +0.081 |
| MCL | 9 | 0.755 | 0.760 | +0.005 |
| Medial Meniscus | 26 | 0.692 | 0.695 | +0.003 |
| Lateral Meniscus | 23 | 0.739 | 0.748 | +0.009 |
| Medial OA | 15 | 0.957 | 0.953 | -0.003 |
| Lateral OA | 11 | 0.769 | 0.725 | -0.044 |
| PF OA | 21 | 0.798 | 0.815 | +0.017 |
| Effusion | 35 | 0.841 | 0.880 | +0.039 |
| Synovitis | 27 | 0.683 | 0.713 | +0.030 |
| Baker's cyst | 12 | 0.916 | 0.909 | -0.006 |
| Contusion | 19 | 0.787 | 0.782 | -0.005 |
| Fracture | 18 | 0.794 | 0.838 | +0.044 |

SOFT is higher on eight findings and lower on four. These findings share one model and the same patients, so the count is descriptive, not a test. With only 9-35 positives per finding, individual intervals are necessarily wide.

##### 5. What changed relative to the original experiment

The first experiment used a modest ResNet-18 2.5D pipeline and three target arms:

| arm | macro AUC | seeds |
|---|---:|---|
| HARD | 0.6497 | 0.6494 / 0.6492 / 0.6504 |
| GRADED | 0.6698 | 0.6732 / 0.6623 / 0.6740 |
| SOFT | 0.6999 | 0.6983 / 0.7048 / 0.6966 |

Its rank-mean SOFT-HARD difference was **+0.0559**, with a paired bootstrap interval of **[+0.0256, +0.0870]**. Splitting the change through the GRADED middle arm attributed +0.0236 to grading hedges and +0.0323 to distinguishing silence from explicit negation.

Those numbers remain valid for that pipeline, but the strong replication changes their interpretation:

1. The effect size did not generalize quantitatively: +0.0559 became +0.0143.
2. The 58-study interval in the strong pipeline no longer excludes zero.
3. The pre-specified mechanism check failed, so the earlier decomposition cannot be promoted from an ablation description to a causal explanation of the strong-pipeline result.

The original experiment was useful because it generated a falsifiable hypothesis. The replication did what a replication should do: it narrowed the claim.

##### 6. Four preprocessing bugs that still matter

These findings survived the architecture change and are independent of the target comparison.

###### 6.1 Slice order

Do not sort `.dcm` files by filename. The filename is a SOP Instance UID, assigned to be unique rather than ordered. Sort by physical position `k = p . n`, where `n` is the cross product of the row and column directions from `ImageOrientationPatient`, and `p` comes from `ImagePositionPatient`.

###### 6.2 Physical scale

Resizing without a physical crop can erase 1-3 mm pathology before the first convolution. We crop to a fixed 130 mm field and resize to 336 pixels, about 0.387 mm/px. Check that the requested crop actually fits; otherwise it can silently become a no-op.

###### 6.3 Laterality

Five targets are medial/lateral pairs, while `train.csv` has no Laterality column. We derive it from geometry using the image centre in patient space, not the corner in `ImagePositionPatient`. Coronal and axial views need a horizontal normalization; sagittal stacks need their order normalized because the medial-lateral axis is the stack axis.

###### 6.4 Encoder input

Stacking 18 slices as channels and averaging the first convolution's pretrained weights destroys the input interface the encoder learned. Feed groups of three adjacent slices through the untouched backbone and aggregate feature vectors afterwards.

##### 7. Reproducibility notes

- 4,407 studies, 4,276 unique report texts.
- Duplicate report texts are grouped before splitting.
- Nine languages are present; Turkish case folding needs explicit handling of dotted and dotless `i`.
- The labels come from a deterministic multilingual lexicon, not an LLM.
- Preprocessing dominates wall time, so cached volumes are essential for paired experiments.
- The six selected checkpoints, a manifest and per-seed/rank-mean CSVs were saved by the successful run.

##### 8. What remains

The pre-specified next external comparison is **SOFT rank-mean vs HARD rank-mean on the competition leaderboard**. The primary pair must be submitted from inference notebooks built from the exact frozen checkpoints. Per-seed submissions are secondary diagnostics and should not be used to search for a favourable configuration.

Until that comparison exists, the defensible summary is:

> In a strong DINOv2 pipeline, SOFT was better on all three seeds and improved the 58-study rank-mean ensemble by +0.0143 macro AUC, but the paired 95% interval [-0.0041, +0.0330] did not exclude zero or establish practical equivalence. HARD was significantly better on an independent binarized-lexicon surrogate, and the proposed target-nuance mechanism was not supported.

I will update this post again when the paired leaderboard scores are available.

_কোনো কমেন্ট নেই।_

<a id="t734095"></a>
## 123. Labeling the 4,349 report-only studies without an LLM, and what it scores on the 58 gold

- **লিংক:** https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/discussion/734095
- **লেখক:** **Akmal Xodarev (Busya PRIME)** (@busyaprime, Expert) · _topic-এর লেখক_ · 2026-08-09 20:31 UTC · LB rank 3238
- **ভোট:** 0 · **কমেন্ট:** 0

The host confirmed that sending report text to a hosted LLM is allowed, with the caveat that the tool has to be reasonably accessible to everyone and of minimal cost. That caveat is the reason I went the other way and wrote a labeler that uses no API, no model weights and no GPU, so it runs inside the competition notebook for free and there is nothing to gate access on. Posting what it does and what it scores, since a transparent zero-cost baseline seems worth having on the record next to the LLM approaches.

![](https://www.googleapis.com/download/storage/v1/b/kaggle-forum-message-attachments/o/inbox%2F35123354%2F299d5ee1f1e6f8c4b88893a5ca761d10%2Fimage-1786307441484.png?generation=1786307460155925&alt=media)

Left: the two osteoarthritis compartments go from no signal to strong once you read the consequences instead of the word. Right: all 12 labels on the 58 gold with 95% bootstrap intervals, macro 0.727, no LLM and no GPU.

The setup, for anyone who has not counted rows yet: train.csv has 4,407 studies but only 58 carry the 12 labels. The other 4,349 have a radiology report and nothing else, so the report is a train-only teacher. test.csv is study ids only, so whatever labels you derive, the model that actually scores has to read pixels.

The labeler is a rule system, not a model. It reads each report in its own language (a crude detector finds at least eight, and reading them I hit English, Spanish, German, Dutch, French, plus Greek and Turkish), fires a per-finding vocabulary, and runs a negation and uncertainty check on every mention so that "no meniscal tear", "sin rotura", "geen scheur" and "kein" count as negative instead of positive. Then it maps the surviving mentions onto the 12 targets.

Two things mattered more than I expected, both measured on the 58 gold:

1. Osteoarthritis is almost never written as the word osteoarthritis. Under naive reading Lateral OA is 0.47 (below chance) and Medial OA is 0.59, essentially no usable signal. They only come alive once you mine the consequences the radiologist actually writes: osteophytes, joint space narrowing, chondral loss, chondrosis, gonarthrose, with the compartment word next to them, plus the rule that "tricompartmental" fires all three OA labels. That vocabulary takes Lateral OA to 0.83 and Medial OA to 0.75. Because the metric is the mean of 12 AUCs, resurrecting two dead labels was the single biggest lever I found.

2. Negation is a real cleanup but a smaller one on the macro. The ablation, macro AUC over the 12 on the 58 gold: naive keyword presence 0.638, add sentence-scope negation 0.667, add the OA consequence vocabulary 0.727. Negation mostly buys precision (fracture precision 0.53 to 0.80, Baker's 0.42 to 0.62) rather than ranking.

Per label the labeler runs from Synovitis 0.61 (the hardest, since on non-contrast MRI it cannot be cleanly separated from a simple effusion) and Effusion 0.63 (only that, despite being the most common finding and the co-occurrence hub) up to ACL and Lateral OA at 0.83. Macro is 0.727 with a study-level bootstrap interval of roughly 0.67 to 0.78. MCL has only 9 positives in the 58, so read its number as a sign, not a measurement.

Honest limits. The vocabulary was refined by reading the 58 gold reports and there is no second labeled set, so 0.727 is an in-sample number and probably optimistic. A good local or hosted LLM will likely beat this on the subtle classes. What the rule labeler gives you instead is that it is free, deterministic, auditable line by line, and never leaves the notebook, so the silver labels it emits are reproducible rather than a black box.

Full method, the ablation, the per-label intervals and the multilingual DICOM figures are in the notebook, and it writes silver_labels.csv for all 4,349 report-only studies as its output, so you can fork it and train an images-only model on it directly:
https://www.kaggle.com/code/busyaprime/osteoarthritis-is-almost-never-written-as-oa

Happy to fold in more finding vocabulary if people paste phrasings they are seeing in languages I undercovered.

_কোনো কমেন্ট নেই।_

<a id="t734004"></a>
## 124. DICOM metadata findings: scanner-grouped CV and PatientSex priors

- **লিংক:** https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/discussion/734004
- **লেখক:** **morningduck** (@morningduck, Contributor) · _topic-এর লেখক_ · 2026-08-09 13:40 UTC · ▲6 · LB rank 2261
- **ভোট:** 6 · **কমেন্ট:** 0

##### Scanner-grouped vs. random CV splits

The competition DICOMs carry `Manufacturer` (Siemens / Philips / GE) and
`MagneticFieldStrength` (1.5T / 3T). There are ~13 distinct scanner groups
across the 4,407 training studies:

| Scanner Group   | Studies |
|-----------------|---------|
| Siemens_1.5T    | 1,148   |
| Siemens_3T      | 781     |
| GE_1.5T         | 698     |
| Philips_3T      | 663     |
| Philips_1.5T    | 619     |

A standard random K-fold split places the same scanner in both train and
val folds. To measure the effect, I trained a metadata-only classifier
(no pixels) and evaluated it under both split types:

| Split type      | Macro-AUC |
|-----------------|-----------|
| Random 5-fold   | 0.652     |
| Scanner-grouped | 0.598     |

The gap is ~0.05 from metadata alone. OA targets showed the largest drop
(0.07–0.09), consistent with field-strength-dependent cartilage contrast
differences.

##### PatientSex in test DICOM headers

`PatientSex` (tag 0010,0040) is present in the test set DICOM headers.

Training distribution: M=2,076 / F=1,894.

| Target      | M prevalence | F prevalence |
|-------------|-------------|-------------|
| ACL         | ~54%        | ~32%        |
| Medial OA   | ~12%        | ~45%        |

Males show higher rates of traumatic injuries (ACL, MCL, Fracture); females
show higher rates of degenerative changes (OA).

##### Other metadata available in test headers

`Manufacturer`, `MagneticFieldStrength`, `SeriesDescription`, and
`ImageOrientationPatient` are all readable via `pydicom` at inference time.

_কোনো কমেন্ট নেই।_

## 125. Rules clarification: may Competition Data be sent to third-party LLM APIs?

- **লিংক:** https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/discussion/733873
- **লেখক:** **FHZ982** (@fhz982, Contributor) · _topic-এর লেখক_ · 2026-08-08 19:04 UTC · ▲3
- **ভোট:** 3 · **কমেন্ট:** 1 · **শেষ কমেন্ট:** 2026-08-09 09:59 UTC (Po-Hao "Howard" Chen)

Only 58 of the 4,407 training studies carry per-condition labels, so a lot of us are deriving labels from the Report text, and most of those pipelines involve a large language model. I would like to get a clear answer on where the line is, because two clauses seem to point in different directions and I would rather ask than guess.

Section 2.6 (External Data and Tools) treats external models as acceptable unless the Host prohibits them, and gives a commercial large language model as an example of a tool that meets the Reasonableness Standard. That reads as though using a hosted LLM is expected.

Section 2.4.b (Data Security) says participants agree not to "transmit, duplicate, publish, redistribute or otherwise provide or make available the Competition Data to any party not participating in the Competition." The RSNA MIRA Dataset Research Use Agreement, incorporated by reference in 2.4.a, is stricter still: §4 prohibits distributing or reproducing any portion of a dataset without prior written permission, and §5 prohibits sharing access.

Sending a report to a hosted model is, on a literal reading of 2.4.b, providing Competition Data to a party that is not participating. On the reading suggested by 2.6, it is ordinary tool use.

Four specific questions:

Does passing Report text to a third-party hosted LLM API count as transmitting Competition Data to a non-participating party under 2.4.b?
If the answer depends on the provider's terms, does an enterprise or API configuration with zero data retention and no training on submitted data change it?
Are locally-run open-weights models (nothing leaves the participant's machine) unambiguously acceptable? That is the conservative option, and it would be useful to have it confirmed.
Does the answer differ for the DICOM images versus the report text? The reports are free-text clinical documents, which feels like a different category from pixel data.

If the answer is that hosted APIs are not permitted, an early and visible statement would help a lot of people — several public notebooks already describe LLM-based label derivation, and I doubt anyone has read this as a restriction.

Thanks.

This is my current understanding. There's no proper ground truth "label" here. A radiologist reads an MRI and writes a report, but scans are hard to read and two radiologists can disagree. That happens in every medical imaging competition, you're just not given the report, so you take the label as truth when it isn't.

What's different is the host told us how the labels were made: two readers against a published rubric, a third adjudicating, same for the test set. So there is a procedure and it disagrees with the reports in a known direction: the readers use stricter thresholds than a radiologist writing for clinical care, so the reports over-call.

The reports are arguably a richer dataset than usual, since a report says more than a binary label. The reason you'd want more than the ~50 provided labels is to calibrate your extractor against the competition's annotators - you're trying to match your label generator to theirs, which is hard with so few.

**[2]** **PC Jimmmy** (@pcjimmmy, Master) · 2026-08-08 23:39 UTC · ▲2 · LB rank 642

You got labels that are ground truths (call them Gold).  The rest of the images need you to create the labels by correctly reading the reports.

I think there have been a number of kaggle competitions over the past years with a similar situation - some GOLD and than rocks you need to pan to find the gold.  My guess - the best panners will be the winners.

And always remember - to be a winner you need to predict the Kaggle truth which is sometimes harder than the real truth :)  Medical competitions are the place I first started using the words "Kaggle Truth".  Reading these images is one heck of a hard job, I would never want to do it.   When humans make visual inspections there is always lots of variation.  We need to use the images without labels but must remember that the host tells us the labelers did NOT use the reports.

<a id="t733517"></a>
## 128. 0.932 LB within one day. Tested for DICOM metadata shortcut

- **লিংক:** https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/discussion/733517
- **লেখক:** **Oleksii Zhukov** (@zhukovoleksiy, Master) · _topic-এর লেখক_ · 2026-08-07 07:12 UTC · ▲18 · LB rank 2849
- **ভোট:** 18 · **কমেন্ট:** 2 · **শেষ কমেন্ট:** 2026-08-08 14:05 UTC (agr hmmm)

**TL;DR: DICOM metadata alone reaches 0.6515 macro AUC under random folds but only 0.598 across unseen scanners. Safe to say no meaningful shortcuts found. Leaderboard scores seem to reflect image reading.**

Public LB scores passed 0.8 and 0.9 within about a day of launch. That seemed strangely high for this label set in such short period, so I tested whether DICOM metadata alone could account for this.

It can't. Full header metadata, no pixels, reaches 0.6515 macro AUC under random folds and 0.5981 under scanner-grouped folds. The 0.053 increment is site memorization and it does not transfer to unseen scanners.

**Method**

Two probes, with the decision threshold written down before running anything:

Probe A: site identifiability. Cluster studies on Manufacturer + ManufacturerModelName + SoftwareVersions + ImagingFrequency + ReceiveCoilName. No labels needed. Result: 265 distinct fingerprints, top 20 covering 45.5% of studies.

Probe B: metadata => targets. HistGradientBoosting on study-level metadata features, targets = report-derived labels, scored under random 5-fold and under GroupKFold on the fingerprint above. The gap is the number of interest.

Features: series composition (plane × Fluid_Sensitive × Fat_Suppression counts, protocol signature string), plus per-study median/min/max of TR, TE, TI, flip angle, echo train length, pixel bandwidth, averages, phase encoding steps, slice thickness, spacing, pixel spacing, matrix size, field strength, ImagingFrequency, and categorical scanner/coil/software identity.

**Results:**

| Label            | Random | Site-grouped |  Drop |
| ---------------- | --------: | -----------: | ----: |
| ACL              |  0.705    |        0.670 | 0.035 |
| MCL              |  0.683    |        0.648 | 0.035 |
| Medial Meniscus  |  0.590    |        0.548 | 0.042 |
| Lateral Meniscus |  0.595    |        0.565 | 0.030 |
| Medial OA        |  0.652    |        0.578 | 0.074 |
| Lateral OA       |  0.637    |        0.563 | 0.074 |
| PF OA            |  0.680    |        0.599 | 0.081 |
| Effusion         |  0.628    |        0.582 | 0.046 |
| Synovitis        |  0.644    |        0.602 | 0.042 |
| Baker's          |  0.765    |        0.717 | 0.048 |
| Contusion        |  0.633    |        0.587 | 0.046 |
| Fracture         |  0.605    |        0.519 | 0.086 |
| **Macro**        |**0.6516** |   **0.5981** | **0.0534** |


Series composition alone (no DICOM reads at all) gives 0.5954. So the entire DICOM header pass adds 0.056 over the four columns already in `train_series.csv`

**Note:**
- Targets are report-derived, not expert annotations. Only 58 studies carry per-condition labels, which is too few to fit or validate against. So part of the 0.053 may be metadata predicting reporting style rather than disease.
- 265 fingerprints is finer-grained than institution. I'm separating individual scanners and software revisions within sites, so the grouped folds are stricter than true site holdout. That makes 0.053 an upper bound for a second, independent reason.


My notebook: https://www.kaggle.com/code/zhukovoleksiy/rsna-metadata-probe

The report extractor used to generate targets is from: https://www.kaggle.com/code/romanrozen/rsna-knee-data-structure-eda-baseline

<a id="t733826"></a>
## 129. Possible inconsistencies between MRI reports and provided labels

- **লিংক:** https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/discussion/733826
- **লেখক:** **Cho Royou** (@ngyzly, Expert) · _topic-এর লেখক_ · 2026-08-08 13:08 UTC · ▲27 · LB rank 1383
- **ভোট:** 27 · **কমেন্ট:** 2 · **শেষ কমেন্ট:** 2026-08-08 14:02 UTC (Po-Hao "Howard" Chen)

Hello,

I am auditing the relationship between the 12 provided binary labels and the corresponding MRI reports. I performed a preliminary report-only review on 20 multilingual studies selected from the 58 studies with complete labels.

For each study, I manually classified ACL injury, MCL injury, medial and lateral meniscus tears, medial/lateral/patellofemoral osteoarthritis, joint effusion, synovitis, Baker's cyst, bone contusion, and acute fracture.

I used a strict textual rule:

- Positive when the report explicitly affirms the finding
- Negative when the report explicitly negates or does not mention it
- Uncertain statements were not treated as definite positives

Across 240 decisions, the report-only labels matched the provided labels in 198 cases:

- Overall agreement: **82.5%**
- Positive predictive agreement: **68/93 = 73.1%**
- Positive recall: **68/85 = 80.0%**
- TP: 68, FP: 25, FN: 17, TN: 130

Some discrepancies appear difficult to explain as ordinary interpretation errors.

###### Examples

| StudyInstanceUID | Report evidence | Provided label |
|---|---|---|
| 1.2.826.0.1.3680043.8.498.11287937729196958426538087439102017580 | Impression explicitly states “Synovitis of left knee and massive joint effusion.” | Effusion = 1, but Synovitis = 0 |
| 1.2.826.0.1.3680043.8.498.54977581323931277817074741182670080450 | “Leve derrame articular con mínima sinovitis” (“Mild joint effusion with minimal synovitis”). | Effusion = 0 and Synovitis = 0 |
| 1.2.826.0.1.3680043.8.498.26790702379506190936834447203448882465 | “There is a mild knee effusion.” | Effusion = 0 |
| 1.2.826.0.1.3680043.8.498.73926443729786165628848843707532839995 | The German report explicitly states “Baker-Zyste.” | Baker's cyst = 0 |
| 1.2.826.0.1.3680043.8.498.90283565381042081768587894596970552767 | “OA femorotibial medial y lateral. OA patelofemoral. Derrame con sinovitis.” | Medial OA = 0 and Lateral OA = 0, while PF OA, Effusion, and Synovitis = 1 |
| 1.2.826.0.1.3680043.8.498.28925345859498351203477642741908452608 | The Turkish report states “Lateral menisküs normal” (“The lateral meniscus is normal”). | Lateral meniscus tear = 1 |
| 1.2.826.0.1.3680043.8.498.12448079646359892252441208258836556945 | The report describes patellar chondropathy but contains no apparent mention of synovitis. | PF OA = 1 and Synovitis = 1 |
| 1.2.826.0.1.3680043.8.498.75187434248356774277526985329346125190 | The Bulgarian report explicitly describes an osteochondral fracture. | Acute fracture = 0 |

The last example may reflect a deliberate distinction between an osteochondral injury and the competition's definition of acute fracture, rather than an incorrect label.

Similar ambiguity may affect:

- Whether chondropathy should be considered osteoarthritis
- Whether bone marrow edema should be considered bone contusion
- Whether osteochondral, subchondral, or insufficiency fractures count as acute fractures
- Whether minimal synovitis should receive a positive synovitis label
- Whether suprapatellar or intra-articular fluid should always count as joint effusion

###### Questions for the organizers

1. Were the labels assigned independently from the MRI images, rather than extracted from the reports?
2. If image interpretation and report text disagree, should the image-derived label be considered authoritative?
3. Could you provide the exact positive-label definitions for osteoarthritis versus chondropathy, bone contusion versus nonspecific bone marrow edema, acute fracture versus osteochondral/subchondral/insufficiency fractures, and joint effusion versus synovitis?
4. Do negative labels mean that the finding was confirmed absent, or can they also mean that it was not annotated?
5. Are multiple reports or bilateral examinations sometimes associated with one StudyInstanceUID? If so, how were the study-level labels consolidated?
6. Should the examples above be considered expected report-image disagreement, or are any of them possible annotation issues?

Clarification would be particularly helpful for participants using the reports to train auxiliary models or generate weak labels.

Thank you.

### কমেন্ট (2)

**[1]** **Handudu** (@handudu, Expert) · 2026-08-08 13:40 UTC

Thank you; this is crucial for the competition.

**[2]** **Po-Hao "Howard" Chen** (@javaduke95, Contributor) · _host_ · 2026-08-08 14:02 UTC · ▲5

Hello - You have encountered a major design choice in this challenge, and what we believe makes this challenge instructive, real-world, and difficult (although we are not blind to the leaderboard 😄 - it is *incredible* how well a community can do when collaborating, better than any one individual team).

A related discussion is [here](https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/discussion/733491).  It outlines the design principles that led to perceived inconsistencies.

Below, I will focus on specific questions.

**Were the labels assigned independently from the MRI images, rather than extracted from the reports?**

Yes

**If image interpretation and report text disagree, should the image-derived label be considered authoritative?**

Yes.  Note that only a small sample of provided data contains both.  It is intended to help participants surface this conclusion.

**Could you provide the exact positive-label definitions for osteoarthritis versus chondropathy, bone contusion versus nonspecific bone marrow edema, acute fracture versus osteochondral/subchondral/insufficiency fractures, and joint effusion versus synovitis?**

Please refer to the pinned [Overview discussion post](https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/discussion/733343).  At the bottom, textual and image-based explanations of each abnormality is provided.

**Do negative labels mean that the finding was confirmed absent, or can they also mean that it was not annotated?**

It means the finding was annotated as absent using methods outlined in [Overview discussion post](https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/discussion/733343) under **Label Description** section.

**Are multiple reports or bilateral examinations sometimes associated with one StudyInstanceUID? If so, how were the study-level labels consolidated?**

Yes. In clinical practice, both knees may occasionally be scanned under one StudyInstanceUID. For the challenge, each bilateral study or bilateral report was individually reviewed, and the released report text or DICOM metadata was adjusted as needed to provide sufficient information for participants to disambiguate the relevant study/studies.

**Should the examples above be considered expected report-image disagreement, or are any of them possible annotation issues?**

Discrepancies are plausible and expected because clinical reports typically involve one signing radiologist who created it for clinical care, and the image-based labels uses multiple readers with stricter image-based thresholds.

I hope this helps, and good luck on the challenge!

## 133. train.csv: PatientSex is documented but not present in the file

- **লিংক:** https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/discussion/733423
- **লেখক:** **epicfangs** (@epicfangs, Contributor) · _topic-এর লেখক_ · 2026-08-06 20:26 UTC · ▲2
- **ভোট:** 2 · **কমেন্ট:** 3 · **শেষ কমেন্ট:** 2026-08-07 14:54 UTC (SpeedSci)

The Data page describes train.csv as containing StudyInstanceUID, PatientSex and Report alongside the twelve labels. The file itself has 14 columns: StudyInstanceUID, Report, and the 12 labels. There is no PatientSex column.

Could the hosts confirm whether PatientSex was intended to be included and was dropped during export, or whether the data description should be updated instead?

Not a blocker either way — just flagging it early so nobody writes ingest code against a column that isn't there.

Thanks for putting together an interesting dataset.

### কমেন্ট (3)

**[1]** **Po-Hao "Howard" Chen** (@javaduke95, Contributor) · _host_ · 2026-08-06 22:45 UTC · ▲1

Hello!

Yes we did intend to have PatientSex be available - at one point we indeed built it into the CSV but since patient sex is also in the DICOM header from image files directly, we felt it's redundant and removed the column from CSV.

We will update the data page to reflect this more accurately. Good call out to avoid confusion. 

Thank you so much for the kind words.

**[1.1]** ↳ **SpeedSci** (@caoyupeng, Expert) · 2026-08-07 14:54 UTC · LB rank 1073

Are you going to modify the training data?

**[2]** **Fabian Friedland - xBrain** (@fabotelli, Contributor) · 2026-08-07 00:37 UTC · LB rank 172

The literature indicate a possible strong AUC between frequency and MCL.
